WeaveFFI (dev)

Introduction

WeaveFFI generates idiomatic bindings for 11 languages from one API definition, over a stable C ABI. Write the API as safe Rust tagged with WeaveFFI’s attributes, or as a YAML or JSON IDL implemented by any library that can export C symbols (C, C++, Zig, and so on). WeaveFFI emits packages for C, C++, Swift, Kotlin, Node.js, WebAssembly, Python, .NET, Dart, Go, and Ruby that all call the same C functions.

The design in one page

One definition, two front doors. A Rust producer tags ordinary items (#[weaveffi::export] on a function or an impl block, #[weaveffi::record] on a struct, and so on). Each attribute emits the item’s extern "C" functions and embeds a description of the item in the compiled library, and the CLI reads that description back out to emit the bindings, so the bindings describe exactly the library you compiled, #[cfg] and Cargo features included. A non-Rust producer writes an IDL instead and implements the C header WeaveFFI generates from it.

One library, one namespace. Every record, enum, error domain, interface, callback interface, and function has a name unique across the library, and every C symbol is the library’s prefix plus that name (kvstore_Store_open). An IDL may still be split across files with imports:, and a Rust crate across modules; neither changes a name.

Identity drives every name. A library has one resolved identity: a package name, a C symbol prefix, and a native library base name. For a Rust producer all three come from the crate (kvstore gives kvstore_Store_open, libkvstore.so, and an npm, PyPI, or NuGet package named kvstore). Nothing a generator emits is named after WeaveFFI, so any number of WeaveFFI-built libraries can live in one process.

A small, explicit C ABI. In revision 6 every value crosses as exactly one C value, in every position: a scalar by value, an optional scalar as a {p}_opt_* struct, an object as a pointer, and everything else (strings, bytes, typed arrays of numbers, and the serialized value buffers that carry records, rich enums, and the remaining optionals, lists, and maps) as one {p}_run, a pointer and a byte length. A callback interface is one {ctx, vtable} struct. Every fallible call reports through a {p}_error struct carrying a code, a length-delimited message, and the code’s fields. Async functions complete through a callback that fires exactly once, and cancellation is a first-class runtime code.

Bindings that refuse to drift. Every declaration exports a contract function returning a hash of its signature. Each consumer checks the ABI revision and every declaration it was generated with when it loads the library, so a stale binding fails loudly, naming the declaration that changed, instead of misreading memory, while a library that only added declarations, error codes, or callback methods keeps loading.

Idiomatic generators. Each target maps the model onto its own idioms: objects become classes with deterministic disposal and a garbage-collector backstop, async functions become async/await, coroutines, promises, or tasks with native cancellation, iterators stay lazy, and error domains become typed exceptions or error values. The capability matrix summarizes all eleven.

Standalone output. Generated packages are self-contained: helper code (the value-buffer codec, error types, object wrappers, loaders) is generated into each package, and no target needs compiled glue of its own, so consumers never install WeaveFFI. weaveffi package bundles prebuilt native libraries into publishable packages per ecosystem.

Where to next

  • Getting Started: a Rust producer called from C and Python in a few minutes, plus the IDL path for a C implementation.
  • The Rust Producer Macros: every attribute, with a compiled example of each.
  • C ABI Contract: the normative description of the boundary.
  • Comparison and FAQ: how WeaveFFI relates to UniFFI, Diplomat, cbindgen, and the single-language bridges.
  • Stability and Versioning: what the version numbers promise.

Getting Started

This guide builds a small Rust library, generates bindings for it, and calls it from C and from Python. A second, shorter path at the end starts from an IDL instead and implements the generated C header in C. The code on this page is the examples/getting-started crate and the examples/idl/greeter project, which the workspace’s tests build and call.

Install the CLI

You need a stable Rust toolchain. Install the weaveffi CLI from crates.io (or grab a prebuilt binary with cargo binstall weaveffi-cli):

cargo install weaveffi-cli
weaveffi --version

To set up tab completion in your shell, see Shell Completions.

Write the producer

Create a library crate and add the weaveffi crate. It needs no crate-type: WeaveFFI builds the C dynamic library itself (with cargo rustc --crate-type cdylib).

cargo new --lib mathlib
cd mathlib
cargo add weaveffi

Replace src/lib.rs with this. Everything here is safe Rust; the attributes write the extern "C" layer.

/// Errors the math functions report.
#[weaveffi::error]
#[derive(Debug)]
pub enum MathError {
    /// division by zero
    DivisionByZero = 1,
}

/// Add two integers, wrapping on overflow.
#[weaveffi::export]
pub fn add(a: i32, b: i32) -> i32 {
    a.wrapping_add(b)
}

/// Divide `a` by `b`, failing when `b` is zero.
#[weaveffi::export]
pub fn div(a: i32, b: i32) -> Result<i32, MathError> {
    a.checked_div(b).ok_or(MathError::DivisionByZero)
}

/// Greet someone by name.
#[weaveffi::export]
pub fn greet(name: &str) -> String {
    format!("Hello, {name}!")
}

// The runtime symbols (errors, memory, cancel tokens), once per library.
weaveffi::export_runtime!();

A few things to notice:

  • Every C symbol starts with the crate’s name, so this crate exports mathlib_add, mathlib_div, and mathlib_greet, plus the runtime (mathlib_error_clear, mathlib_run_free, and so on). A library is one namespace, so a symbol is the prefix and the item’s name, wherever in the crate the item is declared.
  • Result<i32, MathError> makes div throw the MathError domain. The enum’s discriminants are the stable error codes, and the macro generates its Display from each variant’s doc comment (or a #[weaveffi(message = "...")] template), which is the message consumers see. A Result whose error type is an untyped error (String, std::io::Error, weaveffi::Error) works too: it throws a message without a code to match on.
  • weaveffi::export_runtime!() appears exactly once, at the crate root.

The producer macro guide covers records, enums, objects, callbacks, iterators, async functions, and custom types.

Configure and generate

Run weaveffi init in the crate. It writes a weaveffi.toml whose [project] table points at the crate itself, then lists anything the crate still needs (the weaveffi dependency, an exported item, the export_runtime! call):

weaveffi init

The file it writes generates the C header and the Python package; edit targets to pick the languages you ship (without it, every target is generated):

[project]
input = "."
out = "bindings"
targets = ["c", "python"]

Generate the bindings. With a [project] table, weaveffi generate needs no arguments and works from any directory in the project:

weaveffi generate

weaveffi generate builds the library with Cargo’s dev profile (pass --profile <name> for another, and -F/--features for Cargo features), then reads the API from the library it built: the attributes embed a description of every exported declaration in the library, so the bindings match the compiled code exactly, #[cfg] included. weaveffi extract prints that description as an IDL.

The output has one directory per target: the C target writes bindings/c/mathlib.h, and the Python target writes an installable package named mathlib. Run weaveffi generate again after any change: it rewrites only the files whose contents changed and removes files a previous run wrote that it no longer produces, using the generation records it keeps in bindings/.weaveffi/ (commit them with the bindings).

Call it from C

The header is the contract every other binding is built on. Each value is one C value: a string is a mathlib_run (a pointer and a byte length, never NUL-terminated), every call overwrites a mathlib_error the caller provides, and a returned run is released with mathlib_run_free:

/* A C consumer of the getting-started library, built against the generated
   header (bindings/c/mathlib.h). */
#include <stdio.h>
#include <string.h>
#include "mathlib.h"

int main(void) {
    /* Refuse a library built from different source than the header. */
    if (mathlib_abi_version() != MATHLIB_ABI_VERSION || mathlib_contract_check() != NULL) {
        fprintf(stderr, "mathlib.h doesn't match the loaded library\n");
        return 1;
    }

    mathlib_error err; /* every call overwrites it */
    printf("2 + 3 = %d\n", mathlib_add(2, 3, &err));

    mathlib_div(1, 0, &err);
    if (err.code == mathlib_MathError_DivisionByZero) {
        printf("error %d: %.*s\n", err.code, (int)err.message_len, (const char*)err.message_ptr);
        mathlib_error_clear(&err);
    }

    /* A string crosses as a run: its UTF-8 bytes and their length, no NUL. */
    const char* name = "C";
    mathlib_run arg = {(const uint8_t*)name, strlen(name)};
    mathlib_run greeting = mathlib_greet(arg, &err);
    printf("%.*s\n", (int)greeting.len, (const char*)greeting.ptr);
    mathlib_run_free(greeting);
    return 0;
}

Build it against the library weaveffi generate built:

cc -I bindings/c main.c -L target/debug -lmathlib -o main
DYLD_LIBRARY_PATH=target/debug ./main    # LD_LIBRARY_PATH on Linux

2 + 3 = 5
error 1: division by zero
Hello, C!

The first check is the one every generated binding makes for you when it loads the library: mathlib_abi_version() must equal the header’s MATHLIB_ABI_VERSION, and mathlib_contract_check() returns the name of the first declaration the library has changed since the header was generated, or NULL. Errors and Memory states every ownership rule the generated bindings follow for you.

Call it from Python

While you iterate, run weaveffi generate --dev: it generates as usual, then copies the library it built into the generated packages that look for a bundled copy (the Python package does) and prints how the other targets find it. Then install the package:

weaveffi generate --dev
pip install ./bindings/python

import mathlib

print(mathlib.add(2, 3))          # 5
print(mathlib.greet("Python"))    # Hello, Python!

try:
    mathlib.div(1, 0)
except mathlib.MathError as e:
    print(e)                      # (1) division by zero

On import the package checks the library’s ABI revision and every declaration’s contract, so a library built from incompatible source fails to load with an error naming the declaration that’s missing or changed. To load a library from somewhere else, set MATHLIB_LIBRARY to its full path; every target that loads the library at run time honors that {PREFIX}_LIBRARY variable. The Python page documents the generated surface.

Ship it

weaveffi package --target python

weaveffi package builds the library in release mode for this machine (that’s weaveffi build), then writes installable artifacts to dist/: here a wheel with the library inside, which needs neither MATHLIB_LIBRARY nor a compiler:

pip install dist/python/mathlib-*.whl

Packaging covers more platforms (iOS, Android, Windows, wasm32), every target’s artifacts, and publishing.

Implementing an IDL in C

A producer doesn’t have to be Rust. Start from an IDL, generate the C header, and implement it in any language that can export C symbols. Outside a Rust crate, weaveffi init writes a starter IDL and a weaveffi.toml:

mkdir greeter && cd greeter
weaveffi init           # writes greeter.yml and weaveffi.toml

Edit greeter.yml to declare the API (the IDL reference has the full schema):

# yaml-language-server: $schema=https://weaveffi.com/weaveffi.schema.json
version: "0.13.0"
doc: A greeter implemented in C.
functions:
  - name: add
    doc: Add two integers, wrapping on overflow.
    params:
      - { name: a, type: i32 }
      - { name: b, type: i32 }
    return: i32
  - name: greet
    doc: Greet someone by name.
    params:
      - { name: name, type: string }
    return: string

For an IDL the identity comes from [package] in weaveffi.toml, so the prefix and the library are both greeter:

[project]
input = "greeter.yml"
out = "bindings"
targets = ["c", "python"]

[package]
name = "greeter"
version = "0.1.0"

Generate the header and implement it:

weaveffi generate --target c     # writes bindings/c/greeter.h

The API’s own functions come first. GREETER_DEFINE_CONTRACT defines every contract function with the hashes the header was generated with, and a synchronous function stores a cleared error in out_err on success:

/* One contract function per declaration, as the header computed them. */
GREETER_DEFINE_CONTRACT

int32_t greeter_add(int32_t a, int32_t b, greeter_error* out_err) {
    memset(out_err, 0, sizeof *out_err); /* success: a cleared error */
    return (int32_t)((uint32_t)a + (uint32_t)b);
}

greeter_run greeter_greet(greeter_run name, greeter_error* out_err) {
    memset(out_err, 0, sizeof *out_err);
    static const char hello[] = "Hello, ";
    size_t len = sizeof hello - 1 + name.len + 1;
    uint8_t* text = greeter_alloc(len); /* the consumer frees it with greeter_run_free */
    if (text == NULL) {
        static const char oom[] = "out of memory";
        greeter_error_set(out_err, -1, (const uint8_t*)oom, sizeof oom - 1);
        greeter_run none = {NULL, 0};
        return none;
    }
    memcpy(text, hello, sizeof hello - 1);
    if (name.len != 0) memcpy(text + sizeof hello - 1, name.ptr, name.len);
    text[len - 1] = '!';
    greeter_run out = {text, len};
    return out;
}

Every producer also exports the runtime surface: the ABI revision, one allocator for every run it hands out (8-aligned, released by its own run_free), the error helpers, cancel tokens, and the leak counter:

/* The runtime surface every producer exports. */
uint32_t greeter_abi_version(void) { return GREETER_ABI_VERSION; }

/* One allocator for every run: returned strings and buffers, error messages
   and payloads, and runs a consumer allocates. calloc's alignment is at
   least 8 on every supported platform. */
uint8_t* greeter_alloc(size_t len) { return len != 0 ? calloc(len, 1) : NULL; }
void greeter_run_free(greeter_run run) { free((void*)run.ptr); }

static const uint8_t* copy(const uint8_t* ptr, size_t len) {
    uint8_t* run = greeter_alloc(len);
    if (run != NULL) memcpy(run, ptr, len);
    return run;
}

void greeter_error_clear(greeter_error* err) {
    if (err == NULL) return;
    free((void*)err->message_ptr);
    free((void*)err->payload_ptr);
    memset(err, 0, sizeof *err);
}

void greeter_error_free(greeter_error* err) {
    greeter_error_clear(err);
    free(err);
}

void greeter_error_set(greeter_error* err, int32_t code, const uint8_t* message_ptr,
                       size_t message_len) {
    if (err == NULL) return;
    greeter_error_clear(err);
    err->code = code;
    if (message_ptr != NULL && message_len != 0) {
        err->message_ptr = copy(message_ptr, message_len);
        err->message_len = err->message_ptr != NULL ? message_len : 0;
    }
}

void greeter_error_set_payload(greeter_error* err, const uint8_t* ptr, size_t len) {
    if (err == NULL) return;
    free((void*)err->payload_ptr);
    err->payload_ptr = ptr != NULL && len != 0 ? copy(ptr, len) : NULL;
    err->payload_len = err->payload_ptr != NULL ? len : 0;
}

/* Cancel tokens (this API has no async functions, but every library exports
   the same runtime surface). */
struct greeter_cancel_token {
    atomic_int refs;
    atomic_bool cancelled;
};

greeter_cancel_token* greeter_cancel_token_create(void) {
    greeter_cancel_token* token = malloc(sizeof *token);
    if (token != NULL) {
        atomic_init(&token->refs, 1);
        atomic_init(&token->cancelled, false);
    }
    return token;
}

void greeter_cancel_token_cancel(greeter_cancel_token* token) {
    if (token != NULL) atomic_store(&token->cancelled, true);
}

bool greeter_cancel_token_is_cancelled(const greeter_cancel_token* token) {
    return token != NULL && atomic_load(&((greeter_cancel_token*)token)->cancelled);
}

void greeter_cancel_token_destroy(greeter_cancel_token* token) {
    if (token != NULL && atomic_fetch_sub(&token->refs, 1) == 1) free(token);
}

/* Leak counters: kind -1 is 0 because this producer doesn't count. */
uint64_t greeter_debug_live(int32_t kind) {
    (void)kind;
    return 0;
}

The C ABI contract lists what a hand-written producer owes its consumers, including honoring a vtable’s GREETER_VTABLE_THREAD_AFFINE flag once the API has callbacks. Build it as libgreeter.so (libgreeter.dylib on macOS, greeter.dll on Windows). The file defines GREETER_BUILD before it includes the header, so the header’s GREETER_API exports the symbols on Windows too:

cc -shared -fPIC -I bindings/c greeter.c -o libgreeter.so

Then run weaveffi generate for the other targets; they load it exactly as they load a Rust producer. From Python, for example:

weaveffi generate --target python
pip install ./bindings/python
GREETER_LIBRARY=$PWD/libgreeter.so python3 -c "import greeter; print(greeter.add(2, 3))"   # 5

  • Project Configuration: [package] metadata, [build] settings, per-target options, and how regeneration cleans up stale files.
  • Packaging: building every platform and publishing the artifacts.
  • Samples and Examples: the tested example crates, and three complete producers from the minimal calculator to the feature-complete kvstore.
  • Generators: what each language gets.
  • Gate CI on weaveffi generate --check so committed bindings can’t drift; see Stability and Versioning.

The Rust Producer Macros

A Rust producer is an ordinary library crate. You tag the items you want to export with WeaveFFI’s attributes, wherever they are in the crate, and call weaveffi::export_runtime!() once at the crate root. Each attribute emits its item’s extern "C" functions, marshalling every value through the audited runtime inside the weaveffi crate, so the crate contains no unsafe glue. Each also embeds a description of its item in the compiled library, and the CLI reads the API from there (weaveffi generate builds the crate first), so the generated bindings describe exactly what the library was compiled with.

Every example on this page is a region of examples/macro-tour, a small address book whose tests call the generated C functions the way a consumer does.

Setup

[dependencies]
weaveffi = "0.25"

The crate needs no crate-type: the CLI builds the C dynamic library itself with cargo rustc --lib --crate-type cdylib, and weaveffi build adds the staticlib an Apple build needs. Add crate-type = ["cdylib", "rlib"] only if you also want a plain cargo build to produce the dynamic library.

The prefix is the crate name. Every C symbol starts with the crate’s library name ([lib] name, else the package name with - mapped to _), which the attributes read from CARGO_CRATE_NAME as they expand. The CLI derives the same prefix from cargo metadata, and it isn’t configurable: setting [package] c_prefix or library in weaveffi.toml for a Rust producer is an error. The macro-tour crate exports macro_tour_add, macro_tour_Book_new, macro_tour_error_clear, and so on, and builds libmacro_tour.so.

export_runtime!() exactly once, at the crate root:

weaveffi::export_runtime!();

It takes no arguments and exports the runtime surface under the crate’s prefix (abi_version, the error helpers, alloc, run_free, the cancel token functions, and debug_live). It also defines the crate’s hidden tag type, crate::__weaveffi::Tag, which every attribute’s expansion names, so a crate that forgets the call fails to compile at its first exported item with an unresolved crate::__weaveffi.

A library is one namespace. Rust modules organize the source; they don’t scope exported names. A declaration in any module or file joins the library’s one namespace, so every exported record, enum, error domain, interface, callback interface, and free function needs a name unique across the crate. Two items with the same name export the same contract and metadata symbols, so the build fails with a symbol ... is already defined error on the second one.

Each declaration carries its contract and its description. An attribute exports one contract function per declaration ({prefix}_contract_{id}, returning the hash of its canonical signature; see the contract) and one metadata frame (see Library Mode). Both are computed from the same resolved types the CLI uses for the bindings, so a library and the bindings generated from it always agree.

The attributes

AttributeOnEffect
#[weaveffi::export]a fn or async fn; an inherent impl block of an interfaceExports a free function, or every pub fn of the block as a constructor, method, or static.
#[weaveffi::record]a struct with named fieldsA record, crossing by value in a value buffer.
#[weaveffi::enumeration]an enumA C-style enum (unit variants, crossing as an int32_t) or a rich enum (variants with named fields, crossing in a value buffer).
#[weaveffi::error]an enum with explicit positive discriminantsAn error domain. Generates Display and std::error::Error unless written #[weaveffi::error(no_display)].
#[weaveffi::interface]a structA reference-counted object type, which must be Send + Sync. Export its methods with #[weaveffi::export] on its impl blocks.
#[weaveffi::callback_interface]a Send + Sync traitA method set the consumer implements, accepted as Arc<dyn Trait> or Option<Arc<dyn Trait>>.
#[weaveffi::custom(repr = R, lift = f, lower = g)]a type alias or a structA type that crosses the ABI as R.
weaveffi::export_runtime!()the crate root, onceThe runtime symbols and the crate’s tag type.

Two helper attributes refine an item: #[weaveffi(skip)] leaves a pub fn of an exported impl block out of the bindings, and #[weaveffi(message = "...")] sets an error code’s message template. Doc comments flow into the IDL and every binding, and #[deprecated(note = "...")] becomes the IDL’s deprecated: text and each language’s deprecation marker.

Only tagged items are exported; private helpers, use items, and state are left alone. Each attribute expands on its own, and every value is marshalled through its type’s traits, so a type is resolved by rustc wherever it’s declared: in another module, behind a type alias, or (for a custom type) in another crate.

Types

RustIDLCrosses the ABI as
i8..i64, u8..u64, f32, f64, boolsamethe scalar; a bool is a C bool, checked to be 0 or 1
usize, isizeu64, i64the scalar, range-checked on the way in (out of range is -3)
#[weaveffi::enumeration] C-style enumthe enumint32_t, checked to be a declared value
Option<T> of any of thoseT?a {p}_opt_{prim} struct (an optional enum is a {p}_opt_i32)
String, &strstringa {p}_run of UTF-8
charstringa run holding exactly one Unicode scalar value (anything else is -3)
Vec<u8>, &[u8]bytesa run of raw bytes
Vec<T> or &[T] of i8, i16, i32, i64, u16, u32, u64, f32, f64[T]a typed array: a run of packed elements
#[weaveffi::record] struct, rich enumthe typea run holding a value buffer
every other Option<T>, Vec<T>, &[T], and HashMap<K, V> or BTreeMap<K, V>T?, [T], {K:V}a run holding a value buffer
&T, Arc<T>, Option<&T>, Option<Arc<T>> of an interface T; Self or T as a returnT, T?an object pointer
Arc<dyn Trait>, Option<Arc<dyn Trait>> of a callback interfaceTrait, Trait?a {p}_{Trait} {ctx, vtable} struct (parameters only)
weaveffi::Iter<T>iter<T>an iterator pointer (returns only)
a #[weaveffi::custom] typeits repras its repr
weaveffi::CancelTokennone: it makes the function cancellablethe launcher’s cancel-token slot

How a type crosses never changes its IDL spelling or its surface type in a binding (i64? is an optional integer and [f64] a list of doubles in every language); only the transport differs, and inside a value buffer every type uses the buffer encoding. A reference (&str, &[u8], &[f64], &Contact, &Book) is a calling convention, not an IDL distinction: the generated function lends the lifted argument for the call, and a &[T] of a typed-array element reads the caller’s array in place. Objects compose with every buffered shape, so Vec<Arc<T>>, Option<Arc<T>>, an Arc<T> record field, and weaveffi::Iter<Arc<T>> all work.

A parameter that fails these checks fails the call with -3 and a message of the form {param}: {message}, where a rejected value is quoted (value: "ab" is not a valid char, mode: 7 is not a valid Mode).

A type without a WeaveFFI crossing (u128, a raw pointer, a Box<T>, a struct without an attribute) fails to compile, with a trait error naming the type or, for the shapes the attributes recognize, a message saying what to use instead. For integers wider than 64 bits, declare a custom type with a String repr.

Functions

/// Add two integers, wrapping on overflow.
#[weaveffi::export]
pub fn add(a: i32, b: i32) -> i32 {
    a.wrapping_add(b)
}

/// The number of words in `text`.
#[weaveffi::export]
pub fn word_count(text: &str) -> usize {
    text.split_whitespace().count()
}

/// The arithmetic mean of `values`, or none for an empty list.
#[weaveffi::export]
pub fn mean(values: &[f64]) -> Option<f64> {
    if values.is_empty() {
        return None;
    }
    Some(values.iter().sum::<f64>() / values.len() as f64)
}

A function’s parameters are plain names whose types are values (T), borrows (&str, &[f64], &Contact, &Book, Option<&T>), or callback interfaces. It returns nothing, a value, a weaveffi::Iter<T>, or a Result of one. These export macro_tour_add, macro_tour_word_count, and macro_tour_mean:

int32_t macro_tour_add(int32_t a, int32_t b, macro_tour_error* out_err);
uint64_t macro_tour_word_count(macro_tour_run text, macro_tour_error* out_err);
macro_tour_opt_f64 macro_tour_mean(macro_tour_run values, macro_tour_error* out_err);

An exported function can’t be generic, and a parameter named self, ctx, out_err, callback, context, or cancel_token collides with a slot the C ABI adds (SlotCollision).

Errors

A Result<T, E> return makes the callable fallible with return type T (Result<(), E> returns nothing), and E decides what it throws.

Error domains

/// The address book's error domain. Each variant's value is its stable
/// code, and its fields travel with the error as its payload.
#[weaveffi::error]
#[derive(Debug, Clone, PartialEq, Eq)]
#[repr(i32)]
pub enum BookError {
    /// contact not found
    #[weaveffi(message = "no contact with id {id}")]
    NotFound {
        /// The id that was looked up.
        id: u64,
    } = 1,
    /// the book is full
    #[weaveffi(message = "the book is full ({capacity} contacts)")]
    Full {
        /// The book's capacity.
        capacity: u32,
    } = 2,
    /// a contact needs a name
    EmptyName = 3,
    /// a listener rejected the contact
    #[weaveffi(message = "a listener rejected {name}")]
    Rejected {
        /// The rejected contact's name.
        name: String,
    } = 4,
    /// a listener failed
    #[weaveffi(message = "{message}")]
    ListenerFailed {
        /// The listener's message.
        message: String,
    } = 5,
}

/// Any listener failure that isn't a `BookError` code arrives as a
/// `ForeignError`; this keeps it typed for the original caller.
impl From<ForeignError> for BookError {
    fn from(e: ForeignError) -> Self {
        Self::ListenerFailed { message: e.message }
    }
}

When E is a #[weaveffi::error] enum, the callable is throws: E in the IDL, and every binding raises a typed error for each code, with the code’s fields as properties. Book::add below returns Result<Contact, BookError>.

  • Codes. Each variant’s discriminant is its stable code: explicit, positive, and unique within the domain. A variant may carry named fields, which travel as the error’s payload (a value buffer of the fields); Rust then requires the enum to be #[repr(i32)]. A field can’t reach an interface, directly or through a record or rich enum; the CLI’s validator reports it as ObjectInErrorPayload.
  • Messages. The macro generates Display and std::error::Error, so the enum must implement Debug. A variant’s message is its #[weaveffi(message = "...")] template, which may interpolate the variant’s fields by name ({id}, or {id:?} for Debug), else the first line of its doc comment, else the variant name. BookError::NotFound { id: 99 }.to_string() is no contact with id 99, the message every binding’s error carries. To write Display yourself (with thiserror, say), write #[weaveffi::error(no_display)]; the enum must then implement Display, and the macro generates neither impl.
  • Names. Code values only need to be unique within their domain (two domains may both use 1), but code names must be unique across the whole library (DuplicateErrorCodeName), because several targets flatten them into one namespace.
  • Open domains. Adding a code later never breaks a deployed binding, which reports an unknown code as the domain’s base error with its code and message.

Untyped errors

/// Parse a decimal integer. Any `std::error::Error` converts into
/// `weaveffi::Error` with `?`, so this function throws an untyped error
/// carrying the parse error's message.
#[weaveffi::export]
pub fn parse_number(text: &str) -> Result<i64, weaveffi::Error> {
    Ok(text.trim().parse()?)
}

/// The square root of `x`. A `String` error is untyped too.
#[weaveffi::export]
pub fn checked_sqrt(x: f64) -> Result<f64, String> {
    if x < 0.0 {
        return Err(format!("cannot take the square root of {x}"));
    }
    Ok(x.sqrt())
}

When E is an untyped error, the callable is throws: any: a failure reports the runtime code -1 with E’s Display text as the message, and bindings raise the library’s base error. The untyped errors are weaveffi::Error, String, &'static str, std::io::Error, Box<dyn std::error::Error> (with or without + Send + Sync), weaveffi::ForeignError, and, with the weaveffi crate’s anyhow feature, anyhow::Error. Every std::error::Error converts into weaveffi::Error with ?, which is the easy way to return any other error type.

A function that returns a plain T can’t fail. A panic in producer code is caught at the boundary and reported as code -2, which bindings treat as a bug (see Errors and Memory).

Records and enums

/// How a contact is filed: a C-style enum, which crosses as an `int32_t`.
#[weaveffi::enumeration]
#[derive(Clone, Copy, Debug, PartialEq, Eq, PartialOrd, Ord)]
pub enum Kind {
    /// Friends and family.
    Personal,
    /// Colleagues.
    Work = 10,
    /// Everyone else (`11`, one more than the previous variant).
    Other,
}

/// One contact: a record, which crosses by value.
#[weaveffi::record]
#[derive(Clone, Debug, PartialEq)]
pub struct Contact {
    /// The book-assigned id.
    pub id: u64,
    /// The display name.
    pub name: String,
    /// An email address, if known.
    pub email: Option<String>,
    /// How the contact is filed.
    pub kind: Kind,
    /// Free-form labels.
    pub tags: Vec<String>,
}

/// A change to a book: a rich enum, whose variants carry fields.
#[weaveffi::enumeration]
#[derive(Clone, Debug, PartialEq)]
pub enum Change {
    /// A contact was added.
    Added {
        /// The new contact.
        contact: Contact,
    },
    /// A contact was removed.
    Removed {
        /// The removed contact's id.
        id: u64,
    },
    /// Every contact was removed.
    Cleared,
}

A record or rich enum crosses as a value buffer; a C-style enum crosses as its int32_t discriminant, which the producer checks against the declared values (any other is a marshalling failure, -3). None of them exports a C function. A variant’s discriminant is its = value, an integer literal that fits in an i32, or one more than the previous variant’s (Kind::Other is 11); a rich enum’s discriminants are the tags in its buffer encoding. A rich enum’s variants use named fields or none (tuple variants are rejected). A field may be any value type, including usize, char, a custom type, and an object. A record or rich enum may contain itself (or a type that contains it) through a Vec or map, as in children: Vec<Node>. Records, enums, error domains, interfaces, and callback interfaces can’t be generic.

Custom types

A custom type crosses the ABI as another type, its repr, and converts at the boundary with two functions you name. Declare it on a type alias (which may name a foreign type, such as std::time::Duration or uuid::Uuid) or on a struct:

/// A timeout: a `std::time::Duration`, crossing as whole milliseconds. The
/// bindings see a `u64`.
#[weaveffi::custom(repr = u64, lift = timeout_from_millis, lower = timeout_to_millis)]
pub type Timeout = Duration;

fn timeout_from_millis(ms: u64) -> Result<Duration, std::convert::Infallible> {
    Ok(Duration::from_millis(ms))
}

fn timeout_to_millis(timeout: &Duration) -> u64 {
    u64::try_from(timeout.as_millis()).unwrap_or(u64::MAX)
}

/// An email address, crossing as its string. `lift` rejects a string
/// without an `@`, which fails the call as a marshalling error.
#[weaveffi::custom(repr = String, lift = Email::parse, lower = Email::to_text)]
#[derive(Clone, Debug, PartialEq, Eq)]
pub struct Email(String);

impl Email {
    fn parse(text: String) -> Result<Email, String> {
        if text.contains('@') {
            Ok(Email(text))
        } else {
            Err(format!("{text:?} is not an email address"))
        }
    }

    fn to_text(&self) -> String {
        self.0.clone()
    }
}

/// The domain of an email address (what follows the `@`).
#[weaveffi::export]
pub fn email_domain(email: Email) -> String {
    email.0.rsplit('@').next().unwrap_or_default().to_string()
}
  • repr is a type that crosses on its own (String, an integer, Vec<u8>, a record, …). The IDL, the contract, and every binding see the repr, so email_domain is email_domain(email: string) -> string and a Timeout parameter is a u64.
  • lift: fn(R) -> Result<T, E> with E: Display runs on the way in. A failure fails the call with the marshalling code -3 and E’s message, prefixed with the parameter’s name (email: "ada" is not an email address).
  • lower: fn(&T) -> R runs on the way out.

A custom type works in every position: parameters, returns, async results, iterator items, callback parameters and returns, record, rich-enum, and error-payload fields, and nested in Option, Vec, and maps. Bindings expose the repr; mapping it to a language’s own type is up to the consumer.

Interfaces

An interface is shared across the boundary (by consumer wrappers, records, collections, and in-flight async calls), so the type must be Send + Sync and keeps mutable state behind interior mutability. Export its members with #[weaveffi::export] on an inherent impl block (not a trait impl, and not a generic one):

/// An address book: an interface, a reference-counted object the bindings
/// wrap in a class.
#[weaveffi::interface]
pub struct Book {
    capacity: u32,
    next_id: AtomicU64,
    contacts: Mutex<BTreeMap<u64, Contact>>,
    listeners: Mutex<Vec<Arc<dyn Listener>>>,
}

#[weaveffi::export]
impl Book {
    /// An empty book holding up to `capacity` contacts (a constructor: it
    /// returns `Self`).
    pub fn new(capacity: u32) -> Self {
        Self {
            capacity,
            next_id: AtomicU64::new(1),
            contacts: Mutex::new(BTreeMap::new()),
            listeners: Mutex::new(Vec::new()),
        }
    }

    /// The capacity of a book made by `new_default` (a static: no receiver,
    /// and it doesn't return the interface).
    pub fn default_capacity() -> u32 {
        100
    }

    /// An empty book with the default capacity.
    pub fn new_default() -> Self {
        Self::new(Self::default_capacity())
    }

    /// Add a contact, returning it with its new id. Fails with `EmptyName`,
    /// `Full`, or `Rejected`, or with a listener's own error.
    pub fn add(
        &self,
        name: String,
        email: Option<Email>,
        kind: Kind,
    ) -> Result<Contact, BookError> {
        if name.trim().is_empty() {
            return Err(BookError::EmptyName);
        }
        let contact = Contact {
            id: self.next_id.fetch_add(1, Ordering::Relaxed),
            name,
            email: email.map(|e| e.0),
            kind,
            tags: Vec::new(),
        };
        // Never hold a lock while a consumer's callback runs: it may call
        // back into the book.
        for listener in self.listeners() {
            if !listener.approve(&contact)? {
                return Err(BookError::Rejected { name: contact.name });
            }
        }
        {
            let mut contacts = lock(&self.contacts);
            if contacts.len() >= self.capacity as usize {
                return Err(BookError::Full {
                    capacity: self.capacity,
                });
            }
            contacts.insert(contact.id, contact.clone());
        }
        self.notify(&Change::Added {
            contact: contact.clone(),
        });
        Ok(contact)
    }

    /// The contact with `id`. Fails with `NotFound`.
    pub fn get(&self, id: u64) -> Result<Contact, BookError> {
        lock(&self.contacts)
            .get(&id)
            .cloned()
            .ok_or(BookError::NotFound { id })
    }

    /// Remove the contact with `id`, returning whether it existed.
    pub fn remove(&self, id: u64) -> bool {
        let removed = lock(&self.contacts).remove(&id).is_some();
        if removed {
            self.notify(&Change::Removed { id });
        }
        removed
    }

    /// The number of contacts.
    pub fn count(&self) -> usize {
        lock(&self.contacts).len()
    }

    /// The number of contacts.
    #[deprecated(note = "use `count`")]
    pub fn size(&self) -> u32 {
        u32::try_from(self.count()).unwrap_or(u32::MAX)
    }

    /// Another reference to this same book (a method taking
    /// `self: Arc<Self>`).
    pub fn share(self: Arc<Self>) -> Arc<Book> {
        self
    }

    /// Lock the contacts for Rust callers. Its return type can't cross the
    /// boundary, so `skip` leaves it out of the bindings.
    #[weaveffi(skip)]
    pub fn contacts_mut(&self) -> MutexGuard<'_, BTreeMap<u64, Contact>> {
        lock(&self.contacts)
    }

    /// Remove every contact (exported only when the crate is built with
    /// its `admin` feature).
    #[cfg(feature = "admin")]
    pub fn purge(&self) -> u32 {
        let removed = std::mem::take(&mut *lock(&self.contacts));
        self.notify(&Change::Cleared);
        u32::try_from(removed.len()).unwrap_or(u32::MAX)
    }
}

Each pub fn of the block is classified by its signature:

  • a method takes &self or self: Arc<Self> (&mut self and self by value are rejected: objects are shared);
  • a constructor has no receiver, isn’t async, and returns the interface: Self, Arc<Self>, or the type by name, optionally in a Result;
  • a static has no receiver and returns anything else, or is an async fn.

Constructors are synchronous, so an async fn without a receiver that returns the interface (Self, Arc<Self>, or the type by name, optionally in a Result) is an async static factory. A free async fn returning the object works too (the kvstore sample’s open_store).

An object parameter &T is borrowed for the call; Arc<T> (or Option<Arc<T>>) is a new strong reference taken for you, so you may store it. A by-value T parameter doesn’t compile. An object you return transfers one strong reference to the consumer. A private fn, or a pub fn marked #[weaveffi(skip)], isn’t exported, so a public helper whose signature can’t cross the ABI (a guard, a closure, a borrowed iterator) stays usable from Rust. An interface may have several exported impl blocks; the sections below add Book’s iterator, callback, and async members that way.

The attribute also exports {prefix}_{Type}_clone and _destroy, which add and release a reference. Every binding wraps the reference in a class that releases it exactly once:

typedef struct macro_tour_Book macro_tour_Book;
macro_tour_Book* macro_tour_Book_new(uint32_t capacity, macro_tour_error* out_err);
macro_tour_run macro_tour_Book_add(const macro_tour_Book* self, macro_tour_run name,
                                   macro_tour_run email, macro_tour_Kind kind,
                                   macro_tour_error* out_err);
macro_tour_Book* macro_tour_Book_clone(const macro_tour_Book* self);
void macro_tour_Book_destroy(macro_tour_Book* self);

Iterators

Return weaveffi::Iter<T> (built with Iter::new from anything IntoIterator whose iterator is Send + 'static) when the consumer should pull elements lazily instead of receiving a materialized list:

#[weaveffi::export]
impl Book {
    /// Every contact's name in id order, pulled lazily from a snapshot.
    pub fn names(&self) -> weaveffi::Iter<String> {
        let names: Vec<String> = lock(&self.contacts)
            .values()
            .map(|c| c.name.clone())
            .collect();
        weaveffi::Iter::new(names)
    }
}

It may be wrapped in a Result, and T may be any value type, including an object, an optional scalar, or a typed array (weaveffi::Iter<Option<i64>>, weaveffi::Iter<Vec<i32>>). Iterators are returns of synchronous callables only: an Iter parameter, a nested Iter, and an async function returning one are rejected. Each binding pulls one element per step of its own iteration idiom and releases the iterator exactly once.

Callback interfaces

A callback interface is the inverse of an interface: the consumer implements it and the producer calls it, from any thread, for as long as it holds the Arc. When the last clone drops, the consumer’s release hook runs exactly once (on whichever thread drops it).

/// Approves the contacts a book adds and hears about its changes. The
/// consumer implements it; the book calls it, from any thread.
#[weaveffi::callback_interface]
pub trait Listener: Send + Sync {
    /// Whether `contact` may be added. A consumer may also fail with one of
    /// `BookError`'s codes, which `add` then fails with.
    fn approve(&self, contact: &Contact) -> Result<bool, BookError>;

    /// A change the book made.
    fn on_change(&self, change: &Change) -> Result<(), ForeignError>;
}

Accept one as Arc<dyn Trait>, or Option<Arc<dyn Trait>> when the consumer may pass none:

#[weaveffi::export]
impl Book {
    /// Subscribe `listener` until the book is released.
    pub fn subscribe(&self, listener: Arc<dyn Listener>) {
        lock(&self.listeners).push(listener);
    }
}

The rules:

  • The trait is Send + Sync and not generic, and has at least one method. The receiver is &self, and methods are synchronous.
  • Parameters may be any value type, borrowed (&str, &Contact) or owned. Strings, bytes, typed arrays, and buffered values are lent to the consumer for the call; an object parameter moves one strong reference to the consumer, so it’s spelled Arc<T> (or Option<Arc<T>>). &mut parameters are rejected.
  • Every method returns Result<T, E>, because the consumer’s implementation can fail, and E must implement From<weaveffi::ForeignError>. T is () or any value type, including an object (Arc<T>, or Option<Arc<T>> when the consumer may return none). A plain T is a compile error. The consumer allocates a returned run with {prefix}_alloc, and the runtime adopts it for you.
  • A callback interface is valid only as a parameter of a function or an interface member, never in a return, a record, a collection, or another callback.

The error type decides what the method throws. When E is a #[weaveffi::error] domain, the method is throws: E (approve above): the consumer may fail with one of E’s codes, fields included, and it arrives as that typed variant, its message rendered by your Display from the fields. Any other consumer failure (an untyped exception, a code E doesn’t declare) reaches E through From<ForeignError>. When E is anything else (ForeignError itself, as in on_change), the method is throws: any, and every consumer failure goes through From<ForeignError>.

BookError maps foreign failures into its own ListenerFailed code, so Book::add uses ? on a listener’s typed Rejected and on any other listener failure alike, and the original caller sees a typed BookError either way.

ForeignError is what the runtime hands to From: a code (-4 when the consumer’s implementation failed or, for a thread-affine vtable, was called off its thread; -3 when it returned a value the runtime couldn’t accept) and the consumer’s message. It implements Display, so it’s a valid error type for an exported function too (which is then throws: any).

Don’t hold a lock across a callback call: the consumer’s implementation may call back into your library and wait on the same lock. Snapshot the state you need, release the lock, then call out, as Book::add does.

Async functions and cancellation

An async fn (a free function, method, or static) lowers to a launcher that returns immediately and a completion callback that fires exactly once. A trailing weaveffi::CancelToken parameter makes it cancellable:

#[weaveffi::export]
impl Book {
    /// Every contact, in id order, collected on a producer thread.
    pub async fn export_all(&self) -> Vec<Contact> {
        lock(&self.contacts).values().cloned().collect()
    }
}

/// Greet `name` after `delay`. The trailing `CancelToken` makes the
/// function cancellable: cancelling drops the future (and the sleep), and
/// the call completes as cancelled.
#[weaveffi::export]
pub async fn greet_later(name: String, delay: Timeout, _cancel: CancelToken) -> String {
    tokio::time::sleep(delay).await;
    format!("Hello, {name}!")
}

When the consumer cancels, the runtime drops your future at its next suspension point and completes the call with code -5; you don’t need to poll the token. Call cancel.is_cancelled() only for cooperative cleanup, such as work running on another thread. Only an async fn takes a CancelToken, as its last parameter. An async function can’t borrow an optional parameter (take Option<T> by value), can’t return an iterator, and can’t be a constructor. Async and Cancellation covers the executors and the C shape.

Conditional compilation and features

A #[cfg] on an exported item (a function, an impl block, a record, enum, error domain, interface struct, callback trait, or custom type) or on a member of an exported impl block applies to everything generated for it: its C functions, its contract functions, and its metadata. An item a build compiles out has none of them, so the library never claims a declaration it doesn’t have. Book::purge above exists only in a build with the crate’s admin feature.

Because weaveffi generate reads the API from the built library, the bindings follow the build’s #[cfg]. Every command that builds the producer takes -F/--features, --all-features, and --no-default-features (or [build] features, all_features, and no_default_features in weaveffi.toml), and passes them to every build: the one the API is read from and each platform’s in weaveffi build. So weaveffi generate -F admin generates bindings with purge, and they load only a library built with it: against one without it, the contract check fails with “Book.purge is missing from the library”. A library with extra declarations loads fine.

Leak checks

The weaveffi crate’s leak-check feature counts live objects, foreign callbacks, iterators, cancel tokens, and runs (including error messages). {prefix}_debug_live(kind) reports them (0 objects, 1 callbacks, 2 iterators, 3 tokens, 4 runs), and kind -1 returns 1 to say the library counts. Without the feature the symbol still exists and returns 0 for every kind. Every sample enables it, and every conformance consumer asserts that all five counts are zero at exit.

[dependencies]
weaveffi = { version = "0.25", features = ["leak-check"] }

What the macros reject

Each rejection is a compile error on the offending item:

SourceWhy
a crate without weaveffi::export_runtime!()every expansion names the crate’s tag type, which the call defines
a generic function, impl block, or typeeach exported item has one C shape
#[weaveffi::export] on a trait impl, or on anything but a fn or an implexport an interface’s inherent impl block
a parameter that isn’t a plain name (a pattern)each parameter becomes one named C slot
&mut T, *const T, *mut T parameterstake &T or a value and return the result; declare a pointee as an interface
Box<dyn Trait>callbacks are shared: take Arc<dyn Trait>
an interface method on self or &mut selfuse &self or self: Arc<Self> with interior mutability
an interface that isn’t Send + Syncobjects are shared across threads; the compiler names the field
two exported items with the same name, in any modulesa library is one namespace (the second fails with symbol ... is already defined)
a CancelToken that isn’t the last parameter of an async fncancellation belongs to async calls
an Iter parameter, an async function returning an Iteriterators are returns of synchronous callables
a callback method that’s async, takes &mut parameters, or returns a plain Treturn Result<T, E> with E: From<weaveffi::ForeignError>
a callback trait without methods, an empty record, an enum without variantseach lowers to something with at least one member
tuple fields in a record, variant, or error codefields need names to appear in the IDL
an error variant without an explicit positive discriminantcodes are stable values; 0 and negatives belong to the runtime
a discriminant that isn’t an integer literal fitting an i32it crosses as an int32_t
#[weaveffi::error(no_display)] on an enum without Displayyou opted out of the generated impl
#[weaveffi::custom] without repr, lift, or lowerall three are required
arguments to any other attributeonly error and custom take arguments

The rest of the IDL’s rules (error code names unique across domains, map key types, objects in error payloads, reserved names, and so on) are checked by the CLI’s validator when it reads the API, which every command does; run weaveffi validate or weaveffi generate --check in CI.

See also

  • Samples and Examples: kvstore uses every feature on this page.
  • C ABI Contract: what the generated functions implement.
  • Rust API Map: the items the weaveffi crate exports.

Errors and Memory

This guide states who owns every value that crosses the C ABI and how failures travel back to the caller. The generated bindings follow these rules for you; they matter when you consume the C header directly, implement it by hand, or audit a binding. The C ABI contract is the normative text. Examples use the kvstore and calculator samples, whose prefixes are kvstore and calculator.

How values cross

Every value crosses as exactly one C value, chosen by its family, in every position: a parameter, a return, an async result, an iterator item, and a callback method’s argument and return.

FamilyIDL typesC valueInto the producer (parameter)Out of the producer (return, async result, iterator item)Callback argumentCallback return
Directintegers, f32, f64, bool, C-style enumsthe scalar (an enum is an int32_t)copiedcopiedcopiedcopied
OptDirectT? of a Direct type{p}_opt_{prim} ({bool has; T value;})copiedcopiedcopiedcopied
Runstring, bytes, typed arrays ([i8] … [f64]), and every record, rich enum, other optional, list, and map{p}_run ({const uint8_t* ptr; size_t len;})borrowed for the callowned by the consumer: release with {p}_run_freeborrowed for the callallocated by the consumer with {p}_alloc; the producer adopts it
Objectinterfaces, I?const {p}_{I}* as a parameter, {p}_{I}* elsewhereborrowed for the callone strong reference moves to the consumer: release with {p}_{I}_destroyone strong reference moves to the consumerone strong reference (a fresh _clone) moves to the producer
Callbackcallback interfaces (parameters only){p}_{Cb} ({void* ctx; const vtable*})the consumer’s; the producer calls free(ctx) oncenever returnednevernever

Borrowed means the callee may read the value only until the call returns and copies anything it keeps. Everything a producer hands back belongs to the receiver, who releases it exactly once. The transfers in the other direction are a callback method’s return value, which the producer adopts, and an object passed to a callback, which the consumer adopts.

Runs

Every string, bytes, typed array, and value buffer is a run: a pointer and a length in bytes, passed by value. The producer owns the allocator for every run it hands out (a return, an async result, an iterator item, an error’s message and payload), and two runtime functions manage runs:

uint8_t* kvstore_alloc(size_t len);       /* a zero-filled, 8-aligned run; NULL for 0 */
void kvstore_run_free(kvstore_run run);   /* returned runs and alloc runs */

Every run the producer allocates has alignment 8, so a consumer can read a returned run in place as any element type (a typed array of double, say) without copying it first.

A consumer releases a returned run with {p}_run_free, passing it back exactly as it got it. It allocates a run with {p}_alloc(len) only when it hands bytes to the producer: a callback method’s string, bytes, typed array, or buffer return, which the producer adopts and frees, or (on wasm32) an argument staged in the module’s linear memory. Null with length 0 is the empty run everywhere: {p}_alloc(0) returns null, and freeing the empty run is a no-op; a null pointer with a non-zero length is malformed. Never free a run with the language’s own allocator, and never hand the producer memory that didn’t come from {p}_alloc where it adopts it. An error’s message and payload are released with the error ({p}_error_clear or {p}_error_free), never with {p}_run_free.

Strings, bytes, and typed arrays

A string is UTF-8 and never NUL-terminated, so an interior NUL survives the trip. A string that isn’t valid UTF-8, or a run with a null pointer and a non-zero length, fails the call with the marshalling code -3.

/* The calculator sample's `greet(name: string) -> string`. */
calculator_error err;
calculator_run name = {(const uint8_t*)"Ada", 3};
calculator_run text = calculator_greet(name, &err);
if (err.code == 0) {
    printf("%.*s\n", (int)text.len, (const char*)text.ptr);
    calculator_run_free(text);
}

A list of i8, i16, i32, i64, u16, u32, u64, f32, or f64 is a typed array: a run of the packed native elements. Its len still counts bytes (a multiple of the element size), and its pointer must be aligned for the element; a ragged or misaligned array fails with -3. [u8] is bytes, and [bool] is a value buffer, because its bytes must be checked.

/* calculator's `running_total(values: [i32]) -> [i32]`. */
const int32_t xs[] = {1, 2, 3};
calculator_run in = {(const uint8_t*)xs, sizeof xs};
calculator_run out = calculator_running_total(in, &err);
const int32_t* totals = (const int32_t*)out.ptr;   /* {1, 3, 6}: out.len / 4 elements */
calculator_run_free(out);

Optional scalars

An optional integer, float, bool, or C-style enum (i64?, Kind?) is a {p}_opt_{prim} struct, {bool has; T value;}, passed and returned by value; an optional enum is a {p}_opt_i32. value is 0 when has is false, and a present value is still range-checked, so an undeclared enum value fails with -3. Nothing is allocated, so nothing is freed.

/* kvstore's `Store.expires_at(key: string) -> i64?`. */
kvstore_opt_i64 at = kvstore_Store_expires_at(store, key, &err);
if (err.code != 0) {
    kvstore_error_clear(&err);                          /* failed */
} else if (at.has) {
    printf("expires at %lld\n", (long long)at.value);
}                                                       /* else: no expiry */

Value buffers

Records, rich enums, the remaining optionals (all but Interface?), lists, and maps cross as one serialized value buffer in a run, however deep the nesting. A parameter is a run the caller encodes and frees itself. A return is one producer allocation the consumer decodes and then releases once with {p}_run_free; the strings and records it decodes are copies, so nothing inside is freed separately. The exception is an object token inside a buffer, which carries one strong reference the reader adopts. Inside a buffer, optional scalars and numeric lists use the buffer encoding like every other type.

Objects

Interface objects are reference counted by the producer (an Arc<T> in a Rust producer). Each interface has two lifecycle functions:

kvstore_Store* kvstore_Store_clone(const kvstore_Store* self);  /* +1 */
void kvstore_Store_destroy(kvstore_Store* self);                /* -1 */

A pointer is a strong reference. _clone returns another reference to the same object (the pointer value is unchanged), _destroy releases one, and the object is dropped when the last reference goes. Both accept null as a no-op.

PositionRule
parameter (Store, Store?)borrowed for the call; the producer takes its own reference if it keeps the object
return, async result, iterator elementone strong reference moves to the consumer
callback method argumentone strong reference moves to the consumer
callback method returnone strong reference moves to the producer
token inside a value buffer (either direction)carries one strong reference; the reader adopts it

Two consequences follow. A consumer that encodes an object into a buffer must call _clone and write the new pointer, never the one its wrapper holds. And a buffer that contains objects is decoded exactly once, because each token is one reference.

Every generated wrapper owns exactly one reference and releases it through the language’s disposal idiom (close(), Dispose(), deinit, a C++ destructor, Close()), with a finalizer or cleaner backstop where the runtime has one. Each binding also keeps the wrapper alive for the duration of every native call, so a wrapper that becomes unreachable mid-call can’t free the object under it. An in-flight async call holds its own reference.

Callback interfaces

A callback-interface parameter is one {p}_{Cb} struct passed by value: {void* ctx; const {p}_{Cb}_vtable* vtable;}. The consumer owns ctx (a generated binding uses a key into a table that keeps the implementation alive) and a static vtable per interface, whose header records its size, its flags, and its free hook, followed by one entry per method in declaration order. A null vtable is none (Cb?). From the macro tour:

typedef struct macro_tour_Listener { void* ctx; const macro_tour_Listener_vtable* vtable; } macro_tour_Listener;
struct macro_tour_Listener_vtable {
    uint32_t size;            /* sizeof the vtable, as the consumer compiled it */
    uint32_t flags;           /* 0, or MACRO_TOUR_VTABLE_THREAD_AFFINE */
    void (*free)(void* ctx);
    bool (*approve)(void* ctx, macro_tour_run contact, macro_tour_error* out_err);
    void (*on_change)(void* ctx, macro_tour_run change, macro_tour_error* out_err);
};

The producer may call any entry, any number of times, from any thread (subject to thread affinity), until it calls free(ctx) exactly once; free may also run on any producer thread.

Vtables are open. A consumer generated before a method was appended passes a shorter vtable, and the producer accepts it: calling a method past the consumer’s size, or one whose entry is null, fails with -4 and a message naming the method, without calling anything. A vtable shorter than its header fails the call that passed it with -3.

Arguments to a callback method are lent for the call: runs must be copied or decoded before returning, and objects move one reference to the consumer. Returns move to the producer: a run the consumer returns is one it allocated with {p}_alloc(len), which the producer adopts and frees, and an object is a fresh _clone. The producer adopts whatever the method returned even when it failed, so a consumer that fails after allocating doesn’t leak. A return the producer can’t accept (text that isn’t UTF-8, a null I, an out-of-range enum value, a malformed buffer, a misaligned array) fails the method with -3.

Thread-affine callbacks

Some runtimes can only run a value-returning callback on one thread: a Dart callback that returns a value must run on its isolate’s thread. Such a binding sets bit 0 of the vtable’s flags, {P}_VTABLE_THREAD_AFFINE. The producer then records the thread that passed it the vtable, and a method that returns a value, called from any other thread, fails with -4 and the message callback called off its thread without reaching the consumer. void methods and free may still run on any thread, and the binding forwards them to its own thread. In a Rust producer the failure arrives as a ForeignError converted into the method’s error type, like any other callback failure. Only the Dart target sets the flag; every other target passes 0.

Iterators

An iter<T> return is an opaque {p}_..._Iterator*. _next(iter, &item, &err) writes one element and returns 1, or returns 0 at the end; each element is owned by the consumer under the return rules above. _next checks that out_item is non-null before it pulls an element, so a bad call never loses one. _destroy is called exactly once, on exhaustion or when the consumer abandons the iteration early. The generated wrappers do both. An iterator is advanced by one caller at a time: a _next that arrives while another is in progress on the same iterator (from another thread, or re-entrantly from a callback the producer’s next calls) fails with -3 rather than blocking.

/* kvstore's `Store.keys(prefix: string?) -> iter<string>`. */
kvstore_Store_KeysIterator* keys = kvstore_Store_keys(store, no_prefix, &err);
kvstore_run key;
while (kvstore_Store_KeysIterator_next(keys, &key, &err) == 1) {
    printf("%.*s\n", (int)key.len, (const char*)key.ptr);
    kvstore_run_free(key);
}
kvstore_Store_KeysIterator_destroy(keys);

Errors

The error struct

Every synchronous function, interface member, iterator launcher, and iterator _next takes a trailing {p}_error* out_err:

typedef struct kvstore_error {
    int32_t code;                /* 0 success, >0 domain code, <0 runtime code */
    const uint8_t* message_ptr;  /* UTF-8, NOT NUL-terminated, producer-owned */
    size_t message_len;
    const uint8_t* payload_ptr;  /* the code's fields as a value buffer, or NULL */
    size_t payload_len;
} kvstore_error;

The call overwrites the slot without reading it: on success it stores a cleared error, and on failure it stores the code, message, and payload and returns a zero value (0, false, NULL, the empty run, an absent optional) that must not be used or freed. So the caller may pass uninitialized memory, and after reading a failure it releases the message and payload with {p}_error_clear(&err). A struct reused for many calls must be cleared after each failure, or the message leaks.

The message is message_len bytes of UTF-8 at message_ptr, with no NUL terminator, so print it with a length (%.*s) or copy it. An empty message is a null message_ptr with length 0, even when code is non-zero.

kvstore_error err;
uint64_t n = kvstore_Store_count(store, &err);
if (err.code != 0) {
    fprintf(stderr, "count failed (%d): %.*s\n",
            err.code, (int)err.message_len, (const char*)err.message_ptr);
    kvstore_error_clear(&err);
}

An async completion receives a heap-boxed error instead (or NULL on success), released with {p}_error_free, which also frees the box. _clone, _destroy, an iterator’s _destroy, and async launchers take no out_err.

Codes

CodeMeaning
0success
> 0a code of the error domain the callable throws
-1untyped: the failure of a throws: any callable, or an async call the executor couldn’t start
-2the producer panicked; the message carries the panic text
-3marshalling failure: a null run with a non-zero length, a null self or object where one is required, invalid UTF-8, a bool byte other than 0 or 1, an out-of-range enum value or integer conversion (a usize that doesn’t fit, a char that isn’t one scalar), a ragged or misaligned typed array, a custom type’s lift rejecting its input, a malformed value buffer (including a map with a repeated key), an incomplete vtable header, an iterator advanced concurrently
-4a consumer’s callback-interface implementation failed, doesn’t implement the method called, or was called off its thread; the message is the consumer’s or names the method
-5cancelled: an async call’s cancel token fired, or its executor dropped it (async completions only)

Domain codes are positive, so the two ranges never collide. Code values are unique only within a domain (the calculator sample’s CalcError.DivisionByZero and ParseError.NotANumber are both 1), so a binding interprets a positive code by the domain of the callable that reported it. The C header declares each domain as a typedef int32_t with its codes as constants (kvstore_KvError_KeyNotFound = 1001).

Domain errors and the trap channel

Every callable has one error strategy, recorded on it in the model, and every target applies it the same way:

  • Domain (throws: KvError). A positive code is a typed domain error: an exception subclass, a Swift Error case, a Go error value, carrying the code, the producer’s message, and the payload fields as properties. Domains are open: a positive code the binding wasn’t generated with (the producer gained a code since) surfaces as the domain’s base error type with the code and message preserved, and its payload is discarded undecoded. A negative code surfaces as the package’s root error type with that code.
  • Untyped (throws: any). Failure is -1 with a message, and surfaces as the package’s root error type carrying the message. There’s no code to match on. Negative runtime codes surface the same way.
  • Trap (no throws). The function can’t report an error, so any non-zero code is a bug, and the binding traps on it (see the trap policy). It never dresses the code up as a domain error.

Under every strategy, -5 on an async call surfaces as the language’s own cancellation error (see Async and Cancellation).

Error types are named after the IDL (each target’s root error has a fixed name or the package’s; see Naming), never after WeaveFFI. Every target derives a domain’s type name from its IDL name with one shared rule (see Naming): KvError stays KvError (or becomes KvException), and Failure becomes FailureError.

A Rust producer’s domain error message is its Display output, generated from the variant’s #[weaveffi(message = "...")] template or doc comment. The IDL’s message: (a Rust variant’s template, else its doc comment’s first line) is the documented default that generated docs and consumer-side constructors use.

The trap policy

A failure of a call that can’t report one is a producer bug (a panic, an argument the producer couldn’t lift, a callback failure it let through a call that can’t report one), and every binding treats it the same way: it raises the language’s unchecked error rather than a declared one, naming the runtime code and the producer’s message so the bug can be diagnosed from the report. In C, the caller reads err.code itself; Swift, which has no unchecked errors, stops with fatalError; Go panics; the other targets throw an error type outside the domain hierarchy. Each language page names its exact type. Cancellation (-5) isn’t a bug and always surfaces as the language’s cancellation error.

Load-time checks

Before the first call, every binding checks the library’s ABI revision and the contract of every declaration it was generated with. A failed check is a catchable error, never a crash, so an application can report a stale or mismatched library and carry on: the language’s load or import error where loading can fail, and a check function where it can’t (Swift and Go). A C consumer compares {p}_abi_version() with {P}_ABI_VERSION and calls {p}_contract_check() itself, as Getting Started shows.

The message names the declaration that’s missing or changed (“Store.get changed since these bindings were generated”). Declarations the library has and the bindings don’t are fine.

Payloads

An error code may declare fields. When the producer raises it, the fields are serialized into payload_ptr/payload_len in the value-buffer format, and each binding exposes them as properties of the error it raises. A code with no fields leaves the payload null. {p}_error_clear and {p}_error_free release the payload with the message. A payload never carries an object (a binding that doesn’t know a code discards its payload undecoded).

When a callback fails

A consumer’s callback implementation can fail in the consumer’s language. The binding catches the failure in its trampoline and reports it through the vtable entry’s out_err with the runtime surface:

void kvstore_error_set(kvstore_error* err, int32_t code,
                       const uint8_t* message_ptr, size_t message_len);
void kvstore_error_set_payload(kvstore_error* err, const uint8_t* ptr, size_t len);

Both copy their arguments with the producer’s allocator; a consumer never stores its own allocation in the struct. What the consumer reports follows the method’s strategy:

  • A method that throws a domain (throws: KvError) may report a positive code of that domain, attaching the code’s fields encoded as a value buffer with {p}_error_set_payload. Any other failure it reports as -1 with a message.
  • A method that throws any reports -1 with a message.
  • A method that declares no errors reports -4 with a message (any non-zero code is accepted).

On the producer’s side, a declared code whose payload decodes arrives as that typed error. Everything else (-1, a code the domain doesn’t declare, any failure of a method without a domain) reaches the producer as a callback failure, -4 with the consumer’s message, so a consumer bug can’t impersonate a domain error.

The producer decides what the failure means for the call in progress. Nothing unwinds and nothing is deferred: in a Rust producer every callback trait method returns Result<T, E> with E: From<weaveffi::ForeignError>, and the failure arrives as an Err. When E is the method’s domain, a typed code arrives as its variant (with the message re-rendered from its fields by the domain’s Display), and any other failure is converted from a ForeignError. A producer function propagates the failure with ? into its own error type. Mapping ForeignError into a variant of the function’s domain (the macro tour’s BookError::ListenerFailed) keeps the call typed for the original caller; returning ForeignError itself makes the function throws: any, so the original caller sees -1 with the consumer’s message.

Panics

A Rust producer’s generated functions catch panics and report them as -2. Destructors (_destroy) have no error slot, so a panic in a Drop implementation is swallowed rather than unwinding into C. Async functions report panics through the completion callback.

Leak checks

{p}_debug_live(kind) reports how many resources are live, so a test harness can assert that a consumer released everything: 0 objects, 1 foreign callbacks, 2 iterators, 3 cancel tokens, and 4 runs (returned runs, error messages and payloads not yet cleared, and {p}_alloc runs not yet freed or adopted). Kind -1 returns 1 when the producer counts at all and 0 when it doesn’t, so a harness can tell “nothing is live” from “nothing is counted”. A Rust producer counts only with the weaveffi crate’s leak-check feature; without it every kind returns 0. A consumer that keeps a failed error around without clearing it shows up as a live run.

Thread safety

Every #[weaveffi::interface] type is Send + Sync (the macro asserts it), so a macro-built producer may be called from any thread, and the bindings add no thread restriction of their own. Callback methods and async completions may arrive on any producer thread; each binding hops to its own scheduler where the language requires it, and the Dart binding marks its vtables thread-affine. A hand-written producer with thread-affine state should document it, and must honor the vtable flag.

Pitfalls

  • Treating len as an element count. A typed array’s len counts bytes, like every run’s.
  • Reading the message as a C string. It isn’t NUL-terminated and may be null; use message_len.
  • Freeing borrowed inputs. Parameters are the caller’s; never pass them to run_free or _destroy.
  • Misaligned typed arrays. A typed-array parameter must be aligned for its element; slicing a byte buffer at an odd offset fails the call with -3.
  • Encoding an object without _clone. The token hands away a reference your wrapper still needs; the later _destroy over-releases.
  • Decoding an object-carrying buffer twice. The second decode adopts references that no longer exist.
  • Not clearing an error after a failure. The message and payload leak.
  • error_clear on an async error. It leaks the box; use error_free.
  • Freeing a callback’s returned run. A run a callback method returns belongs to the producer once returned; allocate it with {p}_alloc and don’t free it.
  • Holding a lock across a callback call. The consumer’s implementation may call back into the producer, which then waits on the lock you hold; snapshot state, release the lock, then call.

Async and Cancellation

An async function returns immediately and delivers its result later, through the language’s own async idiom: async/await in Swift, Python, JavaScript, C#, and Dart, a suspend fun in Kotlin, a std::future in C++, and a blocking call that honors a context.Context in Go. A cancellable async function is also cancelled through that idiom: cancelling the awaiting task, coroutine, or promise cancels the native call.

Declaring async functions

In Rust, write an async fn (a free function, method, or static). A final weaveffi::CancelToken parameter makes it cancellable. From the macro tour:

#[weaveffi::export]
impl Book {
    /// Every contact, in id order, collected on a producer thread.
    pub async fn export_all(&self) -> Vec<Contact> {
        lock(&self.contacts).values().cloned().collect()
    }
}

/// Greet `name` after `delay`. The trailing `CancelToken` makes the
/// function cancellable: cancelling drops the future (and the sleep), and
/// the call completes as cancelled.
#[weaveffi::export]
pub async fn greet_later(name: String, delay: Timeout, _cancel: CancelToken) -> String {
    tokio::time::sleep(delay).await;
    format!("Hello, {name}!")
}

In an IDL, set async: true, and optionally cancellable: true, on a function, method, or static:

functions:
  - name: greet_later
    doc: Greet `name` after `delay_ms` milliseconds.
    params:
      - { name: name, type: string }
      - { name: delay_ms, type: u64 }
    return: string
    async: true
    cancellable: true

async composes with throws, with object and buffered results, and with every parameter type. Constructors can’t be async (AsyncConstructor), so an async factory is an async function or static that returns the interface. In Rust, an async fn in an exported impl that returns Self (or the interface) is exported as such a static, and a free async fn returning the object works too. Async functions can’t return iterators (AsyncIteratorReturn), and cancellable without async is a validation error (CancellableNotAsync). A Rust async fn can’t borrow an optional parameter (Option<&T>); take Option<T>.

The C ABI shape

An async function lowers to a launcher named after the function itself and a completion callback type named {symbol}_callback:

typedef void (*macro_tour_greet_later_callback)(void* context, macro_tour_error* err,
                                                macro_tour_run result);

void macro_tour_greet_later(macro_tour_run name, uint64_t delay,
                            macro_tour_cancel_token* cancel_token,  /* cancellable only */
                            macro_tour_greet_later_callback callback, void* context);

result is the return’s one C value, exactly as for a synchronous return (a scalar, a {p}_opt_*, a run, or an object pointer), and is absent for a function that returns nothing: the completion is then void (*)(void* context, {p}_error* err). kvstore’s async Store.version_of(key: string) -> u32? completes through void (*)(void* context, kvstore_error* err, kvstore_opt_u32 result). On failure result is the zero value. The launcher has no out_err: every failure, including a marshalling failure of an input, arrives through the callback.

The completion contract

The runtime guarantees these for every launch:

  1. Exactly once. The callback fires exactly once, on success, on a domain error, on a marshalling failure (-3), on a panic (-2, including one while the launcher lifts its inputs), on a consumer callback failure (-4), on cancellation (-5), and when the executor can’t take the call (-1). It may fire before the launcher returns, and a null callback discards the result.
  2. From any thread. It runs on whatever thread completes the future: an executor thread, a Tokio worker, or (on wasm32) the caller’s own stack. Each binding hops back to its own scheduler before touching consumer state.
  3. Inputs taken at launch. The launcher copies runs and retains objects and self before it returns, so the consumer may release its arguments as soon as the launcher returns.
  4. Owned results. err is NULL on success; otherwise it’s heap-boxed, owned by the consumer, and released with {p}_error_free. A run result is owned by the consumer and released with {p}_run_free after decoding, and an object result moves one strong reference.
  5. Dropped futures complete. If the executor drops a future before it finishes (a runtime shutting down, say), the call completes with -5.
  6. Spawn failures complete. If the executor can’t take the future (a custom spawner panics, or the default executor can’t start its threads), the call completes with -1 and a message saying why.

A consumer of the raw C surface follows the same rules in its callback: read or decode everything, free it, and adopt object pointers.

Cancellation

The runtime surface provides reference-counted cancel tokens:

macro_tour_cancel_token* token = macro_tour_cancel_token_create();  /* refcount 1 */
macro_tour_greet_later(name, 60000, token, on_done, ctx);
macro_tour_cancel_token_cancel(token);    /* any thread, any time; idempotent */
macro_tour_cancel_token_destroy(token);   /* drops the consumer's reference */

The launcher takes its own reference on the token, so the consumer may cancel and destroy its reference at any point after the launch, even before the call completes. Cancelling wakes the producer’s future; the runtime then drops the future and completes the call with code -5 and the message cancelled, unless it had already completed. A null token means “never cancelled”.

Producer code doesn’t need to check the token: dropping the future cancels it at its next suspension point, and RAII guards run as usual. Poll cancel.is_cancelled() only for cooperative cleanup of work the future doesn’t own, such as a thread it spawned (the kvstore sample’s Store::compact stops its background pause that way). A future that never suspends can’t be interrupted; it runs to completion and its result wins.

Per-target surface

TargetAsync surfaceCancellation
Clauncher plus callbacka {p}_cancel_token* argument
C++std::future<T>a cancel-token argument
Swiftasync (async throws)cancelling the Task
Kotlinsuspend funcancelling the coroutine
Node.jsPromise<T>an AbortSignal
WebAssemblyPromise<T>an AbortSignal
Pythonawaitablecancelling the awaiting task
.NETTask<T>a CancellationToken
DartFuture<T>a cancel-token object
Goblocking call with a context.Contextthe context’s cancellation
Rubyblocking calla cancel-token keyword argument
