A cancelled call raises the language’s cancellation error. Go and Ruby block the calling goroutine or thread until the completion fires (the native work still runs off-thread), so call them from a goroutine or Thread for concurrency. The exact types and signatures are on each language page.

Choosing an executor

Something has to drive each future between launch and completion. The runtime routes every future to the process-wide executor:

  • Tokio (the default). The weaveffi crate’s tokio feature is on by default, and with it the executor is Tokio: the current runtime when the launcher is called from inside one, otherwise a multi-thread runtime the library creates on first use (its threads are named weaveffi-async). That runtime enables every Tokio driver the build compiles in, so turn on the Tokio features your futures need (time, net, and so on) in your own dependency on tokio, as the macro tour does for tokio::time::sleep. Use tokio::task::spawn_blocking for synchronous work inside a future.

    [dependencies]
weaveffi = "0.25"
tokio = { version = "1", features = ["time"] }

  • A thread per call. With default-features = false, there’s no Tokio dependency, and each async call runs on a thread of its own (also named weaveffi-async), which drives the future with a minimal blocking executor and exits when the call completes. A future woken from another thread resumes on its own thread. There’s no reactor, so a future that awaits Tokio’s I/O or timers never wakes; use this for futures that wait only on channels, other threads, or nothing at all. A thread that can’t start completes the call with -1.

    [dependencies]
weaveffi = { version = "0.25", default-features = false }

  • Custom. Call weaveffi::set_spawner once at startup, before the first async launch; it overrides both defaults. It accepts any Fn(BoxFuture) + Send + Sync + 'static:

    /// Run every async export on `handle`'s runtime instead of the default

/// executor. Call it once, before the first async call. pub fn run_async_exports_on(handle: tokio::runtime::Handle) { let installed = weaveffi::set_spawner(move |future| { handle.spawn(future); }); // Only the first spawner installed in a process takes effect. let _ = installed; }


The first call wins; later calls return an error. The spawner must not
block, because the launcher calls it on the consumer's thread. Every
future it receives is already wrapped so a panic becomes a `-2`
completion, a spawner that drops a future still produces a `-5`
completion, and a spawner that panics completes the call with `-1`.
- **`wasm32`.** There are no threads (Tokio is never compiled in there, so
the `tokio` feature has no effect), and the future is polled inline before
the launcher returns. A future that finishes after bounded work is fine. A
future that's still pending once nothing can wake it (it awaits a timer or
I/O) is dropped, and the call completes with `-1` and the message `async
function suspended with no executor on wasm32` rather than hanging the
module. A call usually completes before an `AbortSignal` can fire, so on
this target cancellation mostly matters for a signal that's already
aborted when the call starts.

## Pitfalls

- **Awaiting Tokio without the `tokio` feature.** The thread-per-call
executor has no reactor, so the future never wakes and the callback never
fires; keep the default feature or install a Tokio spawner.
- **Blocking a Tokio worker.** A future that blocks (synchronous I/O, a
long computation) holds a runtime thread; wrap the work in
`tokio::task::spawn_blocking`.
- **Expecting the token to stop CPU-bound work.** Cancellation drops the
future at a suspension point. Long synchronous loops should yield or check
`is_cancelled()`.
- **Clearing an async error.** `err` is boxed; release it with
`{p}_error_free`, not `error_clear`.
- **Leaking a result run.** A raw C callback that only copies the result
must still free it with `{p}_run_free`.

Project Configuration

A project’s settings live in one weaveffi.toml at its root. The API definition (a Rust producer crate or an IDL) describes only the API; this file says where it is, what the library is called, how it’s built and packaged, and how each target is generated. Every table is optional, and every unknown table or key is an error, so a typo fails the run instead of being ignored.

[project]
input = "."                 # this crate, or an IDL such as "kvstore.yml"
out = "bindings"
targets = ["c", "swift", "kotlin", "python"]

[package]
version = "1.2.0"
description = "An embedded key-value store"
license = "MIT"
authors = ["Example <hello@example.dev>"]
repository = "https://github.com/example/kvstore"

[build]
platforms = ["darwin-arm64", "ios-arm64", "ios-sim-arm64", "android-arm64"]
features = ["sync"]

[generators.swift]
name = "KVStore"

weaveffi init writes a starter file: in a Rust crate it points input at the crate ("."); elsewhere it also writes a starter IDL.

Discovery

With an explicit input (weaveffi generate api.yml, weaveffi generate path/to/crate), the CLI uses the nearest weaveffi.toml at or above the input. With no input, it looks for weaveffi.toml in the current directory and its parents and reads [project] input, so weaveffi generate works from anywhere inside the project, like cargo build. --config <path> names a file explicitly. Without any file, every setting takes its default.

[project]

KeyDefaultMeaning
inputnoneThe API definition: a Rust producer crate (its directory, usually ".", or its Cargo.toml), whose API is read from its built library (see Library Mode), or a .yml, .yaml, or .json IDL (TOML is only for this file)
outbindingsOutput directory for generate
targetsevery default targetTargets to generate when --target isn’t given (weaveffi init writes ["c", "python"])

Paths are relative to the directory holding weaveffi.toml. Command-line arguments (the input, -o/--out, -t/--target) override the table. A targets entry that isn’t a target is an error, so a typo can’t silently generate nothing.

[package]

[package] resolves into the library’s identity: the package name every ecosystem publishes under, the C symbol prefix, and the native library base name. Generators derive every other name from these three.

KeyMeaning
nameDistribution name (may contain - or .)
versionVersion stamped into every manifest (default 0.1.0)
description, license, authors, homepage, repositoryManifest metadata
c_prefixC symbol prefix; IDL inputs only
libraryNative library base name (lib{library}.so); IDL inputs only
distWhere weaveffi package writes its artifacts (default dist); not part of the identity

The rules depend on the input:

Rust producer (a crate)IDL
name[package] name, else the crate’s Cargo package name[package] name, else the input file stem
prefixthe crate’s library name ([lib] name, else the package name with - mapped to _)c_prefix, else snake-case name
librarysame as prefix (the cdylib WeaveFFI builds)library, else snake-case name
metadata[package], falling back to cargo metadata (workspace-inherited version.workspace = true included)[package]

A Rust producer’s prefix is what the attributes compiled into its symbols, so it can’t be configured: setting c_prefix or library for a Rust producer is an error. Renaming the published package with name is fine.

[build]

How a Rust producer is compiled. profile, the deployment targets, and platforms apply to weaveffi build and weaveffi package (see Packaging); the commands that build a producer’s host library to read its API (generate, validate, and extract) use the dev profile unless --profile names another. The feature keys apply to every build.

KeyDefaultMeaning
featuresnoneCargo features every build of the producer enables
all_featuresfalseBuild the producer with --all-features
no_default_featuresfalseBuild the producer with --no-default-features
platformsthe hostPlatform ids to build when --platforms isn’t given
profile"release"The Cargo profile build and package use (--profile overrides it)
macos_deployment_target"11.0"MACOSX_DEPLOYMENT_TARGET, and the macOS version wheel tags carry
ios_deployment_target"13.0"IPHONEOS_DEPLOYMENT_TARGET
android_api21The minimum Android API level, which picks the NDK compiler
manifestthe project’s crateThe producer’s Cargo.toml, for an IDL project built from a Rust crate

Features reach every build. -F/--features, --all-features, and --no-default-features on generate, validate, extract, build, or package replace the three feature keys, and whichever set applies is passed to every build of the producer: the host build library mode reads the API from as much as each platform’s build. So a declaration behind #[cfg(feature = "...")] is in the bindings exactly when it’s in the libraries they load.

[generators.<target>]

One table per target (c, cpp, swift, kotlin, node, wasm, python, dotnet, dart, go, ruby) holds that generator’s options, and each target’s page under Generators lists its keys. Every target that names its package after the library spells the override name (the Swift package and module, the npm package, the .NET namespace, the Go module path, and so on). Name overrides never change which native library a package loads; that’s always {library}. No target has a C prefix of its own: the prefix belongs to the library.

The C target has one key:

KeyDefaultMeaning
buffer_helperstrueAlso emit {library}_buffer.h, with a C struct and codec for every value-buffer type, when the API passes any value buffers

Generation records

Rendering is pure: each target renders its files in memory, and the orchestrator does every write. Every run renders every selected target, then:

  • Only files whose contents changed are rewritten, so file timestamps (and incremental builds) stay stable. When nothing changed, nothing is written.
  • The orchestrator records the files each target produced in {out}/.weaveffi/{target}.json. Files the previous run recorded that the current run no longer produces (a renamed type, a removed function) are deleted. Files you added under the output directory (a node_modules/, a build directory) are never touched, and a record entry outside its target’s own directory is ignored.

Commit the records with the generated bindings, so generate --check on a fresh checkout sees stale files too. --dry-run validates and prints every file the targets render, without writing.

CI

weaveffi generate --check renders in memory and compares with the output directory without writing anything:

weaveffi generate --check                       # uses [project]
weaveffi generate path/to/crate -o bindings --target c,swift --check

It lists every file that would change (+ added, ~ modified, - removed, where a stale file from the previous run counts as removed) and exits 0 when the directory is up to date and 1 otherwise. weaveffi generate --diff prints the same changes as a unified diff, also without writing (it exits 0; add --check to exit 1 on changes). --dry-run and --dev can’t be combined with either. weaveffi validate --format json prints one JSON object with the result, counts, and any errors (each with a code matching the error catalog); --warn adds advisory warnings, which never change the exit status.

Pitfalls

  • Config travels with the definition. Two checkouts with different weaveffi.toml files generate different packages from the same source. Commit the file next to the definition and let discovery find it.
  • Features on one command only. Building the libraries you ship with a feature that generate didn’t have (or the reverse) gives bindings that don’t match them. Put shipped features in [build] features so every command sees the same set.
  • Name overrides don’t rename the library. The targets that load the library at run time also accept a full path in the {PREFIX}_LIBRARY environment variable; the targets that link it (C, C++, Swift, and Go) find it when they’re built (see each target’s page).

Packaging

weaveffi generate emits binding source. Shipping it takes two more steps: weaveffi build cross-compiles the Rust producer once per platform, and weaveffi package turns those builds into the artifacts each ecosystem installs: wheels, npm tarballs, gems, a SwiftPM package with its XCFramework, a NuGet package, and so on. Consumers install them with pip, npm, gem, SwiftPM, or dotnet and need no compiler.

weaveffi build --platforms darwin-arm64,ios-arm64,ios-sim-arm64
weaveffi package --target python,node,swift

Both read [project], [package], and [build] from weaveffi.toml (or take an explicit input), and every artifact loads the library named by the identity’s library field.

Build

weaveffi build finds the producer crate with cargo metadata (the project’s crate, [build] manifest, or --manifest-path) and runs cargo rustc --lib once per platform with the artifact kinds that platform needs, so the crate’s own crate-type doesn’t matter. It builds with the release profile unless --profile (or [build] profile) names another, and with the project’s Cargo features (-F/--features, --all-features, --no-default-features, or the [build] keys). A Rust producer’s API is then read from the first library it built (see Library Mode), so nothing is compiled twice. build produces only the producer’s library: no target needs compiled glue of its own.

PlatformWhat build produces
macOSthe cdylib, with install name @rpath/lib{library}.dylib, plus the staticlib an XCFramework slice is made from
iOSthe staticlib
Linux, Androidthe cdylib, with soname lib{library}.so (Android also 16 KB page-aligned)
Windowsthe DLL and its import library
wasm32the .wasm module, linked with --export-table --growable-table

It sets MACOSX_DEPLOYMENT_TARGET and IPHONEOS_DEPLOYMENT_TARGET from [build], and for Android it finds the NDK (ANDROID_NDK_HOME, ANDROID_NDK_ROOT, or the newest ndk/<version> of the Android SDK) and points Cargo’s linker and the cc crate at the NDK’s clang for the configured API level. Everything lands in the Cargo target directory:

target/weaveffi/
  darwin-arm64/  libkvstore.dylib  libkvstore.a
  ios-arm64/     libkvstore.a
  android-arm64/ libkvstore.so
  wasm32/        kvstore.wasm

Every platform is checked before anything compiles; a platform this machine can’t build (a missing rustup target, a missing NDK, or a host that can’t build it: Apple platforms need macOS, Windows needs Windows) is skipped with a warning naming the fix, and the run fails only when no requested platform can be built.

build and package follow one policy for a missing external tool (a C compiler, the NDK, Xcode, the .NET SDK): they warn, skip the artifact that needs it, finish everything else, and list every skipped artifact at the end. The run still exits 0; pass --strict to exit 1 when anything was skipped (as CI should).

Package

weaveffi package runs build for the requested platforms, then writes each target’s artifacts to the dist directory (-o, else [package] dist, else dist). With --binaries <dir>, it skips the build and reads a directory laid out like target/weaveffi/ instead; that’s how CI hands libraries built on separate runners to one packaging job, and how a producer that isn’t a Rust crate is packaged. A Rust producer’s API is read from the first library in the directory.

TargetArtifactsPublish with
pythonone platform wheel per desktop platform, the library inside the import packagetwine upload
nodeone npm tarball per desktop platform ({package}-{os}-{cpu}) carrying only the library, plus the main package, which lists them as optional dependencies and depends on koffinpm publish for each, platform packages first
wasman npm tarball with the .wasm modulenpm publish
rubyone platform gem per desktop platformgem push
swiftC{Module}.xcframework.zip (static libraries for every Apple platform built), its .sha256, and the SwiftPM package whose binary target points at the archive’s URL and checksumupload the archive, then commit and tag the package
dotneta project with runtimes/<rid>/native/ libraries, and the .nupkg that dotnet pack builds from itdotnet nuget push
kotlina Gradle project with the Android libraries under src/main/jniLibs/<abi>/ and the desktop ones under JNA’s src/main/resources/<os>-<arch>/Gradle
darta pub package with the desktop libraries under native/<platform>/dart pub publish
goa module with the libraries and cgo flags that link the right onepush the module to its repository
c, cpparchives of the headers, the libraries per platform, and a CMakeLists.txtattach to a release

Each target’s page under Generators documents its artifacts’ names and layout. Each archive is deterministic (fixed timestamps and order), so the same builds always produce the same bytes and checksums. A target skips platforms its ecosystem has no slot for (a wheel has no Android tag, NuGet no wasm32 runtime), and a target with nothing to ship is named in a note.

Platforms

--platforms takes a comma-separated list of platform ids; without it, build and package use [build] platforms, else the host. With --binaries, the default is every platform directory present.

Platform idRust targetShipped by
darwin-arm64aarch64-apple-darwinevery desktop target, and swift
darwin-x64x86_64-apple-darwinevery desktop target, and swift
linux-x64x86_64-unknown-linux-gnuevery desktop target
linux-arm64aarch64-unknown-linux-gnuevery desktop target
windows-x64x86_64-pc-windows-msvcevery desktop target
ios-arm64aarch64-apple-iosswift
ios-sim-arm64aarch64-apple-ios-simswift
ios-sim-x64x86_64-apple-iosswift
android-arm64aarch64-linux-androidkotlin (and c)
android-x64x86_64-linux-androidkotlin (and c)
wasm32wasm32-unknown-unknownwasm (and c)

Swift and the XCFramework

package fuses the Apple static libraries into C{Module}.xcframework (iOS device, iOS simulator, and macOS slices, with a group’s architectures fused by lipo), zips it, and writes its SHA-256 checksum. The packaged Package.swift declares the C module as a .binaryTarget(url:checksum:), so apps link the library with no flags. The URL comes from the Swift target’s configuration (see Swift); to publish, upload the archive to that URL and commit the package to the repository consumers depend on. This step runs xcodebuild, so it needs macOS with Xcode; elsewhere the Swift package is skipped.

What still needs the ecosystem’s tools

  • Publishing. There’s no weaveffi publish; run each ecosystem’s own upload command on the artifacts.
  • NuGet. package runs dotnet pack, so the .NET SDK must be installed; without it the .nupkg is skipped (and --strict fails the run) after everything else is written.
  • Kotlin. The output is a Gradle project, not an .aar or .jar, so build one with Gradle.
  • Cross-architecture Linux. Building linux-arm64 on an x64 host needs a cross linker (CARGO_TARGET_AARCH64_UNKNOWN_LINUX_GNU_LINKER); an arm64 runner is simpler.

CI recipe

Build each platform on a runner that can, upload target/weaveffi/, then package once:

jobs:
  build:
    strategy:
      matrix:
        include:
          - runner: macos-latest
            platforms: darwin-arm64,darwin-x64,ios-arm64,ios-sim-arm64
            targets: aarch64-apple-darwin,x86_64-apple-darwin,aarch64-apple-ios,aarch64-apple-ios-sim
          - runner: ubuntu-latest
            platforms: linux-x64,android-arm64,android-x64,wasm32
            targets: aarch64-linux-android,x86_64-linux-android,wasm32-unknown-unknown
          - { runner: ubuntu-24.04-arm, platforms: linux-arm64, targets: "" }
          - { runner: windows-latest, platforms: windows-x64, targets: "" }
    runs-on: ${{ matrix.runner }}
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4
      - uses: dtolnay/rust-toolchain@stable
        with:
          targets: ${{ matrix.targets }}
      - run: cargo install weaveffi-cli
      - run: weaveffi build --platforms ${{ matrix.platforms }} --strict
      - uses: actions/upload-artifact@v4
        with:
          name: weaveffi-${{ matrix.runner }}
          path: target/weaveffi/

  package:
    needs: build
    runs-on: macos-latest   # the XCFramework needs xcodebuild
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4
      - uses: actions/download-artifact@v4
        with:
          pattern: weaveffi-*
          path: prebuilt
          merge-multiple: true
      - run: cargo install weaveffi-cli
      - run: weaveffi package --binaries prebuilt --strict

The Linux runner finds the NDK through ANDROID_NDK_HOME (or the SDK preinstalled on GitHub’s images). A platform you can’t build can be dropped from the matrix; every target packages whatever subset is present.

Library Mode

The CLI never reads Rust source. A Rust producer’s API is read from the library the crate builds: each WeaveFFI attribute embeds a description of its declaration in the compiled library, and weaveffi generate, validate, extract, build, and package read it back out. The bindings therefore describe exactly what the library was compiled with: #[cfg], Cargo features, type aliases, and macro expansion all resolved.

A Rust project’s input is the crate: its directory ([project] input = ".", which weaveffi init writes) or its Cargo.toml.

weaveffi generate                    # build the crate (dev profile), read it, generate
weaveffi generate --profile release  # read a release build instead
weaveffi generate -F sync,metrics    # build it with Cargo features
weaveffi generate --library target/x86_64-unknown-linux-gnu/release/libkvstore.so

Without --library, the CLI resolves the crate with cargo metadata (its name, version, library name, and target directory), runs cargo rustc --lib --crate-type cdylib --profile <profile> with the project’s Cargo features (Cargo’s errors and warnings print as usual), and reads the cdylib it built, so the crate needs no crate-type of its own. --profile defaults to dev, and the features are -F/--features, --all-features, and --no-default-features, else [build] features, all_features, and no_default_features (see Project Configuration): the same set every build of the producer uses, so the bindings and the shipped libraries agree on every #[cfg(feature = "...")].

--library skips the build and reads the given file: a shared library, a static archive, or a .wasm module, for any platform (an Android .so reads fine on a Mac). With no crate at all (no input argument and no [project] input), --library reads the library on its own: the prefix comes from its metadata and the library name from its file name, and a file holding the metadata of several WeaveFFI crates is an error that asks for the crate.

Extracting an IDL

weaveffi extract prints the API the library embeds as an IDL document, which is useful for reviewing the API surface, committing a canonical IDL next to the crate, or handing the API to a non-Rust implementation.

weaveffi extract                       # the project's crate, YAML to stdout
weaveffi extract path/to/crate -o api.yml
weaveffi extract -f json -o api.json   # JSON
weaveffi extract --library libkvstore.dylib
FlagDefaultMeaning
-o, --outstdoutWrite to a file
-f, --formatyamlyaml or json
--librarybuild the crateRead this library instead
--profiledevThe Cargo profile to build the crate with
-F, --features; --all-features; --no-default-features[build]’sThe Cargo features to build the crate with
--configdiscoveredThe weaveffi.toml to use

The document is validated before it’s printed, so what extract prints always generates. An IDL input is an error: there’s nothing to extract.

The extracted IDL carries no package identity. When you generate from it, the identity comes from [package] in weaveffi.toml (IDL rules), not from Cargo.toml, so set name, c_prefix, and library to match the crate if the generated bindings must load the Rust library.

How the API is embedded

For every declaration (a function, an interface and each of its members, a record, an enum, an error domain, a callback interface) the attribute emits an exported static holding one frame, a little-endian u32 length and then compact JSON recording the declaration (in the IDL’s own shape), the source location of its attribute, the crate’s prefix, the schema version, and the ABI revision. The static is named the crate’s uppercase prefix, _META_, and 16 uppercase hex digits of the FNV-1a 64 hash of the declaration’s path (Book.add), so it never collides with another declaration or another crate:

#[unsafe(export_name = "MACRO_TOUR_META_...")]
#[cfg_attr(target_family = "wasm", unsafe(link_section = "weaveffi_meta"))]
#[used]
pub static __WEAVEFFI_META: [u8; N] = /* the frame */;

The CLI reads native libraries with the object crate, walking the symbol tables for _META_ symbols and reading each one’s bytes from the section that holds it (every member of a static archive included). A wasm32 module keeps its data out of reach of any symbol table, so there the frames go to the weaveffi_meta custom section, which the linker fills with every frame back to back. The CLI keeps the frames carrying the crate’s own prefix (a dependency that is itself a WeaveFFI producer embeds frames too) and assembles them into the API, ordering each kind of declaration by where its attribute is written (file, line, then column), whatever order the linker laid the statics out in. A frame built by another WeaveFFI version (another schema version or ABI revision) is an error.

A frame carries its declaration’s #[cfg] (and, for an interface member, its impl block’s), so a declaration a build compiles out is missing from that build’s metadata exactly as it’s missing from its symbols and its contract functions. The macro tour’s Book::purge is in the API only when the crate is built with its admin feature (weaveffi extract -F admin).

A library with no frames for the crate (the API isn’t tagged) is an error that says what’s missing: tagged items and one weaveffi::export_runtime!() call.

Iterating: generate --dev

weaveffi generate --dev generates as usual, then points the generated packages at the library the API was read from (the one it just built, or --library’s):

  • it copies the library into each generated package that looks for a bundled copy (when the library is a shared library), so it loads with no environment variables;
  • for every other target that loads the library at run time, it prints the {PREFIX}_LIBRARY=<path> to set;
  • for the targets that link the library when they’re built (C, C++, Swift, and Go), it prints the directory to link with (-L<dir> -l{library}) and to put on the loader path;
  • WebAssembly needs a wasm32 build, which --dev doesn’t make (weaveffi build --platforms wasm32 does).

--dev needs a Rust producer (or --library), and can’t be combined with --check, --diff, or --dry-run. Rerun it after changing the crate: copies don’t follow later builds. Each target’s page says where its package looks for the library.

Generating from a build script

The CLI is also a library. weaveffi_cli::project::Project locates a project the way the weaveffi command does, and the weaveffi_cli::codegen::Orchestrator generates it, so a crate that ships bindings for an IDL-defined library can generate them from its build.rs:

// build.rs
use weaveffi_cli::codegen::Orchestrator;
use weaveffi_cli::project::Project;

fn main() -> miette::Result<()> {
    println!("cargo::rerun-if-changed=weaveffi.toml");
    println!("cargo::rerun-if-changed=api.yml");
    let project = Project::discover(env!("CARGO_MANIFEST_DIR"))?;
    let targets = project.config.targets(None)?;
    Orchestrator::new()
        .with_targets(targets.iter().map(AsRef::as_ref))
        .run(&project.model()?, &project.config.out_dir(None))?;
    Ok(())
}
[build-dependencies]
weaveffi-cli = "0.25"
miette = "7"

Project::discover finds the nearest weaveffi.toml at or above the directory, and the orchestrator writes the [project] targets into [project] out, rewriting only changed files.

A build script runs before its crate is compiled, so it can’t read its own crate’s library: from a build.rs, the project’s input must be an IDL, or the API must come from a library built earlier, named with Project::library(path). Pointing a crate’s own build.rs at the crate is an error that says so. For a Rust producer, generate after the build instead: weaveffi generate in CI or a cargo xtask.

Type mapping

RustIDL
i8..i64, u8..u64, f32, f64, boolsame
usize, isizeu64, i64
charstring
String, &strstring
Vec<u8>, &[u8]bytes
Vec<T>, &[T][T]
Option<T>, Option<&T>T?
HashMap<K, V>, BTreeMap<K, V>{K:V}
weaveffi::Iter<T>iter<T>
&T, Arc<T> (interface), Self, Arc<Self>T
Arc<dyn Trait> (callback interface)Trait
weaveffi::CancelToken (an async fn’s last parameter)removed; sets cancellable: true
Result<T, E>, E a #[weaveffi::error] domainreturn T, throws: E
Result<T, E>, E an untyped errorreturn T, throws: any
a #[weaveffi::custom] typeits repr
a type aliasits target
a tagged record, enum, or interfaceits name

Compositions map recursively: Option<Vec<i32>> is [i32]? and Vec<Arc<Book>> is [Book]. An async fn sets async: true, and () and Result<(), E> returns are no return. A callback method’s Result<T, E> follows the same rule as a function’s: a domain E throws that domain, and an untyped one (ForeignError included) throws any. A type is named in the IDL by its declaration’s name alone, wherever in the crate it’s declared.

Limits

  • Error messages. A #[weaveffi::error] variant’s doc comment becomes the code’s doc:, and the code’s message: is the variant’s #[weaveffi(message = "...")] template as written, else its doc comment’s first line, else the variant name. The runtime message is the generated Display (the template filled from the fields), which the IDL can’t express.
  • Deprecation versions. #[deprecated(since = "...")] keeps only the note; the IDL has no since field.
  • Parameter docs. Rust doesn’t allow doc comments on function parameters, so an extracted API has no parameter doc; describe parameters in the function’s own doc comment.

The CLI’s tests build the fixture producer in crates/weaveffi-cli/tests/fixtures/producer, check that weaveffi extract prints exactly its expected.yml, and check that generating from the library and from that IDL gives identical output.

IDL Schema

An IDL document describes one library’s API: its records, enums, error domains, interfaces, callback interfaces, and functions. YAML and JSON are accepted (TOML is only for weaveffi.toml, never for an IDL); this page uses YAML, read as YAML 1.2, so a name YAML reads as null (null, ~) has to be quoted (name: "null"). It documents schema version 0.13.0, the only version the current tools accept. A Rust producer never writes one by hand (the attributes embed the API in the built library, and weaveffi extract prints it as an IDL), but the schema is the same either way.

The YAML on this page is included from the documents in examples/idl, which a test loads and validates the way weaveffi validate does. A block whose first line is # rejected: is a document the validator refuses, with the error it reports; the test checks that too.

Editor support

The CLI prints a JSON Schema for the document with weaveffi schema, and a copy is checked in as weaveffi.schema.json and published at https://weaveffi.com/weaveffi.schema.json. Editors using the YAML Language Server pick it up from a header comment:

# yaml-language-server: $schema=https://weaveffi.com/weaveffi.schema.json

Document structure

A library is one namespace, so a document is flat: every declaration sits at the top level, in a list by kind.

version: "0.13.0"
doc: An in-memory address book.
KeyTypeMeaning
versionstring (required)Must be "0.13.0"
docstringDocumentation for the library as a whole
importsarray of pathsOther IDL files whose declarations join this library (see Multi-file IDLs)
recordsarray of RecordPlain data types with named fields
enumsarray of EnumC-style or rich enums
errorsarray of ErrorDomainError domains
interfacesarray of InterfaceReference-counted object types
callbacksarray of CallbackInterfaceMethod sets the consumer implements
functionsarray of FunctionFree functions

The document describes only the API. Package identity and generator options live in weaveffi.toml. Every object rejects unknown keys, so a misspelled field fails to parse instead of being ignored.

Function

The same shape describes free functions, interface constructors, methods, and statics, and callback-interface methods, with the restrictions noted in their sections.

FieldTypeMeaning
namestring (required)Function name
paramsarray of ParamOrdered parameters (default none)
returnTypeRefReturn type; omit for none
docstringDocumentation
throwsstringThe error domain the callable reports (BookError), or any for an untyped error; omit when it can’t fail (see Error domains)
asyncboolComplete asynchronously (default false)
cancellableboolAccept a cancel token; requires async: true
deprecatedstringDeprecation message propagated to every binding

A Param has a required name and type and an optional doc. A parameter may not be named self, ctx, out_err, callback, context, or cancel_token, the slots the C ABI adds (SlotCollision).

Types

A TypeRef is a string:

SyntaxMeaning
i8 i16 i32 i64 u8 u16 u32 u64Fixed-width integers
f32 f64IEEE 754 floats
boolBoolean
stringUTF-8 text
bytesByte string ([u8] is accepted and becomes bytes)
NameA record, enum, interface, or callback interface, by name
T?Optional
[T]List
{K:V}Map
iter<T>Lazy iterator (returns only)

usize, isize, u128, i128, and char aren’t primitives; a reference to one is reported as UnsupportedPrimitive (use u64 or i64 for sizes, and string for characters). A reference to a name nothing declares is UnknownTypeRef.

The parser reads outside in: [Contact?] is a list of optional contacts and [Contact]? an optional list. Composites nest to any depth. Quote anything YAML would otherwise interpret ("string?", "[i32]", "{string:i32}").

How a type is spelled never depends on how it crosses the ABI. At a call boundary every value is one C value: a scalar or C-style enum by value, an optional one (i32?, Kind?) as a {p}_opt_* struct, an interface as a pointer, and everything else as a {p}_run holding a string’s UTF-8, raw bytes, a typed array (a list of any integer or float but u8), or a value buffer. Every binding surfaces them as its ordinary optional and list types; inside a record, variant, or error payload every type uses the value-buffer encoding.

Map keys must be an integer, bool, string, or a C-style enum, so every target can use its native dictionary with exact equality. Floats, bytes, composites, and objects are rejected:

# rejected: InvalidMapKey
version: "0.13.0"
functions:
  - name: by_weight
    params:
      - { name: index, type: "{f64:i32}" }

Iterators are lazy: every binding pulls one element per step of its native iteration idiom and destroys the iterator exactly once. iter<T> is valid only as the outermost return of a synchronous callable. It’s rejected in a parameter, a field, or nested in another type (IteratorInInvalidPosition), and as the return of an async function (AsyncIteratorReturn):

# rejected: IteratorInInvalidPosition
version: "0.13.0"
functions:
  - name: consume
    params:
      - { name: items, type: "iter<i32>" }

Type positions

“Buffered field” means a record field, rich-enum variant field, or error payload field.

TypeParamReturnBuffered fieldInside T? [T] {K:V}Map keyCallback paramCallback return
integers, floats, boolyesyesyesyesintegers, boolyesyes
stringyesyesyesyesyesyesyes
bytesyesyesyesyesnoyesyes
C-style enumyesyesyesyesyesyesyes
record, rich enumyesyesyesyesnoyesyes
interfaceyesyesyes, except in an error payloadyesnoyesyes
callback interfaceyes, bare or Cb?nononononono
T?, [T], {K:V}yesyesyesyesnoyesyes
iter<T>noyes (sync)nonononono

A record or rich enum may not contain itself by value, directly or through other records and variants (RecursiveType); a list, map, or optional of it is fine.

Records

A record is a value type with named fields, crossing the ABI by value as a value buffer. Bindings map it to a data class, struct, or record type; no per-record C functions exist.

FieldTypeMeaning
namestring (required)Type name
docstringDocumentation
deprecatedstringDeprecation message
fieldsarray of Field (required, non-empty)Each with name, type, optional doc
 
records:
  - name: Contact
    doc: One contact.
    fields:
      - { name: id, type: u64, doc: The book-assigned id. }
      - { name: name, type: string }
      - { name: email, type: "string?" }
      - { name: kind, type: Kind }
      - { name: tags, type: "[string]" }
      - { name: notes, type: "{string:string}" }

Enums

An enum is a fixed set of variants.

FieldTypeMeaning
namestring (required)Type name
doc, deprecatedstringDocumentation, deprecation message
variantsarray of Variant (required, non-empty)Each with name, optional value, doc, and fields

A variant’s value is its i32 discriminant; without one it’s one more than the previous variant’s (0 for the first), as in Rust and C, so Kind below is Personal = 0, Work = 10, Other = 11. When no variant has fields, the enum is C-style: it crosses as an int32_t and may be a map key. When any variant has fields, the enum is rich (a sum type): it crosses as a value buffer holding the variant’s value as a tag followed by its fields, and bindings map it to a Swift enum with associated values, a sealed class hierarchy, a tagged union, and so on. Unit and data variants may mix.

enums:
  - name: Kind
    doc: How a contact is filed.
    variants:
      - { name: Personal }
      - { name: Work, value: 10 }
      - { name: Other }
  - name: Change
    doc: A change to a book.
    variants:
      - name: Added
        fields:
          - { name: contact, type: Contact }
      - name: Removed
        fields:
          - { name: id, type: u64 }
      - { name: Cleared }

Variant names and values are unique within an enum, and a variant’s field names are unique within the variant.

Error domains

An error domain is a named set of stable codes that callables report. Each binding generates a typed error (an exception hierarchy, a Swift Error enum, a Go error type) so consumers match on the names you declared. A library may declare any number of domains.

FieldTypeMeaning
namestring (required)Domain type name (BookError)
docstringDocumentation
codesarray of Code (required)The codes

Each code has a required name (PascalCase by convention), positive code, and default message, plus an optional doc and optional fields: a structured payload with the same shape as record fields, delivered as properties of the raised error.

errors:
  - name: BookError
    doc: The address book's errors.
    codes:
      - name: NotFound
        code: 1
        message: contact not found
        fields:
          - { name: id, type: u64 }
      - name: Full
        code: 2
        message: the book is full
        fields:
          - { name: capacity, type: u32 }
      - { name: EmptyName, code: 3, message: a contact needs a name }
      - name: Rejected
        code: 4
        message: a listener rejected the contact
        fields:
          - { name: name, type: string }

A function, constructor, method, static, or callback method says how it can fail with throws:

  • throws: {Domain} names the one domain it reports (Book.add below).
  • throws: any reports an untyped error: a message, with no code a consumer can match on (parse_number below). Bindings surface it as the library’s base error type. A Rust producer’s Result<T, E> is throws: any when E is an untyped error (weaveffi::Error, String, std::io::Error, …).
  • No throws (count) means a plain signature that can’t report an error; a failure there is a producer bug and traps (see Errors and Memory).

A throws that names something other than an error domain is rejected:

# rejected: UnknownErrorDomain
version: "0.13.0"
records:
  - name: Contact
    fields:
      - { name: name, type: string }
functions:
  - name: get_contact
    params:
      - { name: id, type: u64 }
    return: Contact
    throws: Contact

Domains are open. Each code has its own contract function, so a producer may add codes to a domain without breaking a deployed binding. A binding that receives a positive code it wasn’t generated with reports it as the domain’s base error type with the code and message preserved, and discards its payload undecoded. That’s why payload fields are plain data: a field may not reach an interface, directly or through a record or rich enum:

# rejected: ObjectInErrorPayload
version: "0.13.0"
interfaces:
  - name: Store
    methods:
      - { name: count, return: u64 }
errors:
  - name: StoreError
    codes:
      - name: Locked
        code: 1
        message: the store is locked
        fields:
          - { name: holder, type: Store }

Codes must be positive (0 is success and negative codes belong to the runtime) and unique within their domain (DuplicateErrorCode). Code names must be unique across every domain in the library (DuplicateErrorCodeName), because several targets flatten them into one namespace; qualify one (OrderNotFound) when two domains need the same idea. A domain name is a declaration name like any other, but it isn’t a value type: type: BookError is ErrorDomainAsType. any is reserved and can’t name a domain.

Interfaces

An interface is an object type: state with identity and behavior that lives in the producer and crosses the ABI as an opaque, reference-counted pointer. Consumers see a class whose constructors, methods, and statics call into the producer.

FieldTypeMeaning
namestring (required)Type name
doc, deprecatedstringDocumentation, deprecation message
constructorsarray of FunctionReturn a new instance; no return, never async
methodsarray of FunctionTake the instance implicitly
staticsarray of FunctionNo instance
 
interfaces:
  - name: Book
    doc: An address book.
    constructors:
      - name: new
        params:
          - { name: capacity, type: u32 }
    methods:
      - name: add
        params:
          - { name: name, type: string }
          - { name: email, type: "string?" }
          - { name: kind, type: Kind }
        return: Contact
        throws: BookError
      - name: get
        params:
          - { name: id, type: u64 }
        return: Contact
        throws: BookError
      - name: remove
        params:
          - { name: id, type: u64 }
        return: bool
      - { name: count, return: u64 }
      - { name: size, return: u32, deprecated: use count }
      - name: names
        doc: Every contact's name, pulled lazily.
        return: "iter<string>"
      - name: subscribe
        params:
          - { name: listener, type: Listener }
      - name: export_all
        return: "[Contact]"
        async: true
      - { name: merged_with, params: [{ name: other, type: "Book?" }], return: Book }
    statics:
      - { name: default_capacity, return: u32 }

An interface needs at least one member, and constructor, method, and static names share one namespace. A constructor named new becomes the canonical constructor where the target has one (Swift init, Python __init__); others become static factories. For an async factory, declare an async static that returns the interface.

An interface may appear in every position except a map key and an error payload: parameters, returns, optionals, lists, map values, record and variant fields, iterator elements, async results, and callback parameters and returns. Book? at the top level of a parameter or return is a nullable pointer. Every position moves or borrows references by the rules in Errors and Memory.

An interface lowers to an opaque type, one function per member (methods take a leading self), and implicit _clone and _destroy functions you never declare. From the document above (prefix contacts):

typedef struct contacts_Book contacts_Book;
contacts_Book* contacts_Book_new(uint32_t capacity, contacts_error* out_err);
contacts_run contacts_Book_get(const contacts_Book* self, uint64_t id, contacts_error* out_err);
contacts_Book* contacts_Book_merged_with(const contacts_Book* self, const contacts_Book* other,
                                         contacts_error* out_err);
contacts_Book* contacts_Book_clone(const contacts_Book* self);
void contacts_Book_destroy(contacts_Book* self);

Callback interfaces

A callback interface is the inverse of an interface: methods the consumer implements and the producer calls, any number of times and from any thread, for as long as it holds the implementation.

FieldTypeMeaning
namestring (required)Type name
doc, deprecatedstringDocumentation, deprecation message
methodsarray of Function (required, non-empty)In vtable order
 
callbacks:
  - name: Listener
    doc: Approves the contacts a book adds and hears about its changes.
    methods:
      - name: approve
        params:
          - { name: contact, type: Contact }
        return: bool
        throws: BookError
      - name: on_change
        params:
          - { name: change, type: Change }
        throws: any

Methods are restricted:

  • synchronous: no async, no cancellable (InvalidCallbackMethod);
  • they return nothing or any type except an iterator (InvalidCallbackMethod) or a callback interface (CallbackInterfaceInInvalidPosition); a string, bytes, typed-array, or buffered return is a run the consumer allocates with {p}_alloc and the producer adopts, and an object return moves one reference to the producer;
  • parameters may be any type except an iterator (IteratorInInvalidPosition) or a callback interface (CallbackInterfaceInInvalidPosition); an object parameter moves one reference to the consumer.

A method may declare throws like any callable. With throws: BookError its consumer may fail with one of the domain’s codes, with the code’s fields, and the producer receives the typed error; with throws: any a consumer failure reaches the producer as an untyped error with its message. Every failure of a method without throws, and a code its domain doesn’t declare, reaches the producer as the runtime code -4. Each callback method has its own contract function, and vtables are open, so appending a method to a callback interface doesn’t break a deployed binding; reordering methods or inserting one before others moves vtable slots and does.

A callback interface is valid only as a parameter of a function, constructor, method, or static, passed bare (Listener) or optional (Listener?, where none is a null vtable). Anything else is CallbackInterfaceInInvalidPosition:

# rejected: CallbackInterfaceInInvalidPosition
version: "0.13.0"
callbacks:
  - name: Listener
    methods:
      - { name: on_event, params: [{ name: id, type: i64 }] }
functions:
  - name: current_listener
    return: Listener

A callback interface lowers to an argument struct and a vtable type that starts with a header (the vtable’s size, its flags, and the free release hook) followed by one entry per method in declaration order:

typedef struct contacts_Listener { void* ctx; const contacts_Listener_vtable* vtable; } contacts_Listener;
struct contacts_Listener_vtable {
    uint32_t size;
    uint32_t flags;
    void (*free)(void* ctx);
    bool (*approve)(void* ctx, contacts_run contact, contacts_error* out_err);
    void (*on_change)(void* ctx, contacts_run change, contacts_error* out_err);
};
void contacts_Book_subscribe(const contacts_Book* self, contacts_Listener listener,
                             contacts_error* out_err);

Functions

Free functions use the Function shape:

functions:
  - name: greet_later
    doc: Greet `name` after `delay_ms` milliseconds.
    params:
      - { name: name, type: string }
      - { name: delay_ms, type: u64 }
    return: string
    async: true
    cancellable: true
  - name: word_count
    params:
      - { name: text, type: string }
    return: u64
  - name: mean
    params:
      - { name: values, type: "[f64]" }
    return: "f64?"
  - name: parse_number
    params:
      - { name: text, type: string }
    return: i64
    throws: any

async: true makes a function, method, or static complete through a callback, surfaced as each language’s async idiom, and cancellable: true adds a cancel token; both are covered in Async and Cancellation. Constructors can’t be async (AsyncConstructor), and cancellable requires async:

# rejected: CancellableNotAsync
version: "0.13.0"
functions:
  - { name: ping, return: bool, cancellable: true }

deprecated: on a function, method, static, record, enum, interface, or callback interface propagates its message to every binding’s native deprecation marker.

Multi-file IDLs

A large API can be split across files. A document’s top-level imports: lists other IDL files, as paths relative to the importing file, and every declaration of an imported file joins the importer’s one namespace. A declaration in any file may refer to one in any other, in either direction:

version: "0.13.0"
doc: Drawing with shapes, measured in units.
imports:
  - shapes.yml
records:
  - name: Canvas
    fields:
      - { name: width, type: f64 }
      - { name: shapes, type: "[Shape]" }
functions:
  - name: area
    params:
      - { name: canvas, type: Canvas }
      - { name: unit, type: Unit }
    return: f64
    throws: DrawError

drawing.yml uses Shape and throws DrawError from the file it imports:

version: "0.13.0"
imports:
  - units.yml
enums:
  - name: Shape
    variants:
      - name: Circle
        fields:
          - { name: radius, type: f64 }
      - name: Rect
        fields:
          - { name: width, type: f64 }
          - { name: height, type: f64 }
errors:
  - name: DrawError
    codes:
      - { name: NegativeSize, code: 1, message: a shape has a negative size }

which in turn imports Unit, which drawing.yml uses too:

version: "0.13.0"
enums:
  - name: Unit
    variants:
      - { name: Points }
      - { name: Millimeters }

The rules:

  • Every file declares the same version. The library’s doc and imports are the root document’s; an imported file’s doc is ignored.
  • Imports are recursive, and a file reached twice (here units.yml, if drawing.yml imported it too) is read once. An import chain that leads back to a file importing it is an error (import cycle).
  • Names are unique across all the files, exactly as in one document, and a diagnostic points into the file that declares the offending item.
  • weaveffi generate, validate, and generate --check read the root document with every file it imports, so the bindings, the contract, and the drift check cover the whole API.

Names

A library is one namespace. Record, enum, error domain, interface, callback interface, and free function names are unique across the whole library, whichever file declares them (DuplicateName):

# rejected: DuplicateName
version: "0.13.0"
records:
  - name: Stats
    fields:
      - { name: total, type: u64 }
functions:
  - name: Stats
    return: u64

Error code names are unique across every domain (DuplicateErrorCodeName). Members are scoped to their owner: parameters to their callable, fields to their record or variant, variants to their enum, constructors, methods, and statics to their interface, and methods to their callback interface.

Because a name identifies one declaration, a type reference is always the bare name. C symbols are the library’s prefix and the name: a function get_contact is {p}_get_contact, a record Contact is {p}_Contact, and a method get on Book is {p}_Book_get (see Naming and the C ABI contract).

Documentation comments

doc: is accepted on the library, functions, parameters, records, fields, enums, variants, error domains and codes, interfaces and their members, and callback interfaces and their methods. Every generator emits it in the target’s native doc-comment syntax (///, /** */, docstrings, XML docs); multi-line YAML block strings (|) are preserved. An absent doc: emits nothing.

Validation

weaveffi validate (and every generating command) runs every rule and reports every violation in one pass, each with a message, a suggestion, and a source span in the file that declares the offending item when one can be found. --format json emits the same diagnostics, each with a code field naming the error alongside its own fields; --warn adds advisory warnings.

The rules in brief:

  • Names are identifiers (a letter or _, then ASCII letters, digits, and _) and not one of if, else, for, while, loop, match, type, return, async, await, break, continue, fn, struct, enum, mod, or use (and no error domain is named any). Generators escape other languages’ keywords themselves.
  • Declaration names and error code names are unique across the library; every other name is unique in its scope (see Names).
  • Records, enums, interfaces, and callback interfaces aren’t empty, and no record or rich enum contains itself by value.
  • Types resolve and appear only in the positions in the table above.
  • throws names an error domain (or is any); cancellable needs async.
  • Codes are positive and their values unique within a domain.
  • No two declarations lower to the same C identifier. Every symbol is the prefix plus the declaration’s name (Owner_member for a member), so a free function Book_get collides with a get method on Book, a function named Book_clone with Book’s implicit _clone, and a function named foo_callback with the completion type of an async foo. Names in a family the runtime and the C value-buffer helpers reserve ({p}_contract_*, {p}_reader_*, {p}_writer_*, {p}_str_*, {p}_bytes_*, the {p}_buf_* codec names, and the runtime’s own symbols) collide too.
  • No parameter is named after a slot the C ABI adds (self, ctx, out_err, callback, context, cancel_token).
# rejected: SymbolCollision
version: "0.13.0"
interfaces:
  - name: Store
    methods:
      - { name: get, params: [{ name: key, type: string }], return: bytes }
functions:
  - name: Store_get
    params:
      - { name: key, type: string }
    return: bytes

A document with an unsupported version reports only UnsupportedSchemaVersion.

Error catalog

CodeReported when
UnsupportedSchemaVersionversion isn’t 0.13.0
InvalidIdentifiera name isn’t a valid identifier
ReservedKeyworda name is a reserved word, or an error domain is named any
DuplicateNametwo declarations of the library share a name
DuplicateErrorCodeNametwo codes anywhere in the library share a name
SymbolCollisiontwo declarations lower to the same C identifier, or one falls in a reserved family
SlotCollisiona parameter has the name of a slot the C ABI adds
DuplicateParamNametwo parameters of a callable share a name
CancellableNotAsynca synchronous callable is cancellable
UnknownErrorDomainthrows names something that isn’t an error domain
AsyncIteratorReturnan async function returns iter<T>
DuplicateErrorCodetwo codes in one domain share a value
InvalidErrorCodea code is zero or negative
EmptyRecorda record has no fields
DuplicateRecordFieldtwo fields of a record share a name
RecursiveTypea record or rich enum contains itself by value
EmptyEnuman enum has no variants
DuplicateEnumVarianttwo variants of an enum share a name
DuplicateEnumVariantFieldtwo fields of a rich-enum variant share a name
DuplicateEnumValuetwo variants of an enum share a value
EmptyInterfacean interface has no members
DuplicateInterfaceMembertwo members of an interface share a name
ConstructorHasReturna constructor declares return
AsyncConstructora constructor is async
EmptyCallbackInterfacea callback interface has no methods
DuplicateCallbackMethodtwo methods of a callback interface share a name
InvalidCallbackMethoda callback method is async or cancellable, or returns an iterator
UnknownTypeRefa reference names no declaration
ErrorDomainAsTypean error domain is used as a value type
UnsupportedPrimitivea reference names usize, isize, u128, i128, or char
InvalidMapKeya map key isn’t an integer, bool, string, or C-style enum
InterfaceInInvalidPositionan interface is used as a map key
CallbackInterfaceInInvalidPositiona callback interface appears other than as a parameter (bare or Cb?)
ObjectInErrorPayloadan error code’s field reaches an interface, directly or through a record or rich enum
IteratorInInvalidPositioniter<T> appears other than as an outermost return

A file that can’t be read, has no IDL extension, fails to parse, declares another schema version than the root, or closes an import cycle is reported before validation, with the file’s name.

Warnings

Warnings never fail validation; --warn reports them.

CodeFires when
Undocumenteda top-level declaration has no doc:, so its bindings have no doc comment
DeepNestinga type nests composites more than three levels deep
LargeEnumVariantCountan enum has more than 100 variants

C ABI Contract

This page is the normative description of the WeaveFFI C ABI, revision 6. The producer macros implement it, the C generator renders it as a header, and every other generator consumes it. Where another page disagrees with this one, this one wins.

Vocabulary

  • Producer: the native library implementing the symbols (a Rust cdylib built with the producer macros, or a C, C++, or Zig library implementing the header).
  • Consumer: generated bindings, or C code calling the header directly.
  • {p}: the library’s C symbol prefix from its identity, a lower-snake identifier (kvstore). {P} is the same prefix uppercased, used for macros (KVSTORE).
  • Run: a contiguous allocation of bytes that crosses the boundary (a string, bytes, a typed array, a value buffer, an error message or payload).

A library is one namespace: every record, enum, error domain, interface, callback interface, and free function has a name unique across the library, and every identifier the ABI defines starts with {p}_ (functions, types, constants) or {P}_ (macros). There are no fixed weaveffi_* symbols, so any number of WeaveFFI libraries can link into one process, statically or dynamically.

One value, one C value

Every value crosses as exactly one C value, in every position: a parameter, a return, an async result, an iterator item, and a callback method’s parameter and return. Which C value is decided by the value’s family:

FamilyIDL typesC type
Directintegers, f32, f64, bool, C-style enumsthe scalar (int32_t, double, bool, …); an enum’s {p}_{E} is typedef int32_t
OptDirectT? where T is Direct{p}_opt_{prim}: struct { bool has; T value; }; an optional enum is a {p}_opt_i32
Runstring, bytes, [i8] … [f64] (typed arrays), records, rich enums, and every other optional, list, and map{p}_run: struct { const uint8_t* ptr; size_t len; }
Objectinterfaces and Interface?const {p}_{I}* as a callable’s parameter, {p}_{I}* elsewhere

A run holds, by type:

  • string: UTF-8, never NUL-terminated;
  • bytes: raw bytes;
  • [i8], [i16], [i32], [i64], [u16], [u32], [u64], [f32], [f64] (a typed array): the packed native elements, len a multiple of the element size and ptr aligned for the element ([bool] and [u8] aren’t typed arrays: [u8] is bytes, and [bool] is a value buffer);
  • anything else: a value buffer.

len always counts bytes. Null with length 0 is the empty run, and a null pointer with a non-zero length is malformed.

A bool (and an OptDirect’s has) is a C bool, one byte; the producer rejects any byte but 0 and 1 as a marshalling failure (-3). An OptDirect’s value is 0 when has is false.

Two more kinds aren’t value types:

  • A callback-interface parameter (Cb or Cb?, legal only as a parameter of a function or interface member) is a {p}_{Cb} struct, struct { void* ctx; const {p}_{Cb}_vtable* vtable; }, passed by value; a null vtable is none (Cb?).
  • An iterator return (iter<T>, legal only as a return) is an opaque {p}_{Iter}* (see Iterators).

Signatures

A synchronous callable is

R {symbol}([const {p}_{I}* self,] <one slot per parameter>, {p}_error* out_err);

where R is the return’s C type (void for none), the receiver self is an instance method’s, and each parameter’s slot is named after the parameter. A parameter may not be named self, ctx, out_err, callback, context, or cancel_token (validation reports SlotCollision).

DeclarationC identifier
function f{p}_f
interface Itype {p}_I; members {p}_I_{member}; {p}_I_clone, {p}_I_destroy
async function flauncher {p}_f (no suffix); completion type {p}_f_callback
iterator returned by ftype {p}_{Owner_}{PascalF}Iterator, plus {Iter}_next and {Iter}_destroy
C-style enum E, variant Vtype {p}_E (typedef int32_t), constant {p}_E_V
rich enum E, variant Vtag type {p}_E_Tag (typedef int32_t), constant {p}_E_V; struct {p}_E and its codecs in the buffer header
record Sstruct {p}_S and its codecs in the buffer header
error domain D, code Ctype {p}_D (typedef int32_t), constant {p}_D_C; for a code with fields, struct {p}_D_C_payload and its codecs in the buffer header
callback interface Cbargument struct {p}_Cb, vtable type {p}_Cb_vtable
a declaration’s contract{p}_contract_{id:016x} (see The contract)

{Owner_} is empty for a free function and the interface name plus _ for a member, and the function name is converted to PascalCase: stream_items is kitchen_sink_StreamItemsIterator, and Store.keys is kvstore_Store_KeysIterator.

Enum, tag, and error-code types are typedef int32_t with their constants in an anonymous enum, never typedef enum: a C enum’s size is the compiler’s choice, and these values always cross as int32_t.

typedef int32_t kvstore_Kind;
enum { kvstore_Kind_Hot = 0, kvstore_Kind_Cold = 1 };

All names are computed once by the model and recorded in one symbol table; validation fails with SymbolCollision if two declarations would produce the same identifier, or one falls in a reserved family ({p}_contract_*, and the buffer header’s {p}_reader_*, {p}_writer_*, {p}_str_*, and {p}_bytes_*). The buffer header names its optional, list, and map codecs {p}_buf_{stem} ({p}_buf_list_i32); those exact names are reserved too. IDL spellings are never re-cased in C.

Load-time checks

A consumer makes two checks once, when it loads the library, before any other call, and refuses to load if either fails (with the language’s load or import error, or a catchable check function where loading itself can’t fail, as in Swift and Go).

The revision. {P}_ABI_VERSION in the header and {p}_abi_version() in the library must be equal. The revision changes only when the runtime surface, the value-buffer encoding, or a calling convention changes incompatibly; it’s independent of the crate version and the IDL schema version.

Revision 6 is designed to grow without a revision 7: new declarations, new callback methods (appended), and new error codes are additive.

The contract

Every declaration has a contract function:

uint64_t {p}_contract_{id:016x}(void);  /* returns the declaration's hash */

A declaration is a function, an interface, each interface member (constructor, method, or static), a record, an enum, a callback interface, each callback method, an error domain, or each error code. Its

  • id is the 64-bit FNV-1a hash of its dotted path, the name then a member, method, or code (open_store, Store, Store.get, Listener, Listener.on_put, KvError, KvError.NotFound), written as 16 lower-case hex digits in the symbol;
  • hash is the 64-bit FNV-1a hash of its canonical signature string.

FNV-1a 64 starts from 0xcbf29ce484222325 and, for each byte, XORs the byte in and multiplies by 0x100000001b3, wrapping.

The canonical signature is computed from resolved types, never from source spelling: type aliases are substituted, a custom type is its repr, [u8] is bytes, and each type is written in its IDL spelling (i32, string, [Contact], {string:i64}, Store?, Listener?, iter<Item>). Parameter names, field names, documentation, deprecation text, and error messages are excluded.

DeclarationCanonical signature
function or interface member{kind} {name}({type}, ...) -> {type}, where kind is function, constructor, method, or static and a missing return is void, followed by throws {Domain} or throws any, then async, then cancellable, each when set (a constructor’s return is its interface)
interfaceinterface {name} (each member has its own entry)
recordrecord {name} {{type}, ...}: field types only, in order
enumenum {name} {{Variant} = {value}, ...}, a variant with fields followed by {{type}, ...}
callback interfacecallback {name} (each method has its own entry)
callback methodcallback_method #{slot} {name}({type}, ...) -> {type}, where slot is the method’s zero-based position in the vtable and a missing return is void, followed by throws {Domain} or throws any when set
error domainerrors {name} (each code has its own entry)
error codecode {Code} = {value}, a code with fields followed by {{type}, ...}

For example, fn get(&self, key: String) -> Result<Entry, KvError> on interface Store has the path Store.get and the signature method get(string) -> Entry throws KvError.

A consumer checks every declaration it was generated with: it calls the declaration’s contract function and compares the result with the hash it was generated with. A missing function fails with “Store.get is missing from the library”; a different hash fails with “Store.get changed since these bindings were generated”. Declarations the library has and the consumer doesn’t are fine, so adding a declaration (a function, a method, a record, an error code, or a callback method at the end of its interface) never breaks a deployed binding. A consumer that links the library (C, C++, Swift, Go) calls the functions directly, so a missing declaration fails to link; one that loads it at run time resolves each function by name.

Variant and code names stay in their signatures because they’re values a consumer sees; parameter and field names don’t affect the encoding, so renaming one is ABI-neutral. The flip side is that swapping two parameters or fields of the same type passes the check too; treat that as a breaking change even though the check can’t see it.

The header declares every contract function, commented with its path and canonical signature, a checker, and a macro a hand-written producer defines them all with:

KVSTORE_API uint64_t kvstore_contract_dc5ad183936cb6fc(void); /* Store.get: method get(string) -> Entry throws KvError */
static inline const char* kvstore_contract_check(void);  /* the path of the first mismatch, or NULL */
#define KVSTORE_DEFINE_CONTRACT ...                       /* defines every contract function */

Runtime surface

Every producer exports these symbols (weaveffi::export_runtime!() emits them for a Rust producer):

#define {P}_ABI_VERSION 6u
uint32_t {p}_abi_version(void);

typedef struct {p}_run { const uint8_t* ptr; size_t len; } {p}_run;
typedef struct {p}_opt_i32 { bool has; int32_t value; } {p}_opt_i32;  /* and one per scalar */

typedef struct {p}_error {
    int32_t code;                /* 0 ok; >0 domain code; <0 runtime code */
    const uint8_t* message_ptr;  /* UTF-8, NOT NUL-terminated, producer-owned */
    size_t message_len;
    const uint8_t* payload_ptr;  /* the code's fields as a value buffer, or NULL */
    size_t payload_len;
} {p}_error;
void {p}_error_set({p}_error* err, int32_t code,
                   const uint8_t* message_ptr, size_t message_len);          /* copies */
void {p}_error_set_payload({p}_error* err, const uint8_t* ptr, size_t len);  /* copies */
void {p}_error_clear({p}_error* err);  /* frees message and payload; code = 0 */
void {p}_error_free({p}_error* err);   /* clear, then free a heap-boxed error */

uint8_t* {p}_alloc(size_t len);      /* a zero-filled 8-aligned run, NULL for 0 */
void {p}_run_free({p}_run run);      /* returned runs and {p}_alloc runs */

typedef struct {p}_cancel_token {p}_cancel_token;
{p}_cancel_token* {p}_cancel_token_create(void);           /* refcount 1 */
void {p}_cancel_token_cancel({p}_cancel_token* token);      /* idempotent */
bool {p}_cancel_token_is_cancelled(const {p}_cancel_token* token);
void {p}_cancel_token_destroy({p}_cancel_token* token);     /* drops one reference */

#define {P}_VTABLE_THREAD_AFFINE 1u  /* a callback vtable flag; see below */

uint64_t {p}_debug_live(int32_t kind);  /* leak counters */

There is one {p}_opt_{prim} per scalar: bool, i8, i16, i32, i64, u8, u16, u32, u64, f32, and f64.

Every function accepts null where a pointer is optional (error_set, error_set_payload, error_clear, error_free, the token functions, _clone, _destroy) and treats it as a no-op; a null token reads as never cancelled. {p}_run_free of the empty run is a no-op. {p}_error_set with a null message_ptr (whatever the length) sets an empty message, and a producer never fails on message bytes that aren’t UTF-8 (the Rust runtime replaces them with U+FFFD), so reporting a failure never fails.

Every run the producer hands out (a string, bytes, typed-array, or value buffer return; an error’s message and payload) and every {p}_alloc(len) run is allocated with alignment 8, so a run can be read in place as any element type. The consumer releases a returned run with {p}_run_free, passing it back exactly as it got it; it never frees a run with its own allocator. A consumer allocates a run with {p}_alloc(len) when it hands memory to the producer (a callback method’s string, bytes, typed-array, or buffer return, which the producer adopts) or, on wasm32, when it stages an argument in the module’s linear memory. An error’s message and payload belong to the error struct and are released by {p}_error_clear or {p}_error_free, never by {p}_run_free.

{p}_debug_live(kind) reports live resources for leak checks: 0 objects, 1 foreign callbacks, 2 iterators, 3 cancel tokens, 4 runs (returned and allocated ones not yet freed or adopted). Kind -1 returns 1 when the producer counts at all and 0 when it doesn’t, so a harness can tell “nothing is live” from “nothing is counted”; an unknown kind returns 0. A Rust producer counts only with the leak-check feature; without it every kind but -1 returns 0.

Errors

Every synchronous API symbol (a function or interface member, an iterator launcher, and an iterator’s _next) takes a trailing {p}_error* out_err after all inputs; _clone, _destroy, an iterator’s _destroy, async launchers, and the runtime surface don’t. The call overwrites the slot without reading it: on failure it stores the code, message, and payload and returns a zero value (0, false, NULL, the empty run, an absent optional) the caller must not use; on success it stores a cleared error. So the caller may pass uninitialized memory, and after reading a failure releases its message and payload with {p}_error_clear (a struct reused for many calls must be cleared after each failure, or its message leaks).

The message is message_len bytes of UTF-8 at message_ptr, without a NUL terminator (a consumer that needs a C string copies it). An empty message is a null message_ptr with length 0, even on failure.

CodeMeaning
0success; every pointer is null and every length 0
> 0a code of the error domain the callable throws; payload_ptr/payload_len hold the code’s fields, if any
-1untyped: the failure of a throws: any callable, or an async call the executor couldn’t start
-2the producer panicked
-3marshalling failure: an argument couldn’t be lifted (a null run with a non-zero length, a null self or object where one is required, invalid UTF-8, a bool byte other than 0 or 1, an out-of-range enum value or integer conversion, a misaligned or ragged typed array, a malformed buffer, a map with a repeated key, an incomplete vtable header, an iterator advanced concurrently)
-4a consumer’s callback-interface implementation failed, doesn’t implement the method called, or was called off its thread (see Callback interfaces)
-5cancelled (async completions only)

What a callable may report depends on its throws:

  • No throws. Only runtime codes. A non-zero code is a producer bug or a failed callback; consumers trap on it rather than report a typed error (see Errors and memory).
  • throws: {Domain}. The domain’s positive codes plus runtime codes. Domains are open: a consumer that receives a positive code it wasn’t generated with (the producer gained a code since) maps it to the domain’s base error type with the code and message preserved, never to a crash or a generic failure. It discards that code’s payload without decoding it, which is why an error code’s fields never carry an object (validation reports ObjectInErrorPayload).
  • throws: any. Code -1 with a message, plus runtime codes; never a positive code.

A producer lifts every argument of a call before it reports the first one that failed, so the inputs it adopts (a callback argument, the object tokens in a value buffer) are adopted and released even when another argument, or a null self, fails.

Objects

Objects are reference counted by the producer. A {p}_{I}* is one strong reference. _clone returns another strong reference to the same object (the pointer value is unchanged); _destroy releases one, and the object is dropped with the last reference. A consumer may hold any number of references and use them from any thread.

PositionRule
parameter (const {p}_{I}*)borrowed for the call; the producer takes its own reference if it retains the object
return, async result, iterator elementone strong reference moves to the consumer
callback method argumentone strong reference moves to the consumer
callback method returnone strong reference moves to the producer
object token inside a value buffercarries one strong reference; the reader adopts it

A consumer encoding an object into a buffer writes a fresh reference from _clone. An in-flight async call holds its own reference, so releasing a wrapper while a call is pending is safe.

Callback interfaces

typedef struct {p}_{Cb}_vtable {p}_{Cb}_vtable;
typedef struct {p}_{Cb} { void* ctx; const {p}_{Cb}_vtable* vtable; } {p}_{Cb};

struct {p}_{Cb}_vtable {
    uint32_t size;            /* sizeof the vtable, as the consumer compiled it */
    uint32_t flags;           /* 0, or {P}_VTABLE_THREAD_AFFINE */
    void (*free)(void* ctx);  /* always first, at a fixed offset */
    /* one entry per method, in declaration order: */
    R (*{method})(void* ctx, <one slot per parameter>, {p}_error* out_err);
};

A callback interface has no exported symbols; it lowers to an argument struct and a vtable type. The consumer passes a ctx it owns and a pointer to a vtable that outlives the producer’s use of it (in practice, one static vtable per interface), with size set to sizeof the vtable.

Vtables are open. The producer accepts any vtable whose header is complete (size at least the header’s), so a consumer generated before a method was appended keeps working: when the producer calls a method that lies past the consumer’s size, or whose entry is null, the call fails with -4 and a message naming the method, without calling anything. A vtable shorter than its header fails the call that passed it with -3. Methods are only ever appended; reordering or inserting one moves slots and fails the contract check.

The producer may call any entry, any number of times, from any thread, until it calls free(ctx) exactly once, after which it never touches ctx again. free may also run on any producer thread, and a null free is skipped.

Thread affinity. Bit 0 of flags is {P}_VTABLE_THREAD_AFFINE (1u); every other bit is reserved and 0. With the flag set, a method that returns a value may only be called on the thread that passed the vtable to the producer. The producer records that thread when it adopts the vtable and fails such a call from any other thread with -4 and the message callback called off its thread, without calling the method. void methods and free may still be called from any thread. The Dart target sets the flag; every other target passes 0.

Arguments are lent for the duration of the call (runs are borrowed), except objects, which move one strong reference to the consumer.

Returns are the C return of the method. A run the consumer returns is one it allocated with {p}_alloc(len), which the producer adopts and frees; an object it returns is a fresh strong reference (_clone) the producer adopts. The producer adopts whatever the method returned whether or not it failed, so a consumer that fails after allocating doesn’t leak. A return it can’t accept (a string that isn’t UTF-8, a null I, an out-of-range enum value, a malformed buffer) fails the method with -3.

Errors. A consumer implementation that fails reports it with {p}_error_set(out_err, code, message_ptr, message_len), which copies a borrowed message with the producer’s allocator; it never writes the message itself. What it reports follows the method’s throws:

  • throws: {Domain}: a positive code of that domain, attaching the code’s fields encoded as a value buffer with {p}_error_set_payload, which reaches the producer as that typed error; or -1 with a message for any other failure.
  • throws: any: -1 with a message.
  • No throws: -4 with a message (any non-zero code is accepted).

Anything but a declared code with a payload that decodes reaches the producer as a callback failure, -4 with the consumer’s message, so a consumer bug can’t masquerade as a domain error. A Rust producer’s method returns Result<T, E>, its E built from a typed code or, for a callback failure, with From<weaveffi::ForeignError>.

Async functions

typedef void (*{p}_{f}_callback)(void* context, {p}_error* err, R result);
void {p}_{f}([self,] <one slot per parameter>, [{p}_cancel_token* cancel_token,]
             {p}_{f}_callback callback, void* context);

result is the return’s one C value, as for a synchronous return, and is absent for a function that returns nothing (the completion is then void (*)(void* context, {p}_error* err)). err is NULL on success; otherwise it’s heap-boxed, owned by the consumer, and released with {p}_error_free, and result is the zero value. The launcher has no out_err; every failure, including an input marshalling failure, is delivered through the completion.

The runtime guarantees:

  • The completion fires exactly once, from any thread, possibly before the launcher returns. A null callback discards the result.
  • The launcher takes everything it needs before returning (it copies runs and retains objects and self), so the consumer may release its arguments as soon as it returns.
  • If the executor can’t take the call (it failed to start, or a custom executor panicked), the completion fires with -1 and a message.
  • If the executor drops the future before it completes, the completion fires with -5.
  • For a cancellable function, the producer takes its own reference on the token at launch; the consumer may cancel and destroy its reference at any time afterward. Cancelling wakes the future; the runtime then drops it and completes with -5 and the message cancelled, unless it already completed. Producer code may still poll the token for cooperative cleanup.

Which threads drive the futures is the producer’s business; see Async for the Rust runtime’s executors.

Iterators

{Iter}* launcher([self,] <one slot per parameter>, {p}_error* out_err);
int32_t {Iter}_next({Iter}* iter, T* out_item, {p}_error* out_err);  /* 1 item, 0 done */
void {Iter}_destroy({Iter}* iter);

T is the element’s one C value, and each element follows the return rules of its family. _next checks that out_item is non-null before it pulls an element (failing with -3 otherwise), so a bad call never loses one. _next never blocks on another _next: a call that arrives while the same iterator is being advanced (from another thread, or re-entrantly from inside the producer’s own next) fails with -3. The consumer calls _destroy exactly once, after exhaustion or to abandon the iteration.

Value buffers

Buffers have one encoding, specified in Value Buffers: little-endian, packed, u32 lengths and counts, object tokens as u64. Error payloads use the same encoding, and so do optional scalars and numeric lists nested inside a buffer (a record’s i64? field is a presence byte and a value; its [f64] field is a count and packed elements).

Producers in other languages
