The default flavor is an Android library; the jvm flavor is a plain Kotlin/JVM library for desktop and server JVMs. Records become data classes, interfaces AutoCloseable wrappers, async functions suspend functions, callback interfaces Kotlin interfaces, and the free functions members of one object named after the library, whose functions are @JvmStatic, so Java calls them as static methods.

Kotlin is a Tier 1 target: it tracks every ABI revision as it lands and runs the full conformance suite in CI.

What gets generated

For a library whose C prefix and library name are kitchen_sink:

kotlin/
  settings.gradle.kts          repositories and the root project name
  build.gradle.kts             Android library (or Kotlin/JVM), JNA, maven-publish
  consumer-rules.pro           R8 keep rules (Android flavor only)
  src/main/kotlin/kitchen_sink/
    KitchenSink.kt             every public declaration and the KitchenSink object
    CLibrary.kt                internal: the JNA binding, contract rows, vtables
    Runtime.kt                 FfiException, NativeBugException, NativeSequence, NativeLibrary, lifetime
    Abi.kt                     internal: the C structs JNA maps and the runtime surface
    Buffers.kt                 value-buffer reader and writer, content equality (when the API needs them)
    Codecs.kt                  one codec per composite type (when the API has any)
    Async.kt                   coroutine bridge (when the API has async)

The package defaults to the identity’s C prefix (kitchen_sink), the Gradle group to the package, and the root project and artifact ID to the [package] name. Nothing outside the generated-file headers carries WeaveFFI branding.

Configuration

[generators.kotlin]
name = "com.example.kitchen"   # the Kotlin package; default: the C prefix
flavor = "jvm"                 # "android" (default) or "jvm"
min_sdk = 21                   # Android minSdk (default 21)
compile_sdk = 35               # Android compileSdk (default 35)

Building and loading

The tree builds with Gradle as is (no wrapper is generated), and gradle publishToMavenLocal publishes the module. settings.gradle.kts declares mavenCentral() and the Gradle plugin portal, plus google() for the Android flavor. The Android flavor uses the Android Gradle plugin 8.7.3 and Kotlin 2.0.21 and compiles to Java 8 bytecode; the JVM flavor uses Kotlin 2.0.21. Both depend on JNA 5.18.1 (net.java.dev.jna:jna, as its @aar on Android, which carries JNA’s own native library for every ABI), and an API with async functions also depends on kotlinx-coroutines-core 1.9.0.

  • Android. Put the producer library for each ABI in src/main/jniLibs/<abi>/libkitchen_sink.so, and the AAR packages it.
  • JVM. Put the producer library for each desktop platform under src/main/resources/<os>-<arch>/, the classpath prefix JNA looks in (darwin-aarch64, darwin-x86-64, linux-x86-64, linux-aarch64, win32-x86-64), or anywhere on the system’s library search path or JNA’s jna.library.path.

weaveffi package writes the Gradle project, plus a README, to kotlin/{name}/ with both layouts filled in from the per-platform builds, so building the AAR or JAR needs only Gradle (gradle assembleRelease, gradle jar); see Packaging.

At first use the bindings load the producer with JNA’s Native.load, from the absolute path in KITCHEN_SINK_LIBRARY when it’s set, else wherever JNA finds kitchen_sink. Then they make the two load-time checks: the producer’s kitchen_sink_abi_version() must return 6, and every contract function the bindings were generated with must exist and return its hash. If either check fails, loading fails with an UnsatisfiedLinkError that names the problem:

`Gadget.describe` is missing from the kitchen_sink library (/path/to/libkitchen_sink.dylib)
`Item` changed since these bindings were generated (/path/to/libkitchen_sink.dylib)

A producer that adds declarations, error codes, or callback methods still loads: only the rows the bindings know about are checked.

A failed load is catchable. NativeLibrary.load() loads and checks the library eagerly and throws the UnsatisfiedLinkError that names the problem, the same one on every later call, so an app can probe for the library at startup and fall back gracefully:

try {
    NativeLibrary.load()
} catch (e: UnsatisfiedLinkError) {
    // The library is missing, or it doesn't match these bindings.
}

Without that call, the first use of the bindings throws the same UnsatisfiedLinkError; every later use throws NoClassDefFoundError. Both are LinkageErrors, so catch (e: LinkageError) covers either.

How values cross

CLibrary.kt declares one JNA Library interface, CLibrary, with a method per C function, named after its symbol and typed after the header, so each value crosses as the one C value the ABI gives it:

internal interface CLibrary : CRuntime {
    fun kitchen_sink_Gadget_readings(self: Pointer?, limit: OptU32.ByValue, out_err: NativeError?): Run.ByValue
    fun kitchen_sink_subscribe(listener: CallbackArg, out_err: NativeError?): Int
    ...
}
IDL typeKotlin typeCrosses JNA as
i8, i16, i32, i64Byte, Short, Int, Longthe same
u8, u16, u32, u64UByte, UShort, UInt, ULongthe signed type of the same width
f32, f64Float, Doublethe same
boolBooleanByte (a C bool is one byte; JNA’s boolean is four)
stringStringRun.ByValue of UTF-8
bytesByteArrayRun.ByValue
enumenum class with valueInt
recorddata classRun.ByValue holding a value buffer
rich enumsealed classRun.ByValue holding a value buffer
T? of a scalar or enumInt?, Priority?, …OptI32.ByValue and the like, {has, value}
[T] of i8…f64 (not bool, u8)List<Int>, List<ULong>, …Run.ByValue of the packed elements
other T?, [T], {K:V}T?, List<T>, Map<K, V>Run.ByValue holding a value buffer
interface, Iface?wrapper class, nullable wrapperPointer? (null is none)
callback interface, Cb?Kotlin interface, nullableCallbackArg by value (a null vtable is none)
iter<T>NativeSequence<T>Pointer?

Run, NativeError, CallbackArg, and the eleven Opt* classes are JNA Structures whose ByValue forms cross by value, as the header’s structs do. A run the bindings pass in is staged in JNA Memory the call borrows; a run the library returns is copied out and released with kitchen_sink_run_free. Strings cross as UTF-8 with an explicit length, so interior NULs and supplementary characters round-trip exactly. Unsigned integers are Kotlin’s unsigned types everywhere: parameters, returns, record and variant fields, error payload fields, collection elements and map keys, iterator elements, async results, and callback parameters and returns. Each crosses in the signed type of the same width, converted bit for bit (toUInt(), toInt()), so u64 max is ULong.MAX_VALUE.

An optional scalar or C-style enum (i64?, Priority?) and a list of fixed-width numbers ([f64], [u64]) never go through a value buffer at a call boundary. The same types nested in a record or a collection do.

Value buffers are encoded and decoded by generated functions: one pack_ and unpack_ pair per record and rich enum (next to its declaration) and one per distinct composite type in Codecs.kt, named with the composite’s canonical stem (pack_list_Entry, unpack_map_string_Store, pack_opt_StoreInfo), so every call site calls a function instead of inlining a loop.

Records holding bytes compare by content: a data class with a ByteArray field (or one inside a list, map, or optional) overrides equals, hashCode, and toString to use the array’s content, since a ByteArray’s own equals is identity. Other data classes keep the generated members.

Naming

User types and the object holding the free functions live at the top level of the package. The object is named after the library in UpperCamelCase (kitchen_sink is KitchenSink, kvstore is Kvstore). Two rules keep the generated names valid:

  • A user type named like a Kotlin keyword, a type Kotlin or Java imports by default (Unit, String, UInt, Result, …), a JNA type the generated sources import (Pointer, Structure, Callback, …), or a runtime declaration (FfiException, NativeSequence, Run, …) gains a trailing underscore: Unit becomes Unit_.
  • The object, when its name equals a type’s or a generated file’s (Runtime, Abi, Buffers, Codecs, Async, CLibrary), gains a Library suffix: a store library with a Store interface puts its functions on StoreLibrary.

Functions, methods, parameters, record fields, variant fields, and error payload fields are lowerCamelCase (expires_at is expiresAt). Keywords gain a trailing underscore (class_), as does a method named like a member every wrapper already declares or inherits (close, handle, toString, wait, …; see reserved member names), and an error payload field named like a property every exception has (message, code, cause, …): CallbackFailed.message_. Identifiers in backticks in IDL docs and deprecation messages are rewritten to these spellings (Use `new_op` instead reads Use `newOp` instead).

Objects and lifetime

An interface wrapper owns one strong reference:

class Gadget private constructor(pointer: Pointer) : AutoCloseable {
    internal val handle: NativeHandle = NativeCleaner.register(this, NativeHandle(pointer, Lib.c::kitchen_sink_Gadget_destroy))
    ...

    /** Render the gadget as a human-readable string */
    fun describe(): String {
        return handle.borrow { _self ->
            take(callNative(0) { _err -> Lib.c.kitchen_sink_Gadget_describe(_self, _err) }) { _run -> _run.string() }
        }
    }

Every call borrows the receiver and every object argument for its whole duration. close() (or use { }) releases the reference; if a call is in flight, the release waits until the call returns, and if the wrapper becomes unreachable mid-call, its handle is still borrowed, so the object is never freed under native code. Using a closed wrapper throws IllegalStateException; closing twice is safe. Wrappers that are never closed are released by a daemon thread draining a PhantomReference queue, which works on every Android API level.

Objects inside records and collections cross as new strong references: the encoder clones each one (and releases the clones again if encoding fails), and the decoder adopts each one into a new wrapper.

Errors

Every error is an FfiException with a code and the producer’s message. Each error domain becomes a subclass named with the shared Exception spelling (KvError becomes KvException; KitchenErrors becomes KitchenException), with one nested class per code whose payload fields are properties and whose message defaults to the IDL’s:

open class KitchenException internal constructor(code: Int, message: String) : FfiException(code, message) {
    class NotFound(message: String = "Item not found") : KitchenException(1, message)

A callable declared throws: KitchenErrors raises that domain’s exceptions. Domains are open: a code the bindings don’t declare (one a newer library added) raises a plain KitchenException carrying that code and the library’s message, so an app catches it with the rest of its domain. Codes are unique only within a domain, so each call maps its codes through its own domain: code 1 is CalcException.DivisionByZero from divide and ParseException.NotANumber from parse.

A callable declared throws: any fails with code -1 and a message, raised as the root FfiException. A runtime failure of any throwing call is the root FfiException too, with its negative code: -1 a generic producer error, -2 a producer panic, -3 a marshalling failure, and -4 a callback implementation that failed. Every throwing function carries @Throws for its exception class, so Java callers can catch it as the checked exception it is.

A callable that isn’t throws can’t report an error, so any failure is a bug in the library, and the binding raises the unchecked NativeBugException (an IllegalStateException) instead, following the trap policy. Its code and message name the runtime code and the producer’s message, as in native call failed with code -2: {the panic message}.

A malformed value buffer or an undeclared enum value read from the producer raises NativeBugException with code -3 as well.

Async and cancellation

Async functions are suspend functions. A cancellable one creates a native cancel token for the call, and cancelling the awaiting coroutine (directly, by its scope, or through withTimeout) cancels the token, so the producer drops the pending work:

@JvmStatic
suspend fun doCancellable(input: String): String {
    val input_ = runOf(encodeUtf8(input))
    return awaitNative(true, 0, { _raw -> take(_raw as Run) { _run -> _run.string() } }) { _token, _context ->
        Lib.c.kitchen_sink_do_cancellable(input_, _token, Completions.runDone, _context)
    }
}

The completion callback the launcher gets is one static JNA callback per result type (Completions.runDone), so it stays reachable for as long as the library can call it; the context names the pending call it completes. The coroutine resumes with CancellationException when it’s cancelled or when the producer completes the call with code -5. Completions arrive on producer threads, which JNA attaches to the JVM for the call; the token is destroyed once the call completes. The coroutines runtime (kotlinx-coroutines-core) is a dependency only when the API has async functions.

Callbacks

A callback interface is a plain Kotlin interface (a fun interface when it has one method, so a lambda implements it), and an optional callback parameter (Cb?) takes null for none, which passes a null vtable. Each callback interface has one static vtable whose entries are static JNA callbacks; passing an implementation registers it under a fresh context pointer until the producer calls the vtable’s free, and each entry looks the implementation up by its context. So every JNA callback the library can call stays reachable for good, and an implementation stays reachable exactly as long as the library holds it. Every vtable’s flags is 0: the JVM can run a method on any thread.

interface Policy {
    /**
     * Admit an entry about to be stored, returning it as it should be
     * stored. ...
     *
     * Throw a [KvException] to report a typed error to the library; anything else
     * thrown reaches it as an untyped failure with its message.
     */
    @Throws(KvException::class)
    fun admit(entry: Entry): Entry
    ...
}

fun interface Scorer {
    @Throws(Exception::class)
    fun scores(sizes: List<ULong>): List<Double>
}

val ranked = store.rank { sizes -> sizes.map { it.toDouble() } }
  • Parameters. Strings, bytes, typed arrays, and value buffers are decoded from the borrowed runs before the method runs. An object parameter arrives as a new wrapper that owns the reference the producer handed over; close it, or let the cleaner release it.
  • Returns. A method may return any type but an iterator or a callback interface. A scalar or enum is returned by value, and an optional one as its Opt* struct. An object is returned as a new strong reference (the wrapper keeps its own). A string, bytes, value buffer, or typed array is copied into a run allocated with kitchen_sink_alloc (8-aligned), which the producer adopts.
  • Failures. Whatever a method throws is caught in its vtable entry; nothing unwinds through native frames. A method declared throws: KvError may throw that domain’s exceptions (KvException.Rejected, say), which reach the producer with their code and payload fields, so a producer that propagates one hands the original caller the same typed error (with the message the producer renders from the fields). Anything else it throws, and anything a throws: any method throws, reaches the producer as code -1 with the exception’s message. A method that doesn’t throw reports any failure as code -4.

Iterators

An iter<T> return is a NativeSequence<T>, a Sequence that’s also AutoCloseable, so toList(), for, and the rest of the sequence operations work on it. It can be iterated once. Each hasNext() pulls at most one element. The native iterator is released when the sequence is exhausted, on close(), or by the cleaner. A NativeSequence isn’t thread-safe: iterate it from one thread at a time.

Known limitations

  • Gradle builds aren’t exercised by the conformance suite, which compiles the same sources with kotlinc against the JNA jar.
  • Every call goes through JNA’s reflective dispatch, which costs more per call than a hand-written JNI binding. A typed-array argument is copied twice (the List into a primitive array, then into native memory).
  • Kotlin’s type system can’t express a callback return the producer would reject (a null object for a non-optional I, or a malformed record), so those -3 paths aren’t reachable from Kotlin.

Node.js

The Node.js target generates an npm package: an ES module with TypeScript declarations that loads the producer’s shared library with koffi and calls its C ABI (revision 6) directly. There’s no generated C, no addon, and nothing to compile at install time. The JavaScript API is shared with the WebAssembly target; only the transport underneath differs, so code written against one runs against the other.

Node.js is a Tier 1 target: it tracks every ABI revision as it lands and runs the full conformance suite in CI.

What gets generated

For a library named kvstore (the snapshot fixtures use kitchen_sink):

node/
├── package.json   ES module manifest, depending on a pinned koffi release
├── index.js       the C signatures, the load-time checks, and the API
├── index.d.ts     TypeScript declarations
├── runtime.js     the shared runtime (codec, checks, errors, wrappers, load-time checks)
├── native.js      the transport: loading the library with koffi and binding its symbols
├── debug.js       the `./debug` export: leak counters for tests
├── debug.d.ts
└── README.md

The package name defaults to the package identity’s name; set name under [generators.node] in weaveffi.toml to override it, and node_engine to change engines.node (default >=18). The package’s one dependency is koffi, pinned to an exact release (3.3.2).

Install and load

The generated directory is a complete package. Install it like any other, and npm fetches koffi (which ships prebuilt binaries for every platform it supports):

npm install ./node

Importing the package loads the library. The first of these that exists wins:

  1. the path in the {PREFIX}_LIBRARY environment variable (KVSTORE_LIBRARY), the library file or the directory holding it;
  2. the copy in the {package}-{os}-{cpu} package npm installed for this platform (see Packaging);
  3. a copy next to index.js, where weaveffi generate --dev puts it;
  4. the bare file name (libkvstore.so, libkvstore.dylib, kvstore.dll), which the system’s library search resolves (LD_LIBRARY_PATH, DYLD_LIBRARY_PATH, PATH).
import { Store, EntryKind, KvstoreError } from 'kvstore';

const store = Store.open('/tmp/data');
store.put('greeting', new TextEncoder().encode('hi'), EntryKind.Volatile, null);
store.close();

If no candidate loads, the import throws an Error that lists the paths it tried and names the environment variable.

Load-time checks

Before any other call, the import checks that the library matches the bindings (see Load-time checks): the library must implement ABI revision 6, and it must export every declaration’s contract function, returning the hash the bindings were generated with. index.js embeds one row per declaration, error code, and callback method, each with its canonical signature:

const $contract = [
  ['kitchen_sink_contract_784cfd093415f2b5', 0xffcf2bd3b04aa002n, 'Gadget.new'], // constructor new(i64) -> Gadget
  // ...
];
$verify($m, 'kitchen_sink', 6, $contract);

If a check fails, the import throws an Error naming the declaration: kvstore: Store.put is missing from the library or kvstore: Store.put changed since these bindings were generated. Declarations the library has and the bindings don’t are fine, so a library that only adds to its API (declarations, error codes, callback methods) keeps working with older bindings. Since the import fails the same way when the library can’t be found, await import(...) in a try can fall back.

Names

A library is one namespace, and every declaration is a named export of the package: classes for interfaces and errors, frozen objects for C-style enums, functions, and types for records, rich enums, and callback interfaces.

import * as kitchen from 'kitchen_sink';

const gadget = new kitchen.Gadget(1n);
kitchen.optI32Id(null);

Functions, methods, and parameters are camelCase. A free function or parameter whose name is a JavaScript reserved word gains a trailing underscore (a function delete becomes delete_), and so does a free function named init, which the WebAssembly target exports, so the two APIs stay the same. Methods keep reserved words, which are legal property names (store.delete(key)), except that an instance method named close or constructor, and a static named name, length, prototype, or caller, gains one too (reserved member names). Record fields keep their IDL spelling. Doc comments in index.d.ts name declarations in this spelling too: an IDL doc or deprecation message that says `new_op` reads `newOp` there.

Inside index.js, each declaration is bound under its public name plus $ (Gadget$) and exported under the public name, so no declaration can shadow a global the generated code uses, such as Map or TypeError.

Type mapping

IDL typeTypeScript typeNotes
i8, i16, i32, u8, u16, u32numberA fractional, non-finite, or out-of-range argument throws a RangeError; nothing wraps
i64, u64bigintIntegral numbers in range are accepted as arguments; anything else throws a RangeError
f32, f64numberNaN, the infinities, and -0 round-trip; an f32 argument rounds as Float32Array does
boolboolean
stringstringUTF-8 in a run, so interior NULs survive
bytesUint8Array
recordinterfaceA plain object; fields keep their IDL names
C-style enumenumA frozen object with forward and reverse mappings (negative values too)
rich enumtagged union{ tag: 'Circle', radius: 2.5 }
interfaceclassSee Objects and lifetime
callback interfaceinterfaceAny object with the methods; see Callback interfaces
T?T | nullundefined is accepted as null. An optional scalar or C-style enum crosses directly as a {prefix}_opt_* struct, with no buffer
[T]T[]A list of i8, i16, i32, i64, u16, u32, u64, f32, or f64 crosses as a typed array: an argument may be an array (checked element by element) or the matching typed array (Float64Array for [f64], passed as is), and a result is a plain array
{K: V}Record<string, V>Record<number, V> for integer keys of 32 bits or fewer, Partial<Record<E, V>> for C-style enum keys; keys are property names (strings) at run time, and a Map is accepted as an argument
iter<T>NativeIterator<T>Lazy; an IterableIterator<T> with close() and [Symbol.dispose]()

Arguments are checked before the native call: a value of the wrong type throws a TypeError, and an integer that is fractional or out of its type’s range a RangeError, whether it’s an argument, an element of a list, a field of a record, or a callback’s return value.

Value buffers are encoded and decoded by one function per type: each record and rich enum ($w$Entry, $r$Entry), each interface carried as an object token, and each distinct optional, list, and map type in the API, named by its canonical stem ($w_list_opt_Entry writes [Entry?]). Call sites name these functions.

How values cross

index.js lists the C signature of every function it calls ($sigs), one slot kind per C value: a scalar (i32, bool, …), an optional scalar (opt_i64), a run (run), a pointer (ptr), a callback-interface argument (cb), and the slots the transport fills in itself (the error slot err, an iterator’s item slot out:<kind>, and an async launcher’s completion done:<kind> and context ctx):

const $sigs = {
  kitchen_sink_opt_mixed: ['opt_u16', ['f64', 'opt_f32', 'opt_i64', 'run', 'err']],
  kitchen_sink_do_cancellable: ['void', ['run', 'ptr', 'done:run', 'ctx']],
  kitchen_sink_StreamItemsIterator_next: ['i32', ['ptr', 'out:run', 'err']],
  // ...
};

native.js declares the C structs ({prefix}_run, the {prefix}_opt_* structs, {prefix}_error, and the callback-interface argument) with koffi’s type syntax and binds each function by value, exactly as the header declares it. A bound function takes and returns one JavaScript value per C value: a Uint8Array for a run (lent for a parameter, and a copy for a return, whose run it then releases with {prefix}_run_free), the value or null for an optional scalar, a bigint handle or null for an object. After a synchronous call it reads the error slot, and on a failure it clears the slot and throws. The generated API above it does the rest: it encodes strings as UTF-8, checks and copies typed arrays, encodes and decodes buffers, and lends, adopts, and clones objects.

  poke(times) {
    const $self = $lend(this, Gadget$);
    try {
      const $a0 = $check.I32(times, 'times');
      return $f.kitchen_sink_Gadget_poke($self, $a0);
    } catch ($e) {
      throw $from$KitchenErrors($e);
    } finally {
      $unlend(this);
    }
  }

A typed-array argument is lent to the library in place: its elements are aligned for their type, since a typed array’s offset is always a multiple of its element size.

Objects and lifetime

An interface is a class whose instances each own one strong reference to the native object. close() releases it, and so does [Symbol.dispose]() (for using declarations); both are idempotent, and a wrapper that is garbage collected unclosed is released by a FinalizationRegistry. Every call lends the wrapper to the native side for its duration, so a close() that races the call (from a callback, or while an async method is pending) waits for it to return. Using a closed wrapper throws a code -3 error.

The synchronous constructor named new is the class constructor; other constructors are static factories, and a class without one can’t be constructed directly. Objects returned, awaited, iterated, decoded from a record, or passed to a callback method are new wrappers the receiver owns. Two wrappers may share one native object.

Errors

Every error extends the package’s root class, named after the package (here KitchenSinkError), which carries the ABI code. When the package’s own name is also a declaration’s (the codec sample’s error domain is CodecError), the root class adds Library (CodecLibraryError).

  • Each error domain is a class, named by the shared rule (a domain KitchenErrors is KitchenError, KvError stays KvError, and Failure becomes FailureError), and each of its codes a subclass (NotFoundError) with a static CODE and the IDL message as its default message. Codes of different domains may share a value. Payload fields become properties. A code without fields is constructed as new InvalidPathError(message?); a code with fields takes them first: new RejectedError({ key, reason }, message?). A code whose class would take a name another export has gains its domain’s name first.
  • A function declared throws: SomeDomain fails with that domain’s classes. Domains are open: a positive code the bindings don’t know (the library added it later) is an instance of the domain class itself, with its code and message.
  • A function declared throws: any fails with the root class, code -1, and the library’s message.
  • A cancelled call rejects with CancelledError (code -5).
  • Other runtime failures throw the root class itself: -2 producer panic, -3 marshalling failure, -4 a callback implementation failed.

A failure of a function that doesn’t declare throws is a producer bug, and the binding throws the root class with the code and the producer’s message (see the trap policy).

Async and cancellation

An async function returns a Promise. Its completion is a JavaScript function koffi registered once per result type, and it may arrive on any producer thread; koffi runs it on the JavaScript thread, and it settles the promise. While any call is pending, the transport keeps the event loop alive, so a script that awaits nothing else still waits for it. A cancellable function takes an optional last argument with an AbortSignal:

export declare function doCancellable(input: string, options?: { signal?: AbortSignal }): Promise<string>;

Aborting the signal cancels the native token, and the call rejects with CancelledError. An already-aborted signal cancels the call before it starts.

Callback interfaces

Any object with the interface’s methods implements it; the methods are camelCase. Arguments arrive converted: strings and bytes as string and Uint8Array, records decoded, and objects as new wrappers the implementation owns (close them, or let the garbage collector release them). A parameter of an optional callback interface (Cb?) takes null for none.

An implementation crosses as the {prefix}_{Cb} struct: a context, an integer the package maps back to the implementation, and the interface’s static vtable. The vtable is built once per interface, the first time an implementation is passed, from JavaScript functions registered with koffi (free first, then one per method); it lives as long as the process, so the number of registered callbacks stays small however many implementations are live. The library’s call to free releases the implementation.

A method’s return value is checked and handed to the library in its family’s form:

IDL returnReturn from JavaScriptWhat the library receives
direct (numbers, bool, C-style enums)the valuethe value (integers range-checked)
optional scalar (i32?, Mode?, …)the value, or nulla {prefix}_opt_* struct
numeric list ([f64], …)an array or the matching typed arraya copy in a run from {prefix}_alloc, which the library adopts
string, bytesa string, a Uint8Arraya copy in a run the library adopts
record, rich enum, other T?, [T], {K:V}the valueits encoding, in a run the library adopts
Ia wrapper (never null)a new strong reference to its object
I?a wrapper or nulla new strong reference, or null

Nothing unwinds through native code; how a failure reaches the library follows the method’s throws:

  • A method declared throws: SomeDomain may throw one of that domain’s errors, and the library receives its code and fields. (A Rust producer re-renders the message from the fields.) Any other exception is reported as code -1 with its message.
  • A method declared throws: any reports any exception as code -1 with its message.
  • A method that doesn’t declare throws reports an exception, or a return value of the wrong type, as code -4 with its message.
const policy = {
  ttlFor(key, requested) {
    return key.startsWith('tmp/') ? 60n : requested;
  },
  admit(entry) {
    if (entry.key.startsWith('secret')) {
      throw new RejectedError({ key: entry.key, reason: 'no secrets' });
    }
    return { ...entry, tags: ['admitted'] };
  },
  route(key, home) {
    return home;
  },
};
store.setPolicy(policy);

Threading

The producer may call back from any thread, and every JavaScript call runs on the JavaScript thread:

  • A call made on the JavaScript thread (because the JavaScript thread is inside a call into the library) runs directly, nested inside that call.
  • A call made on any other thread is queued by koffi for the JavaScript thread and runs from its event loop, while the producer thread waits for it to return. Nothing runs while the JavaScript thread is busy, so such calls are delivered only once the current JavaScript task returns to the event loop.
  • The library’s release of an implementation (its vtable’s free) may come from any thread and follows the same rules.

The waiting in the second rule can deadlock: if the JavaScript thread is inside a synchronous call into the library that itself waits for the thread making the callback (joins it, or waits on a lock it holds), neither thread can proceed. So:

  • Make calls that wait for callbacks from other threads async, or have the producer call back on the calling thread.
  • Return to the event loop regularly while callbacks from other threads are expected: a long-running JavaScript task delays them.

Iterators

An iter<T> result is a lazy NativeIterator<T>: each next() makes one native call. The native iterator is destroyed when it’s exhausted, when it fails, on return() (which for...of calls on break), on close() or a using declaration, or when the iterator is garbage collected.

using chunks = kitchen.streamChunks();
const first = chunks.next(); // released when the block exits

Packaging

weaveffi package writes npm tarballs in the esbuild-style layout: one {package}-{os}-{cpu} package per desktop platform, gated by npm os and cpu and carrying only that platform’s library, plus the main package, which lists them in optionalDependencies. npm install then fetches the main package, koffi, and the one platform package that matches, and the import loads the library from it. See Packaging.

Leak counters

The package’s ./debug export reads the library’s live-resource counters, for tests of code that uses the bindings. It isn’t part of the API:

import { debugLive } from 'kvstore/debug';

debugLive(0); // live objects, as a bigint (1 callbacks, 2 iterators, 3 cancel tokens, 4 runs)

Kind -1 is 1n when the library counts at all (built with weaveffi’s leak-check feature); otherwise every counter is 0n.

Known limitations

  • A callback from another thread waits for the JavaScript thread, so a synchronous call that waits for that callback’s thread deadlocks (see Threading).
  • A callback implementation that holds a wrapper of the object retaining it forms a cycle the garbage collector can’t see; close the wrapper or release the callback explicitly.
  • The package runs on the platforms koffi supports, which ships prebuilt binaries for the desktop and server platforms Node.js runs on.
  • A numeric list result is copied into a plain array. As an argument, the matching typed array is lent to the library without a copy, while a plain array is checked and copied first.
  • The package is ESM only, and its declarations need TypeScript 5.2 or later (for Symbol.dispose).

WebAssembly

The WebAssembly target generates an npm package for a wasm32-unknown-unknown build of the library: an ES module with TypeScript declarations over a transport that stages values in the module’s linear memory and calls its C ABI (revision 6). The JavaScript API is the Node.js target’s, so the sections there on names, types, objects, errors, callbacks, and iterators apply here unchanged; this page covers what differs.

WebAssembly is a Tier 2 target: it may lag behind a new ABI revision, but it passes the same conformance suite before a release.

What gets generated

For a library named kvstore (the snapshot fixtures use kitchen_sink):

wasm/
├── package.json   ES module manifest
├── index.js       the C signatures, `init()`, and the API
├── index.d.ts     TypeScript declarations
├── runtime.js     the shared runtime (codec, checks, errors, wrappers)
├── debug.js       the `./debug` export: leak counters for tests
├── debug.d.ts
├── linear.js      the transport: loading, staging, table functions
└── README.md

The package name defaults to the package identity’s name; set name under [generators.wasm] to override it. The package has no dependencies. weaveffi package writes the npm tarball wasm/{package}-{version}.tgz with the module weaveffi build linked for wasm32 as {library}.wasm.

Build the module

The transport installs async completions and callback-interface methods in the module’s function table, so link the module with an exported, growable table:

RUSTFLAGS="-C link-arg=--export-table -C link-arg=--growable-table" \
  cargo build --release --target wasm32-unknown-unknown

weaveffi build --platforms wasm32 passes those flags itself and writes the module to target/weaveffi/wasm32/{library}.wasm.

Load

init() loads the module and checks it before any other export works: the module must implement ABI revision 6 and export every declaration’s contract function with the hash the bindings were generated with (see the Node.js page). A failed check rejects with an Error naming the declaration (kvstore: Store.put changed since these bindings were generated). Until the promise resolves, every other call throws.

import { init, Store } from 'kvstore';

await init();
const store = Store.open('/tmp/data');

Without an argument, init() reads the file named by the {PREFIX}_LIBRARY environment variable (KVSTORE_LIBRARY) when it’s set on Node.js, and otherwise loads {library}.wasm from next to index.js (by fetch in a browser, from disk on Node.js). It also takes a URL, a path (resolved against index.js), bytes, a Response, or a compiled WebAssembly.Module:

export declare function init(
  source?: string | URL | BufferSource | WebAssembly.Module | Response,
): Promise<void>;

init() may be called more than once; later calls return the same promise, and a failed load can be retried.

Transport

linear.js binds the same C signatures ($sigs) the Node.js transport does, so the API above it is identical. Each bound function lowers its arguments to the module’s C calling convention for wasm32, which Rust’s extern "C" follows: integers of 32 bits or fewer, bool, size_t, and pointers are i32; int64_t and uint64_t are i64; float and double are f32 and f64. A struct (a {prefix}_run, a {prefix}_opt_*, or a callback-interface argument) is passed indirectly, as the i32 address of a copy the caller owns, and a struct return comes back through a hidden first i32 parameter, the address the callee writes it to, with the function returning nothing. None of the ABI’s structs is a single scalar, so none is passed directly. These are the export types a build of the samples has:

kvstore_Store_tick         (i32 self, i64 seconds, i32 out_err) -> i64
kvstore_Store_open         (i32 path*, i32 out_err) -> i32
kvstore_Store_put          (i32 ret*, i32 self, i32 key*, i32 value*, i32 kind, i32 ttl_seconds*, i32 out_err) -> ()
kvstore_Store_expires_at   (i32 ret*, i32 self, i32 key*, i32 out_err) -> ()
kvstore_Store_subscribe    (i32 self, i32 listener*, i32 out_err) -> i32
kvstore_run_free           (i32 run*) -> ()
codec_echo_opt_bool        (i32 ret*, i32 value*, i32 out_err) -> ()

The copies live on a small stack the transport carves from linear memory, popped as each call returns. A run argument is staged in a run from {prefix}_alloc (8-aligned, so a typed array’s elements are aligned for their type) and released with {prefix}_run_free once the call returns; a returned run is copied out and released the same way. The transport reads and writes multi-byte values through a DataView. One error slot and one iterator item slot are reused by every call; they, the stack, and the callback vtables live in 8-byte-aligned blocks carved from arenas the transport allocates once and never frees. The ./debug export’s debugLive(4) leaves those arenas out of its count of runs. Object handles are linear-memory addresses.

Async completions and callback-interface methods are JavaScript functions installed in the module’s function table, with the same lowering in reverse (a method returning a run or an optional scalar writes it through its hidden first parameter). The transport uses WebAssembly.Function where the engine provides it and otherwise compiles a tiny module that imports the function with the needed signature and re-exports it, so no experimental flag is needed.

Type mapping

The mapping is the Node.js target’s: 64-bit integers are bigint, bytes is Uint8Array, records are plain objects, rich enums are tagged unions, C-style enums are frozen objects, interfaces are classes with close() and [Symbol.dispose](), optionals are T | null, lists are arrays (a numeric list argument may also be the matching typed array), maps are Record objects (a Map is accepted as an argument), and iter<T> is a lazy NativeIterator<T> with close(). Integer arguments are range-checked the same way, so a value that throws a RangeError on one target throws it on the other. A usize is 32 bits on wasm32, so a wider value fails the producer’s conversion with code -3.

Async and cancellation

Async functions return a Promise, and cancellable ones take an optional { signal } last argument, exactly as on Node.js. The module is single-threaded, and on wasm32 the default executor polls a future inline until it completes, inside its launcher, so an async call has completed by the time the Promise is returned; only a signal aborted before the call can cancel it. A future that’s still pending once nothing can wake it (it awaits something only another thread or a reactor would complete) fails with code -1 instead (async function suspended with no executor on wasm32). A cancelled call rejects with CancelledError (code -5).

Callbacks and errors

Callback interfaces work as on Node.js: returns of every family (a string, bytes, buffer, or typed-array return is copied into a run from {prefix}_alloc that the producer adopts, an optional scalar is a {prefix}_opt_* struct, and an object return is a new reference), errors reported by each method’s throws (a domain’s codes with their fields, -1 for throws: any, -4 for a method that doesn’t throw), and null for an optional callback (Cb?), which passes a null vtable. The vtable starts with its size, flags (0), and free entries, like any consumer’s.

There are no threads, so a callback runs only while a call into the module is on the stack, on the calling thread, and the Node.js target’s threading rules don’t arise.

Traps

Because wasm32-unknown-unknown aborts on a panic, a producer panic traps the module. The call that trapped fails with the package’s root error, code -2 (the native library trapped: unreachable). A trap can leave the module’s memory inconsistent, so it also poisons the instance: every later call fails with code -2 (kvstore: the WebAssembly instance trapped earlier (...) and can't be used anymore) without running, while releases (closing a wrapper or an iterator, a garbage-collected wrapper, a cancel token) do nothing, so no finalizer throws. Load the module again in a new process or page to recover.

Known limitations

  • The module must export its function table and allow it to grow.
  • An async call can’t be cancelled once launched, since it completes before its launcher returns, and a future that waits on another thread or an external event fails with code -1.
  • A producer panic traps rather than unwinding, and the instance is unusable afterward (see Traps).
  • Calls nested more than a few hundred deep (callbacks that call back into the module) exhaust the transport’s 16 KiB stack of struct copies and throw a RangeError.
  • Only wasm32-unknown-unknown builds are supported.

Python

The Python target produces a pip-installable package of pure-Python ctypes bindings over the C ABI (revision 6). There’s no compilation step, no native extension, and no third-party runtime dependency; the package works on any Python 3.10+ interpreter that can load the producer library. The implementation module is fully annotated and the package ships py.typed, so type checkers read the bindings themselves (there’s no separate stub to drift out of date), and the generated code is written to pass mypy --strict.

Every value crosses the boundary as one C value. Records and rich enums are frozen dataclasses that cross as value buffers. Interfaces are reference-counted wrapper classes with a context manager. Error domains are exception classes. Callback interfaces are abstract base classes the consumer subclasses. Async functions are coroutines whose cancellation cancels the native call, and iter<T> returns are lazy NativeIterator[T] objects.

Python is a Tier 1 target: it tracks every ABI revision as it lands and runs the full conformance suite in CI.

What gets generated

For the kvstore sample (package name kvstore, C prefix kvstore):

python/
├── pyproject.toml          # PEP 621 metadata, built with setuptools
├── README.md
└── kvstore/                # the import package, named after the C prefix
    ├── __init__.py         # re-exports the module's __all__
    ├── kvstore.py          # runtime, prototypes, and wrappers
    └── py.typed            # PEP 561 marker

The distribution name is the package identity’s name and the import package is its C prefix, so a crate named key-store installs as key-store and imports as key_store. Both are configurable:

[generators.python]
name = "kvstore-python"      # distribution name (default: package name)
import_name = "kvstore_py"   # import package (default: C prefix)
requires_python = ">=3.12"   # default ">=3.10"

A library is one namespace, and so is the package: every record, enum, error class, interface, callback interface, and free function is exported from the package itself under its IDL name (functions in snake case). The module lists its public names in __all__ (the error classes, LibraryLoadError, NativeIterator, every type, and the free functions), so from kvstore import * and the package’s __init__.py export the API and none of the runtime’s helpers. Annotations use builtin generics and X | None.

Install and load

weaveffi generate -o bindings --target python   # in the producer crate
pip install bindings/python

At import, the module loads the producer library from the first of:

  1. the path in {PREFIX}_LIBRARY (for example KVSTORE_LIBRARY),
  2. a library bundled inside the package directory (what weaveffi package produces, and where weaveffi generate --dev copies the debug build), and
  3. the system loader’s search path, by platform file name (libkvstore.dylib, libkvstore.so, or kvstore.dll).

It then makes the two load-time checks before binding anything else. kvstore_abi_version() must report revision 6, and every declaration the bindings were generated with (every function, interface member, type, error code, and callback method) must have its contract function in the library, returning the hash the bindings embed. The module holds one row per declaration: the contract function, the hash, and the declaration’s path, with its canonical signature:

_CONTRACT: list[tuple[str, int, str]] = [
    ("kvstore_contract_c6c5d9ee1b7cfa9e", 0x36b344095a0f5bf9, "Store.rank"),  # method rank(Scorer) -> [string] throws KvError
    ...
]

When the library can’t be loaded, or a check fails, the import raises LibraryLoadError, an ImportError subclass whose message says what went wrong and whose path names the library:

kvstore.kvstore.LibraryLoadError: Store.put changed since these bindings were generated (/opt/lib/libkvstore.dylib)

The other messages are Store.put is missing from the library, a C ABI mismatch naming both revisions, a library that doesn’t export kvstore_abi_version, and the loader’s own error with a hint to set KVSTORE_LIBRARY. A stale binding never misreads a buffer at runtime. Declarations the library has and the bindings don’t are fine, so a library that grew new functions, error codes, or callback methods still loads. Since the failure happens during the import, catch it as ImportError around the import statement.

Every C prototype is then bound once, in a module-level table:

_c_Store_count = _bind("kvstore_Store_count", ctypes.c_uint64, ctypes.c_void_p, ctypes.POINTER(_ErrorStruct))

On macOS, the system python3 strips DYLD_LIBRARY_PATH, so prefer {PREFIX}_LIBRARY there.

Type mapping

Parameters accept abstract containers (any Sequence or Mapping) and returns hand out concrete ones (list, dict). Every value is one C value in every position (a parameter, a return, an async result, an iterator item, and a callback method’s arguments and return), and the runtime mirrors the C structures as ctypes.Structure subclasses passed and returned by value: _Run for {prefix}_run (a pointer and a byte length) and _OptI32, _OptF64, and so on for each {prefix}_opt_{prim} (a presence flag and a value).

IDL typePython typeCrosses the ABI as
i8 to u64intc_int8 to c_uint64, range-checked
f32, f64floatc_float, c_double
boolboolc_bool
C-style enumIntEnum subclassc_int32
scalar or enum T?T | None_Opt{Prim} (an enum’s is _OptI32)
stringstr_Run of UTF-8
bytesbytes_Run of the bytes
[i8] to [u64], [f32], [f64]Sequence[int] or Sequence[float] in, list out_Run of the packed elements (a typed array)
recordfrozen @dataclass_Run holding a value buffer
rich enumbase class plus one frozen @dataclass per variant_Run holding a value buffer
other T?, [T], {K:V}T | None, Sequence[T], Mapping[K, V] in; T | None, list[T], dict[K, V] out_Run holding a value buffer
interfacewrapper classc_void_p
interface ?... | Nonec_void_p, null for None
callback interfacesubclass of the generated ABC_{Name}Arg (a context and a vtable)
callback interface ?... | None_{Name}Arg with a null vtable for None
iter<T>NativeIterator[T]iterator handle

ctypes truncates an out-of-range integer to the parameter’s C width without a word, so every integer argument is checked first: a value out of range raises OverflowError naming the parameter (a: 2147483648 is out of range for i32), and a non-integer raises TypeError. The checks run before anything is lent or registered for the call, so a failed check leaks nothing.

An optional scalar (i64?, bool?, Priority?) never touches a value buffer: None passes an absent _Opt{Prim}, and a returned one becomes the value or None. A numeric list parameter accepts any sequence or iterable of numbers (a list, a tuple, a range, a generator); it’s packed into an array.array with range checks, whose buffer Python aligns for every element type, and lent to the call. An array.array of the matching type code is lent as is, without a copy. A str or bytes argument is refused rather than reinterpreted. Returned typed arrays are copied into a list and released.

Strings and bytes pass without per-byte loops: a str is encoded once and its bytes are lent to the call as a run, so interior NUL characters survive. Returned strings, bytes, typed arrays, and buffers are copied with ctypes.string_at and released with {prefix}_run_free.

Every record and rich enum has one writer and one reader function (_write_Entry, _read_Entry), and so does every distinct composite type the API uses, named by the composite’s canonical stem (_write_list_string, _read_map_string_Store, _read_opt_Entry); a call site makes one call instead of inlining a loop. Lists of a fixed-width number type inside a buffer are packed and unpacked with one struct call. A value out of range for its field raises OverflowError while encoding. A decoded buffer that’s truncated, has trailing bytes, an invalid flag, invalid UTF-8, an undeclared enum value, or a repeated map key raises InternalError with code -3.

Records are @dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True): they compare and hash by value (a record holding a list or dict field compares by value but can’t be hashed), and a changed copy comes from dataclasses.replace:

entry = store.get("alpha")
renamed = dataclasses.replace(entry, tags=["archived"])

Rich-enum variants work with isinstance and with match:

match change:
    case kvstore.ChangePut(entry=entry, replaced=True):
        print("replaced", entry.key)
    case kvstore.ChangeRemoved(key=key):
        print("removed", key)

The module starts with from __future__ import annotations, so annotations name types declared later in the file without quotes. Backticked identifiers in IDL docs and deprecation messages are rewritten to their Python spellings in docstrings and DeprecationWarnings.

Objects and lifetime

An interface wrapper holds one strong reference and releases it exactly once: from close(), the with statement, or the __del__ backstop. close() is idempotent, and using a closed wrapper raises ValueError. Each call lends the pointer for its duration, so close() from another thread while a call is in flight defers the release until the call returns. Two wrappers compare equal (and hash alike) when they hold the same native object, so store.share() == store and a record holding a Store compares by identity. An interface member named close is close_, so it never replaces the wrapper’s own (reserved member names).

A constructor named new becomes __init__; other constructors are @classmethod factories:

    @classmethod
    def open(cls, path: str) -> Store:
        _err = _ErrorStruct()
        _ret = _c_Store_open(_run_in(path.encode("utf-8")), ctypes.byref(_err))
        if _err.code:
            raise _kv_error_from(*_read_error(_err))
        return cls._adopt(_required(_ret))

Objects returned by the producer, decoded from a buffer, or passed to a callback are adopted into new wrappers. Writing an object into a value buffer (a record field or a list element) mints a new reference only after the whole argument list has encoded, so a failed encoding leaks nothing.

Errors

Every declared error derives from the package’s root Error (named {PascalName}Error if the API declares its own Error type), which carries code and message. Each error domain is a subclass named by the shared rule (KvError stays KvError, KitchenErrors becomes KitchenError, Failure becomes FailureError). Each code is a subclass of its domain named {Code}Error (KeyNotFoundError), also reachable as KvError.KeyNotFound, carrying its stable CODE. When {Code}Error would shadow a Python builtin (TimeoutError) or another declaration, it’s qualified with the domain’s stem (KvTimeoutError). A code’s payload fields are constructor arguments and attributes; a field named like an exception attribute (message, code, args) gets a trailing _:

try:
    store.get("nope")
except kvstore.KvError.KeyNotFound as e:
    print(e.code, e.key, e.message)  # 1001 nope key not found: nope

What a failed call raises follows its throws:

  • throws: SomeDomain: the code’s class. Codes are matched within the callable’s domain, so two domains may reuse a value. Domains are open: a positive code these bindings don’t know (from a newer library) raises the domain’s base class with its code and message.
  • throws: any: the root Error with code -1 and the producer’s message.
  • A negative code on a throwing call (a panic, a marshalling failure, or a failed callback) raises the root Error with that code; the constants are GENERIC_ERROR_CODE (-1), PANIC_ERROR_CODE (-2), MARSHAL_ERROR_CODE (-3), and FOREIGN_ERROR_CODE (-4).

A call that declares no errors can only fail through a bug, so it follows the trap policy: it raises InternalError, a RuntimeError subclass outside the Error hierarchy whose message names the code and the producer’s message (InternalError: (-3) ...). A cancelled async call (-5) raises asyncio.CancelledError instead.

Each call passes a fresh error slot, which the producer overwrites. Error messages cross as UTF-8 bytes plus a length, so they may contain any character; an empty message is "".

Async and cancellation

An async function is an async def that must run on an asyncio event loop. The launcher receives a static completion trampoline and an integer key; the trampoline takes ownership of the result (one C value, like a sync return) on the producer’s thread and settles the future through call_soon_threadsafe:

async def open_store(path: str) -> Store:
    _call, _future = _async_begin()
    try:
        _c_open_store(_run_in(path.encode("utf-8")), _open_store_completion, _call)
    except BaseException:
        _async_abandon(_call)
        raise
    _result: Store = await _future
    return _result

The launcher copies everything it needs before it returns, so the arguments’ memory needn’t outlive the call.

For a cancellable function, each call owns a native cancel token. Cancelling the awaiting task (directly, through asyncio.wait_for, or by cancelling a gather) cancels the token, waits for the producer’s cancelled completion, and raises asyncio.CancelledError. Cancelling the task awaiting a function that isn’t cancellable stops the wait only; the native call runs to completion and its result is dropped.

Callbacks

A callback interface is an abc.ABC with one abstract method per IDL method. Passing an instance registers it in a handle table under an integer key, and the call passes the producer a _{Name}Arg structure holding that key as ctx and the address of the interface’s one vtable; None for an optional callback parameter passes a null vtable. The vtable is built the first time an implementation is passed and kept for the process lifetime. It starts with the header (its size, flags, and the shared free entry), and the producer’s free(ctx) removes the table entry, so an implementation lives exactly as long as the producer holds it. flags is 0: the producer may call any method from any thread.

Each method’s vtable entry is a trampoline that takes the method’s C arguments and returns its one C value. A ctypes callback can’t return a structure, so a method returning a _Run (a string, bytes, typed array, or buffered value) or an _Opt{Prim} is a libffi closure the runtime builds directly, through the libffi ctypes itself runs on; every other method is a plain ctypes callback.

Trampolines run on whichever thread the producer calls from; ctypes acquires the GIL first. A trampoline adopts the object arguments first, converts the rest (an optional scalar arrives as T | None, a typed array as a list, and a run is copied, since it’s only lent), calls the method, and returns:

  • a number, bool, or enum, checked for its type and range inside the trampoline (so a wrong return is reported as a failure rather than truncated);
  • an optional scalar as its _Opt{Prim};
  • an object as a fresh strong reference the producer adopts (None is allowed only for I?);
  • a str, bytes, typed array, record, or other buffered value as a run allocated with {prefix}_alloc, which the producer adopts and frees.
def _Loader_load(ctx: int | None, key: _Run, out_err: int | None) -> _Run:
    try:
        _ret = _callback_get(ctx).load(_peek_str(key))
        return _alloc_run(bytes(_ret))
    except BaseException as exc:
        _callback_fail(out_err, exc, _GENERIC, KvError)
        return _Run()

Nothing unwinds through C. A method that throws a domain may raise that domain’s codes, which reach the producer as that code with the fields as the error payload ({prefix}_error_set and {prefix}_error_set_payload):

class EmptyLoader(kvstore.Loader):
    def name(self) -> str:
        return "empty"

    def fallback(self, key: str) -> kvstore.Store | None:
        return None

    def load(self, key: str) -> bytes:
        raise kvstore.KeyNotFoundError(key=key)

A Rust producer decodes such a code into its typed error, so the caller of the original function sees the domain’s own message rendered from the fields. Any other exception, or a return of the wrong type, reaches the producer as code -1 with the exception’s message (-4 for a method that declares no errors); a Rust producer receives it as a ForeignError, which the kvstore sample turns into KvError.CallbackFailed.

Iterators

An iter<T> return is a NativeIterator[T], a lazy iterator: each next() makes one native next call through the pre-bound function, receiving the item in one out slot of its C type, and the handle is released on exhaustion, close(), the end of a with block, or garbage collection. A closed iterator is exhausted.

for key in store.keys(None):
    print(key)

with store.entries("user.") as entries:
    first = next(entries)   # the rest is released when the block ends

Packaging

weaveffi package --target python writes one wheel per desktop platform built, python/{name}-{version}-py3-none-{platform}.whl (the distribution name escaped for a wheel file name, so Key-Store becomes key_store), with the producer library inside the import package. The platform tag matches the build: the [build] macOS deployment target (macosx_11_0_arm64), the newest glibc the library links against on Linux (manylinux_2_17_x86_64 or later), or win_amd64. The wheels install with pip and upload with twine; Android, iOS, and wasm32 builds have no wheel platform and are skipped. See Packaging.

Known limitations

  • ctypes dispatch costs more per call than a compiled extension; hot loops that cross the boundary per element will feel it. The bindings keep the per-call work small (prototypes and vtable addresses are resolved once, and the error check is inline), but each call still allocates its error slot, and each integer argument costs a range check.
  • A callback method that returns a structure needs libffi, which the runtime finds through the _ctypes extension or by name (ctypes.util.find_library("ffi")). A Python whose ctypes links libffi statically and hides its symbols, with no libffi installed, raises OSError the first time an implementation of such an interface is passed. The closures are built for the 64-bit platforms wheels target.
  • A typed-array parameter is copied into an array.array unless it already is one of the matching type code; a NumPy array is copied element by element.
  • The package’s namespace also holds the typing names the module imports (Any, Callable, ClassVar, Iterable, Iterator, Mapping, Sequence): a declared type with one of those names still works at run time, but type checkers reject the module.
  • A long-running callback stalls the producer thread that called it, and a callback that waits for the thread blocked in the calling Python code deadlocks.
  • LibraryLoadError is raised during the import, so it can only be caught as ImportError (the package isn’t importable to name the class).

.NET

The .NET target emits a standalone C# class library over the C ABI (revision 6). Native calls go through source-generated [LibraryImport] declarations whose slots are all blittable, with runtime marshalling disabled for the assembly ([assembly: DisableRuntimeMarshalling]), and callbacks go through [UnmanagedCallersOnly] function pointers, so the bindings are trim- and AOT-friendly. The project targets net8.0 and builds with any newer SDK.

.NET is a Tier 1 target: it tracks every ABI revision as it lands and runs the full conformance suite in CI.

What gets generated

For a package named kvstore:

dotnet/
  Kvstore.cs        generated API: types, wrappers, native imports, contract
  Runtime.cs        fixed runtime: exceptions, loader, codec, async and iterator helpers
  Kvstore.csproj    SDK-style project with the NuGet metadata
  README.md

The namespace, assembly name, and NuGet id are the package name in PascalCase (my-kv becomes MyKv). Set name to override all three:

[generators.dotnet]
name = "Acme.Storage"

The whole library is one namespace. Records, enums, interfaces, callback interfaces, and exceptions sit directly in it, and the free functions are static methods of the library class, {Namespace}Library (from the namespace’s last segment), which also holds Check() and AbiVersion:

using Kvstore;

using var store = await KvstoreLibrary.OpenStore("/data");
var stats = KvstoreLibrary.Summarize(store, null);

using static Kvstore.KvstoreLibrary; brings the functions into scope unqualified. A function spelled like a member the class already has (Check, AbiVersion, Equals, ReferenceEquals) or like the class itself gains a trailing _. The project generates an XML documentation file, so the IDL’s docs show up in IntelliSense; backticked identifiers in them (`new_op`) are rewritten to their C# spelling (NewOp).

Build and load

Reference the generated project from yours, or pack it:

dotnet add reference bindings/dotnet/Kvstore.csproj
dotnet pack bindings/dotnet/Kvstore.csproj -c Release

The bindings load the native library by its base name (kvstore), so .NET probes libkvstore.dylib, libkvstore.so, or kvstore.dll next to the app and on the platform’s library path. Set KVSTORE_LIBRARY (the {PREFIX}_LIBRARY variable) to a full path to load that file instead (and only that file). The loader is a DllImportResolver on the generated assembly.

weaveffi package writes the project to dotnet/Kvstore/ with each desktop platform’s library under runtimes/<rid>/native/, where NuGet resolves it at restore time, and runs dotnet pack on it to produce dotnet/Kvstore.{version}.nupkg (so it needs the .NET SDK; without it the package is skipped with a warning). See Packaging.

Load-time checks

Every import resolves through the generated resolver, which loads the library once, checks {prefix}_abi_version() against 6, and then checks the contract: for every declaration the bindings were generated with (each function, interface, member, record, enum, callback interface and method, error domain, and error code), it resolves the declaration’s contract function by name, calls it, and compares the hash it returns with the embedded one:

static partial void VerifyContracts(IntPtr library)
{
    VerifyContract(library, new (string, ulong, string)[]
    {
        ("kvstore_contract_2bef5883c0f5eed1", 0xe3faf8ccf74791cfUL, "Store.put"), // method put(string, bytes, EntryKind, i64?) -> Entry throws KvError
        // ... one row per declaration
    });
}

Declarations the library has and the bindings don’t are fine, so a library that only adds declarations (new functions, error codes, or callback methods) keeps working with older bindings.

A library that can’t be found or loaded, or that doesn’t match, throws NativeLoadException, a catchable exception naming the problem (couldn't find the kvstore native library ..., Store.put is missing from the library kvstore, Store.put changed since these bindings were generated). The failure is remembered: every later call throws the same exception, before any call can misread memory. To check at startup instead of on the first call, call the library class:

try
{
    KvstoreLibrary.Check();
}
catch (NativeLoadException e)
{
    Console.Error.WriteLine($"can't use kvstore: {e.Message}");
}

Type mapping

Every value crosses as one C value in every position: a parameter, a return, an async result, an iterator item, and a callback method’s argument and return.

IDL typeC# typeCrosses the ABI as
i8 … u64, f32, f64sbyte … ulong, float, doubleby value
boolboolone byte, 0 or 1 (never the four-byte Win32 BOOL)
stringstringan FfiRun ({prefix}_run) of UTF-8, encoded into a pooled buffer; interior NULs survive
bytesReadOnlySpan<byte> as a parameter, byte[] otherwisean FfiRun, a parameter pinned in place
C-style enumenum with the IDL discriminantsint32_t
recordpositional sealed recordan FfiRun holding a value buffer
rich enumabstract record with one nested sealed record per variantan FfiRun holding a value buffer
T? of a scalar, bool, or C-style enumT? (int?, Color?)an FfiOpt* struct ({prefix}_opt_{prim}): a presence byte and the value
other T?T?a value buffer (Iface? is a nullable pointer, Cb? a null vtable)
[P] of i8 … u64 (not u8), f32, f64ReadOnlySpan<P> as a parameter, P[] otherwisean FfiRun of packed elements, its length in bytes; a parameter is pinned in place, a result is copied once
other [T]IReadOnlyList<T>a value buffer
{K: V}IReadOnlyDictionary<K, V>a value buffer
interfacesealed class : IDisposableobject pointer
callback interfaceinterface I{Name}an FfiCallback ({prefix}_{Cb}): the ctx and a static vtable, by value
iter<T>IEnumerable<T>iterator handle

The runtime declares these structs once, all blittable and passed by value ([StructLayout(LayoutKind.Sequential)]): FfiRun (byte* Ptr; nuint Len), one FfiOpt* per scalar (byte Has then the value, a byte again for FfiOptBool), and FfiCallback (IntPtr Ctx; IntPtr Vtable). No native signature mentions C#’s bool, so nothing depends on how the runtime would marshal one.

A typed-array or bytes parameter is a span, so an array, a slice of one (values.AsSpan(1, 3)), or stack memory passes without a copy; it must be aligned for its element type (managed arrays always are), or the producer rejects it as a marshalling failure (-3). Inside a value buffer (a record field, a list element) the same types are ordinary lists: IReadOnlyList<double>.

Returned strings, bytes, typed arrays, and buffers are copied and released with {prefix}_run_free. Records and rich enums encode themselves (WriteTo and ReadFrom); optionals, lists, and maps go through the runtime’s generic codec (WriteList, ReadMap, …), handed a static lambda per element, so no code is generated per collection shape.

Records

A record is a positional record, so it has value equality, with expressions, deconstruction, and a readable ToString:

public sealed record Entry(string Key, byte[] Value, EntryKind Kind, uint Version, long? ExpiresAt,
    IReadOnlyList<string> Tags, IReadOnlyDictionary<string, string> Metadata)

A record with a byte array, list, or map field overrides Equals and GetHashCode to compare those fields by content, so two decodes of the same entry are equal, and entry with { Tags = new[] { "x" } } isn’t. Any IReadOnlyList<T> or IReadOnlyDictionary<K, V> works as a field value (an array, a List<T>, an immutable collection). A field spelled like the record itself or like a member every record has (ToString, Equals) gains a trailing _.

A rich enum is a closed hierarchy you can switch on:

switch (change)
{
    case Change.Put put: Console.WriteLine(put.Entry.Key); break;
    case Change.Removed { Expired: true } removed: Console.WriteLine($"{removed.Key} expired"); break;
}

Objects and lifetime

An interface wrapper holds one strong reference in a nested SafeHandle subclass whose ReleaseHandle calls _destroy. Every import takes that handle, not a raw pointer, so the interop stub adds a reference for the duration of the call: a wrapper that’s disposed on another thread or collected mid-call can’t free the object under it. Dispose releases the reference (an in-flight call finishes first); the SafeHandle’s finalizer covers wrappers that are never disposed. Calling a disposed wrapper, or passing one as an argument, throws ObjectDisposedException.

Returned objects, object fields of decoded records, and objects handed to a callback each arrive as a new wrapper that owns its reference. Two wrappers are equal (Equals, GetHashCode) when they reference the same native object. Writing an object into a value buffer clones its reference first, so the producer can keep it.

An interface member spelled like a member the wrapper declares or inherits (Dispose, Handle, Equals, ToString, …) or like the interface itself gains a trailing _: an IDL method dispose is Dispose_() (reserved member names).

Errors

What a failed call throws follows its throws clause (the trap policy):

  • throws: SomeDomain throws the domain’s exception. Each error domain becomes a class deriving from NativeException, named with one Exception suffix (KvError is KvException, KitchenErrors is KitchenException), with one nested sealed class per code whose fields are typed properties; catch the nested class for one code or the domain class for any of them. Domains are open: a positive code the bindings don’t know (one the library added later) throws the domain class itself, with that Code and the producer’s message. Codes are only unique within a domain, so a call’s own domain decides the type.
  • throws: any throws NativeException itself with Code -1 (GenericErrorCode) and the producer’s message.
  • A runtime code of a throwing call (-2 panic, -3 marshalling failure, -4 a failed callback) throws NativeException with that Code; its constants name them (PanicErrorCode, MarshalErrorCode, ForeignErrorCode).
  • A call that can’t fail (no throws) failing anyway is a producer bug: it throws NativeBugException, an InvalidOperationException whose Code is the runtime code and whose message names the code and the producer’s message.
  • Cancellation (-5) always throws OperationCanceledException.

For the kvstore sample’s KvError domain:

public class KvException : NativeException
{
    /// <summary>key not found</summary>
    public sealed class KeyNotFound : KvException
    {
        /// <summary>The domain code, <c>1001</c>.</summary>
        public const int ErrorCode = 1001;

        public string Key { get; }

        public KeyNotFound(string key, string? message = null) : base(ErrorCode, message ?? $"key not found: {key}")
        {
            Key = key;
        }
    }

    // ... Expired, StoreFull, InvalidPath, Rejected, CallbackFailed
}

try
{
    store.Get("nope");
}
catch (KvException.KeyNotFound e)
{
    Console.WriteLine($"{e.Code} {e.Key}: {e.Message}"); // 1001 nope: key not found: nope
}

The message is the producer’s (a Rust producer’s Display output, empty messages arriving as the IDL default); a code class constructed in C# defaults to the IDL message, with the code’s fields filled in where the message names them (key not found: {key}). A code field named message (or code) is Message_ (or Code_), since Exception already has the property. If the API declares a type named like a runtime class (NativeException, NativeBugException, NativeLoadException), the runtime class takes the namespace’s last segment as a prefix (KvstoreNativeException), and a type named like the library class moves it to {Namespace}NativeLibrary.

Async and cancellation

An async function returns Task or Task<T>, and every one takes an optional CancellationToken. The launcher receives an [UnmanagedCallersOnly] completion and a GCHandle to the pending call; the completion runs on a producer thread, receives the result as its one C value, decodes it, releases everything the call owned, and completes the task (continuations run asynchronously).

public Task<uint?> VersionOf(string key, CancellationToken cancellationToken = default)
  • For a cancellable function the token cancels the native call through the native cancel token; when the producer completes with -5 the task becomes canceled, so await throws TaskCanceledException (an OperationCanceledException).
  • For any other function, cancelling stops waiting: the task becomes canceled at once, and the native call, which can’t be stopped, finishes in the background. Its result is released then (a returned object wrapper is disposed right away).
  • An already-cancelled token returns a canceled task without launching.

The consumer’s native cancel token is destroyed after completion, and a registration racing the completion never touches a destroyed token.

Callbacks

A callback interface becomes a C# interface the consumer implements, with the same types as the rest of the API:

public interface IPolicy
{
    /// <exception cref="KvException">Reported to the library with its code and fields.</exception>
    long? TtlFor(string key, long? requested);

    /// <exception cref="KvException">Reported to the library with its code and fields.</exception>
    Entry Admit(Entry entry);

    Store Route(string key, Store home);
}

public interface IScorer
{
    double[] Scores(ReadOnlySpan<ulong> sizes);
}

Passing an implementation pins it in a GCHandle (the ctx) and hands the producer an FfiCallback of that ctx and one process-wide vtable of [UnmanagedCallersOnly] trampolines, by value. The vtable is allocated once in native memory and never freed, so it outlives every use; it starts with the {size, flags, free} header (size is the vtable’s real size as C# lays it out, flags is 0: methods may run on any thread), then one function pointer per method in declaration order. The free entry releases the handle when the producer drops its last reference, possibly on another thread; the producer takes over the registration as soon as the call reaches it, even when the call then fails. An optional callback parameter (ILoader? loader) passes a null vtable for null.

Trampolines run on whatever thread the producer calls from. Each argument is one C value: string and buffer arguments are decoded, typed-array and bytes arguments are ReadOnlySpan<T>s over the producer’s memory, valid only during the call (copy with ToArray() to keep them), and object arguments are adopted, each a new wrapper the implementation owns. The return is one C value too:

  • a scalar, bool (one byte), or C-style enum is returned as is, and an optional one as its FfiOpt* struct;
  • an object is a fresh reference the producer adopts (CloneHandle()); a null for a required object reaches the producer as null, which it rejects as a marshalling failure;
  • a string, bytes, typed array, record, rich enum, optional, list, or map is written into a run allocated with {prefix}_alloc and returned as an FfiRun, which the producer adopts and frees.

An exception thrown by an implementation never unwinds into native code. A method declared throws: SomeDomain that throws that domain’s exception reports the code, the message, and the code’s fields (encoded as the error payload with {prefix}_error_set_payload), so the producer sees exactly that domain error:

public Entry Admit(Entry entry)
{
    if (entry.Key.StartsWith("secret"))
    {
        throw new KvException.Rejected(entry.Key, "no secrets");
    }
    return entry with { Kind = EntryKind.Encrypted };
}

Any other exception is reported with code -1 and its message (-4 from a method that can’t fail). What the failure means for the call in progress is the producer’s decision; the kvstore sample turns it into KvException.CallbackFailed carrying the message.

Iterators

An iter<T> function returns an IEnumerable<T> that streams lazily, one native call per item, each item arriving as its one C value. It’s re-enumerable: each enumeration (each GetEnumerator, so each foreach or LINQ pass) launches a new native iterator, so a failure to start throws from GetEnumerator, at the start of the loop, rather than from the call. The enumerator owns its native iterator as a SafeHandle, destroyed when enumeration ends, when the enumerator is disposed (foreach does this on early exit), or by its finalizer. Span parameters of an iterator function are copied once, since the sequence outlives the call.

Known limitations

  • Only one DllImportResolver can exist per assembly. If you compile the generated sources into an assembly that already has one, the {PREFIX}_LIBRARY override and the implicit load-time check are skipped; call {Namespace}Library.Check() yourself.
  • Object references cloned into a value buffer leak if encoding a later field throws before the call (for example a disposed wrapper in the same list).
  • C# can name an undeclared enum value ((Color)3); the producer rejects it as a marshalling failure (-3), also inside an optional (Color?).
  • A record’s hash code of a list or map field uses its count, so records that differ only in collection contents share a hash (equality still tells them apart).

Dart

The Dart target generates a standalone Dart package that calls the producer’s C ABI (revision 6) through dart:ffi. It works in Dart command-line apps and in Flutter apps on the desktop, Android, and iOS. Records become value classes, rich enums sealed class hierarchies, interfaces disposable wrapper classes, async functions Futures, iterators lazy Iterables, and callback interfaces abstract classes you implement.

Dart is a Tier 2 target: it may lag behind new ABI work, but it’s held to the same conformance suite as every other target.

What gets generated

dart/
  pubspec.yaml               package `{package}`, depends on `ffi`
  README.md
  lib/{package}.dart         the library: its imports and parts
  lib/src/runtime/*.dart     the runtime (loader, errors, codecs, ...) and contract rows
  lib/src/api.dart           every declaration of the library

The whole binding is one Dart library split into parts, so you import a single file. A library is one namespace, and so is its Dart package: every type and function is a top-level name. The package name defaults to the library’s C prefix (kvstore for the kvstore sample), so consumers write import 'package:kvstore/kvstore.dart';. Runtime sections the API doesn’t use (async, callbacks, typed arrays, optional scalars, …) aren’t emitted.

Configuration

[generators.dart]
name = "kvstore"          # package name and library file (default: the C prefix)
